My Go-To Marinade for Chicken Is 3 Ingredients

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

It’s That Simple is our series about recipes so easy, you can make them with your eyes closed. Think tiny ingredient lists, laid-back techniques, and results so delicious you’ll text home about them.

By all logic I should be sick of this chicken. I have cooked it, give or take, a hundred times. When I don’t know what to make for dinner—or, honestly, don’t want to make dinner at all—it is there for me, like a gentle friend.

The marinade is deceptively simple: equal parts miso and honey, plus a glug of soy sauce. Nothing more. There’s no gotcha moment, no pepper hiding in plain sight or olive oil I forgot to account for. The flavor excels because each ingredient punches above its weight. Both fermented, the miso and soy sauce add umami depth, while honey supplies sweetness and makes the chicken glossy, like ceramics pulled straight from the kiln.

I’m often deflated when I see the word marinade in a recipe. No, I don’t have time for “at least an hour” or, more likely, the dreaded “preferably overnight.” Don’t they know it’s a Tuesday? This marinade, mercifully, is immediate. Because it is more of a paste than a liquid, you can slather it directly onto the chicken before hurling the baking sheet into the oven. (Of course, if you’re the type of person who has the wherewithal to start meal prep the night before, that works too.)

A short stint under the fierce heat of the broiler transforms the miso coating into a burnished, sticky crust in 15-ish minutes while you work on the rest of the meal. Old-school drawer-style broilers can get a little overexcited, so start peeking in after 10 minutes. Now is not the time to check the mail or start a load of laundry.

This chicken shines with rice tucked underneath, which catches every saucy streak. But you could build it out with roasted vegetables. Sometimes I’ll add oiled-up broccoli or asparagus to the baking sheet. Other times it’s just the chicken, with a shower of scallions if I’m feeling fancy. Then I make it all again next week.

How to Remind Your Boss of Your PTO Without Pissing Them Off

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

You're about to slam that laptop shut for two weeks when the boss messages, “See you Monday!”

We've all been there, staring down a manager who has forgotten about our paid time off—even though we told them!

When we communicate something that's important to us, we can forget that it's not necessarily as meaningful to the recipient. Surely you've been on the flip side, too, annoyed at a coworker who hasn't replied to your messages, until someone mentions that they're on holiday.

A key to efficient communication in the workplace is to repeat yourself. It sounds counterintuitive, but it's true. We're all better off when we overcommunicate by saying the same thing two or three times.

The trick is how and when you do it. Think of all the avenues you have for requesting, recording, and reminding people of your PTO. Use all the channels, and spread out the messaging so that each instance has relevant context and timing.

If you like to plan in advance, you might request time off four to six months out. Not all managers appreciate this much notice, however, because they might not know what the workload will be when your vacation rolls around, making it hard for them to approve your dates.

How far in advance you should request time off is a conversation to have with your direct boss. It depends on them as a person, the type of work you do, the size of your team, and other factors. If this conversation doesn't give you much clarity, four to six weeks before your time off is probably about right. Start there and adjust as necessary.

Occasionally, you need approval well in advance for an event that cannot move and will cost you a lot of money to cancel, like your own wedding or a nonrefundable trip. In those cases, six months or more is not unreasonable, but bring it up from time to time so no one forgets.

Whenever you put in your initial request, it must be recorded. Log it into a time-off system if your organization uses one (human resources software, a shared calendar) or write it in an email with a clear subject line, and make sure your boss replies saying it's approved.

About two weeks before your time off, remind your boss and colleagues verbally in meetings. The ideal settings are in a weekly one-on-one meeting with your direct manager and in a team meeting.

Save your info for the end of the meeting after all pressing business has been discussed. If you need to make sure your responsibilities are covered while you're out, raise that point privately with your manager rather than in a team meeting so the boss has time to decide how to handle it.

If you need to tell someone beyond your team—such as clients or another department—about your planned time off, do so in writing a week before you leave. This gives them plenty of opportunity to reach out while you’re still available.

Email is fine, though if you usually communicate with these people through Slack or Microsoft Teams, that may be a better option. How you format the message matters—see the next step for tips.

Two business days before your time off begins, send a short, clear message to everyone who needs to know that you will be out. If the norm is to use Slack or Microsoft Teams, use that.

How you format this message makes a huge difference. The first sentence should say something like, "Just a reminder, I will be out beginning DATE. I will be back on DATE." This sentence can be in bold. The second DATE is the day you return to work, not the last day of your PTO. What's relevant to your colleagues is the day you are available.

If you need to give more information, such as who is covering for you, put that in a new paragraph. Do not go overboard with detail. It will make people crazy. Should you need to leave detailed instructions, put them in a shared document and link to it.

Why send this message two business days out? Simple: If your first PTO day is Monday, you send this message on Thursday so that anybody who takes off or calls in sick on Friday still sees it in time.

My favorite PTO reminder is the status indicator in a team messaging platform. Set it to show you're out and add an appropriate emoji, like a palm tree or airplane.

Even if you dislike emoji, they are extremely efficient and effective communicators here. At a glance, everyone who sees it is reminded that you're out.

You can typically set a status message too; use this to tell everyone the date you’ll be available again.

For those supremely forgetful bosses and coworkers, you might set up an automated message that appears at 8:30 am the first day you're out. Slack, Teams, email, or even a text message will do the trick. Be concise. Be kind. And have a sense of humor. The point of the message is to be helpful—not make someone else feel like a forgetful jerk.

This Trending Home Feature Is More Practical Than Pretty

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

100+ Most Unique and Practical Gifts 🎁 for Homeowners and DIYers

Published Nov 8, 2026 11:00 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

Each year, Zonda publishes an annual Cost vs. Value (CVV) report, ranking the most common home renovation projects across the nation by return on investment (ROI) value. For the first time, backup power generators entered the top 10 ranking on the list at number 7, with ROI exceeding 100 percent in hurricane-prone and storm-affected regions.

We wanted to understand why home backup power is more popular than ever, since it’s long been considered a luxury upgrade as opposed to an essential part of modern home preparedness. Here’s what several home generator experts and real estate pros think are the reasons why homeowners and homebuyers are now prioritizing backup power and whole-home generators when it comes to home value.

Before we dive into the reasons more people are opting to have a standby generator or whole-home backup generator, it’s important to understand exactly what it does and how it functions. Permanently installed outside a home, a standby generator produces enough electricity to power an entire house indefinitely in the event of a power outage. It connects directly to the home’s electrical panel and uses the natural gas line for fuel. One of the major benefits is that a standby generator automatically turns on within seconds of the power going out, seamlessly transferring the power source from the grid to the generator.

A portable generator is a gas-powered machine that you can carry or wheel over short distances. It has outlets so you can power several appliances. However, it only functions as a temporary power source when the electricity shuts off, making it less reliable than a standby generator. It’s also noisier, requires setup each time you use it, and needs to be refueled periodically.

This whole-home standby generator features a dual-fuel design and a transfer switch that automatically kicks on when the power goes out.

A rise in storm frequency and severity plays a role in more people wanting a backup generator for their home. “Outages stopped feeling rare,” says Beau Tyrrel, product training manager at Jackery. “The average U.S. customer lost about 11 hours of power in 2024, nearly double the decade before. When something happens twice as often, people start planning for it.”

Bert Woidan, operations manager at Trademark Electric, a Generac PowerPro Prestige dealer in Ocala, Florida, recognizes how generator sales are a storm-driven market. “I believe with the increase in weather-related power outages, it only makes sense that more homeowners would want a backup generator with their home,” he explains. “When our company first started selling generators over 20 years ago, I remember standing in the parking lot talking with our founder, and we set a goal to install at least one generator a week. Today, we order generators by the semi load, and during a busy storm season, we’re installing two to three generators a day.”

More storms mean more stress over potential damage. “Especially in our region, if you lose power, the biggest concern is your AC going out,” notes Woidan. “With how humid it gets, it’s not just uncomfortable, but the humidity and moisture can damage your home very quickly, so it’s super important to keep your AC running even if you lose power.”

In the Midwest, the big fear is the basement, says Omar Muhammad, owner of Excel Electrical Technologies in Orland Park, Illinois. “Most homes in our area have a sump pump, and the storms that knock out power are the same storms that send water toward your foundation.” A finished basement can take on tens of thousands of dollars of damage in one night if the pump stops, so buyers and residents understand that a generator protects the most expensive square footage in their house.

Technological innovation has been a game changer for how Americans live. Homes depend on electricity in ways they didn’t 20 years ago, says Muhammad. “A lot of people work from home now and some run medical equipment. Many have an EV in the garage; a high-efficiency furnace that won’t run without power; and a smart thermostat, security system, and Wi-Fi network they rely on every day,” he explains. “An outage is a lot different than it used to be—now it can mean you can’t work, can’t heat the house, and can’t charge your car.” Therefore, a standby generator has become a non-negotiable addition for many homeowners who can’t afford to have their power shut off for any period of time.

Standby generators are now recognized as being as necessary as smoke detectors and security systems, adding value to a home. According to Briggs & Stratton, a permanently installed generator could get you a 3 to 5 percent boost to your home value. And they are now more widespread and becoming the norm, so buyers expect to see them. “Most new builds automatically include a backup generator installation in the blueprints,” notes Woidan.

From a resale perspective, a home generator can help a property stand out by offering buyers peace of mind, especially in neighborhoods that have experienced repeated power disruptions, says Dell Jeanty, founder of Dell Residential Group in Arlington, Virginia. “A home with a generator during an outage demonstrates preparedness, which can be a meaningful selling point,” says Jeanty. “Some estimates suggest standby generators can recover as much as 95 percent of their installation cost at resale, although the actual return depends on the market, installation costs, and buyer demand.”

Curb appeal does not have to be solely cosmetic. See which good-looking projects we are prioritizing to make homes safer, more energy-efficient, and weather resistant.

Fala Atelier embeds trio of playful volumes into Portuguese hillside for Cookie Cutter Retreat

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Photography by Francisco Ascensão (unless stated otherwise)

Three semicircular volumes of different scales make up this retreat for digital nomads in Portugal, completed by Porto architecture studio Fala Atelier.

The three volumes are nearly identical, emerging from the site with blocky, blue chimneys and topped with grass roofs, which Fala Atelier said “almost disappear” into the landscape.

“The three buildings enjoy considerable freedom in their relationships with the site, the slope and one another, sharing a geometric vocabulary without submitting to a rigid overall composition,” the studio told Dezeen.

“From a distance, they almost disappear, leaving the windows and bright blue chimneys to announce their presence,” it added.

“Their placement makes the landscape a fluid game.”

The retreat’s three buildings were scaled as small, medium and large versions of the same semicircular volume. and built with the same materials and dimension ratios.

Each one is composed of a concrete frame, which is embedded into the landscape and incorporates round columns. These are adorned with glazed fronts and tilted metal canopies.

Diagonal walls slice through each of the volumes to distinguish the public and private areas, behind which are spaces that draw on houses by architects Frank Gehry, Toyo Ito and Alvaro Siza, according to Fala Atelier.

“Each [volume] is divided by a diagonal wall, placing a dense collection of small rooms beside an expansive, almost empty space,” the studio said.

“We treated fragments of [Gehry, Ito and Siza’s] plans as objets trouvés, borrowing their internal formal relationships almost verbatim.”

The hall, which is the largest of the three spaces, was designed by Fala Atelier to “host almost any activity”.

Its front portion is open plan, while private spaces sit behind, finished with a combination of white-painted and tile-clad walls contrasted by blue and fluorescent-yellow details.

This same materiality and colour palette is repeated throughout the retreat’s private and shared dwellings to create a consistent visual language across the site.

“The consistency allows the differences to become more intriguing,” the studio said. “You recognise the family immediately, even if you briefly wonder which relative you have entered,” it added.

Elsewhere in Portugal, Corkinho used solid cork to construct a pavilion dedicated to silence in Alentejo and António Costa Lima Arquitectos has completed a warehouse-style housing block in Lisbon.

Photography by Francisco Ascensão (unless stated otherwise)

Fuentesaúco Pharmacy / David Hernandez Arquitectura

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Read the original version in Spanish here.

Text description provided by the architects. The Fuentesaúco Pharmacy offers a reflection on the role of architecture in the recovery and modernization of essential services in rural areas. In a town of approximately 1,500 inhabitants, the project goes beyond renovating a commercial establishment to reaffirm the importance of maintaining nearby, accessible healthcare facilities that are connected to the everyday life of the community. In a context marked by population decline, architecture also becomes a tool for preserving quality of life and strengthening the community's identity.

The Sunday Papers

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The Sunday Papers is our weekly roundup of great writing about (mostly) videogames from across the web.

Sundays are for waking up covered in sand. The roof of your house is missing. A small man stands in the emptied bed of a dump truck. "You know, everybody's always asking about Elder Scrolls 6," he says. "You know, everybody's always asking about Elder Scrolls 6," he repeats. "You know, everybody's always asking about Elder Scrolls 6," he declares once again, beginning to move towards you this time. "You know, everybody's always asking about Elder Scrolls 6." You realise your hands are tied down. Oh no.

Is he about to sit on your face? WAIT, THAT'S NOW ILLEGAL HERE IN THE UK. NO, PLEASE, DON'T SIT ON MY-time seems to slow as the bare backside approaches your visage. As its cheeks prepare to become as sandy as yours, you hear its wielder say: "And we thought, hey, given the moment..."

Panicking, you scrabble for anything to say which might free you before something that might get a British MP's hackles up can occur. "Things first went awry when I turned up for my appointment to find a tall German man wearing a cowboy hat asking a PR if he could play the game," you blurt out. The posterior momentarily stops moving towards you. Buoyed, you continue to tell a story you heard from VGC's Chris Scullion.

I was asked if I minded sharing my one-to-one appointment with this chap too, to which my inherent British cowardly courtesy kicked in and I lied that it was perfectly fine.

"I want drums", he said as he entered the room. "We are playing Dani California by Red Hot Chili Peppers." Rather than ask him whether he also fancied telling me where I was going for dinner later, I picked up my guitar and went along with it, eager to just play the game.

The bottom begins its agonising crawl towards your kisser once more. You can feel the occupants of parliamentary benches begin to sense a disturbance in the force. Maybe if you can make the bumholder sad, that'll do the trick. You try to remember how Sebastian Cardoso of Game Studio Unlocked's presentation on the tragic fates of game studios after acquisitions went.

A hundred and twenty-three of the 362 developers were shut outright. The average life from acquisition to closure is 6.4 years and the median is 5, meaning half of all closed studios were dead within five years of their acquisition.

The familiar names mostly died fast. EA closed Pandemic two years after buying it. Sony shut Firewalk barely a year and a half in. Warner Bros. closed Player First roughly seven months after acquiring it. Bluepoint, closed at the five-year mark, is exactly what the median looks like.

It's no use. That arse's shadow its creeping across ever more of your face like your're a planet with a nose and it's the Vogon Constructor Fleet. Maybe you can somehow contort your head to the side. Arrrggghh. You call on all the strength of muscles you barely ever use and think of the incredible gymnastic feats executed by Physical Contortion Gamer's Josh Wolens during an ongoing GTA diary series.

Attempting to do flips, then, just sort of rotates your entire body below the diaphragm while your head and chest area remain bolt upright, rigid in such a way that you could feasibly fly a flag from your own thorax.

Which looks odd. Nevertheless, it is pretty fun to scoot and roll your way through Los Santos. At one point, I attempted a double frontflip in the middle of a part of the map that had been deleted for some reason. It didn't quite work out, and I ended up colliding with and possibly killing a man in a fursuit.

It doesn't work. You can't get away. You begin to scream. The posterioperator stops repeating the words "You know, everybody's always asking about Elder Scrolls 6". Instead, he asks where that mysterious German cowboy you mentioned earlier has gone. You don't have a clue, and can't help admitting as much. "Ah well." The derriere resumes its descent, now mere inches from your mug. This is the end. You have one last rummage through your mind for possible solutions.

Respec's Tom Orry hasn't managed to save you. "Fine, sit on my face and send me to hell," you shout into the pinkish ring of doom as British cops prepare to ram-raid your abode and arrest you for crimes against a prude's definition of acceptable shagging.

The bum stops. Its owner asks if you've just said the word Sentinel. "Yes," you lie.

The arse is raised and swapped for the face of a man. "What a perfect time for you to realise what The Elder Scrolls 6 is actually called," he says.

You notice he's not Todd Howard. Or Adrian Edmondson.

"It's Ade's month off and they needed another baldy to play this esteemed role," Sir Patrick Stewart announces, clearly responding the look of befuddlement your face can still have since it hasn't been crushed by a ten-tonne tush. You wonder how you didn't recognise his voice before, especially given he played the emperor in Oblivion.

"Here's to the next Bethesda game after Starfield," the Stew sings as the police bust through the door and he's beamed up before they can tackle him.

I Tried the Nike Air Zoom Hyperslide Every Day for a Month and Now I’m Spoiled

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

The Hyperice pod hidden in the straps elevates the decadent coddling the Nike Air Zoom Hyperslide delivers. (Photo: 101 Degrees West)

For more than a decade, every morning while I’m hand-grinding coffee beans for my moka pot, I do 15 to 20 squats. It’s a comforting morning routine, but it is mostly a discipline, a way to ensure I don’t let busy days keep me from working on my glutes to counteract hours of sitting.

A month ago, however, I added decadence to my discipline, making me look forward to the coffee-grinding routine (almost) as much as I do the coffee. That decadence comes in the form of a comfy pair of slides and a hot foot massage, bundled together as the Nike x Hyperice Air Zoom Hyperslide.

Is this $250 recovery slide an expensive gimmick? Well . . . yes. But an oh-so-comfortable one that I’m now reluctant to give up.

The slide itself is a slab of supportive foam topped by responsive, low-profile Air Zoom units (the same pressurized-air and trampoline-like tensile-fiber pods used in Nike’s high-end running shoes, here tuned more for comfort than performance). The Air Zoom cushioning feels both plush and lightly bouncy underfoot, and the wide platform provides reassuring stability, making my feet feel coddled and supported while doing my squats, walking around my office, or sitting at my desk.

But that coddling is just the beginning. Hidden beneath the Velcro top straps are a pair of Hyperice pods that magnetically attach to the main instep straps and transmit heat and vibration to the tops of my feet. Buttons on each side of the pods cycle through three levels of vibration (50, 75, and 100 Hz) and three levels of heat (109°F, 113°F, and 117°F). You can also use the Bluetooth-connected phone app, but as far as I can tell, its main benefit is only letting you set the run time longer than the default fifteen minutes.

The first time I used the Hyperslides, I dutifully wore socks and selected the lowest settings, as recommended. Quickly, however, I was cranking them up to high on both heat and vibration. I have, however, kept wearing the socks, because they seem to help wrap my foot and transmit the sensations farther around it rather than having them isolated in one spot on top.

It didn’t take long before I craved the buzzing warmth radiating through my feet each morning, although the sensation felt simply pleasurable, not therapeutic. That was before the day I woke up with a sharp pain between two metatarsal heads that I traced back to a twisting foot plant on a trail run the day before. When I slid my foot into the slide and started the massage, it initially felt a bit intense on the sore spot, but as the heat and vibrations loosened the stiffness, the pain all but disappeared and stayed away all day.

The Hyperslides do what they do better than I ever imagined (having never imagined a heated, vibrating slide), but after using them for a while, I have a couple of nitpicky wishes.

The batteries are supposed to last for an hour on full power, which was pretty close to what I get. Because I almost always restart them after the first fifteen minutes, I run out of juice after two days. They usually sputter out of power before the full 30 minutes on the second day, which I prefer to having them complete the cycle because the dying pods prompt me to plug them in. There’s no battery-level indicator, so if they turn off without dying, I don’t know if I need to charge them unless I remember how many days it’s been since they were charged and how many cycles I’ve run since (I don’t have the mindspace for that). The worst is when I turn them on thinking they are charged, and the battery runs out in a few short minutes—then I have to wait three hours for them to charge. I wish they had some way to let me know how much juice was left.

My only other wish is that I’d like the heat and vibration to reach down to my arch better. But that would require a bigger, more encompassing device, like a boot. Oh! Nike and Hyperice made one of those too. I may have to try them someday, but for now I’ll luxuriate in the easy, slip-on, push-button pleasure that the Hyperslides add to my mornings.

A 55-Year-Old Math Problem Was Solved—This Time by Humans

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The original version of this story appeared in Quanta Magazine.

The late Ronald Graham wore two hats. He was a renowned mathematician and, at one time, president of the American Mathematical Society. He was also a serious juggler and president of the International Jugglers’ Association. “He loved tricks,” said Fan Chung, a mathematician at UC San Diego who was married to Graham. “You know, spinning a ball, spinning a coat hanger, spinning several balls together, throwing pens against the wall.”

Sometimes Graham wore both hats at once. “It’s interesting, in fact, that many mathematicians and computer scientists have an interest in juggling,” he said in a 1980 television interview. “I think it’s the search for patterns and structure that is responsible for this.”

He would go on to write numerous papers, some with Chung, on the mathematics of juggling. But back in 1971, decades before he made that connection explicit, he posed a question that some mathematicians now say might have been inspired by juggling, too.

Start with a random set of different integers, not including zero. Can you always rearrange them so that if you add up the first two numbers, then the first three, then the first four, and so on, every “partial sum” turns out different? In the language of juggling, this would mean that if each ball stays in the air for a different amount of time, you can always find an order to throw them in such that two balls won’t come crashing down on the same beat—which would ruin the act.

If the numbers are all positive, then the answer to Graham’s question is obviously yes: The sums will always grow larger as you add more numbers. Similarly, if there are both positive and negative numbers in the mix, the answer is also known to be yes. But what if the numbers live in a finite world—like numbers wrapped around a clock, which repeat after a certain count?

That’s what Graham wanted to know. He conjectured that the answer should still be yes. It often happens, he figured, that even when dealing with rigid constraints, you can still find enough flexibility to construct special patterns or structures—just as it’s usually possible to find a valid sudoku board or Latin square (another kind of puzzle) despite their many rules. “It fits nicely in all these questions about designs and about very symmetric structures,” said Noga Alon, a mathematician at Princeton University. But for decades, no one could prove Graham’s intuition to be true.

That changed recently, when several young mathematicians picked up the balls. In a proof that spanned four papers and various fields of mathematics, they finally resolved Graham’s rearrangement conjecture. The final paper, by Lisa Sauermann of the University of Bonn and Huy Tuan Pham of the University of Chicago, appeared in February 2026, officially closing the problem.

Across the papers, one theme prevailed: the power of randomness to draw out patterns. As Alon put it, “It’s the power of collaboration, the power of the young generation, the power of probabilistic methods” that solved the problem.

Alp Müyesser, a mathematician at the University of Oxford, often finds himself drawn to problems whose solutions need two ingredients: a random process and something extra as well. After solving one such problem in 2022 while he was still a graduate student, he encountered Graham’s conjecture and realized that his just-finished proof could help there, too.

The conjecture is set in the world of clock arithmetic. You start by placing the whole numbers on a number line, then you wrap the line around the face of a clock so that the numbers repeat after some prime number, p. Say p is 7, for instance. In this setting, 0, 7, 14, and all other multiples of 7 are equivalent—meaning that you can add two positive numbers (like 3 and 4) and get zero.

Graham asked the following: If you pick any set of nonzero numbers off this number line (for any p), can you always rearrange them so that the partial sums you get are all different?

The challenge depends on how big your set is compared to p. The more numbers you pick, the more sums there are to manage. But if you choose fewer numbers, there will be fewer ways to rearrange them. These different cases inspire different approaches.

Müyesser, along with his former adviser, Alexey Pokrovskiy of University College London, tackled the case where your set includes almost every possible number up to p. With sets this large, it can be extremely hard to construct a valid ordering. But it turned out that starting with a random ordering can bring you most of the way there.

“Computer scientists often call this a ‘finding the hay in the haystack’ problem,” Müyesser said. You might know that lots of good orderings are out there, but actually finding one is hard. “If you do it randomly, it’s likely going to work, but it’s hard to explicitly describe what the solution is supposed to look like.”

Müyesser and Pokrovskiy needed to ensure that no sequence of numbers anywhere in the ordering added up to zero. Otherwise, adding those numbers to the previous partial sum would repeat that sum.

A completely random ordering might have a few of these troublesome sequences. So Müyesser and Pokrovskiy first set aside a few specially chosen numbers from the set, then randomly scrambled the rest. They scanned their random ordering for any problems; if they came across an interval that added up to zero, they could insert one of the spare numbers to change it. In 2022, they posted their solution, though it was hidden in a paper that focused on applying the same technique to a more general problem.

A couple of years later, Noah Kravitz of Oxford, unaware of Müyesser and Pokrovskiy’s solution, stumbled on Graham’s conjecture in an online archive of unsolved problems. “I saw there was an open problem, and I was like, it’s embarrassing for humanity that we don’t know this,” Kravitz said. “This situation just had to be rectified.”

He decided to approach the conjecture from the opposite end. Together with Benjamin Bedert of Oxford, he considered the case where the set of numbers is tiny compared to p—for instance, Alon said, if you have a set of 100 numbers where p is 1 billion.

Kravitz and Bedert solved Graham’s conjecture for those cases and posted their proof in September 2024. Müyesser saw it and reached out, sharing his own work; the three of them (plus two other colleagues) then teamed up to extend Müyesser’s original approach.

“It was a pretty unlikely combination of people,” Kravitz said. He and Müyesser come from two areas of combinatorics that don’t typically collaborate. “Different sections have completely different techniques,” he said.

Their paper, which they posted in August 2025, handled more cases where the set of numbers is relatively large compared to p. But between those cases and the small-set cases that Kravitz and Bedert had covered, a gap remained. No one could figure out what to do about medium-size sets, such as those that include roughly half as many numbers as p. “Our methods didn’t work there, and there were clear reasons that they would not have worked,” Müyesser said.

It seemed as though research on the problem might enter another long hiatus. Then, in February 2026, a surprise appeared online.

Lisa Sauermann and Huy Tuan Pham were old friends. The two mathematicians had met in 2015 at Stanford University, where Sauermann was a graduate student and Pham an undergraduate. Today they live on different continents—Sauermann in Bonn, Germany, and Pham in Chicago. But a conference in Germany in September 2025 provided a rare chance for them to share a chalkboard again, and afterward Pham followed Sauermann to Bonn for a short visit. All they needed was a problem to work on.

Lisa Sauermann and Huy Tuan Pham have been friends for the past decade. Their recent collaboration on the Graham conjecture finally settled the problem.

At the conference, they heard two talks on Graham’s conjecture by mathematicians who had attempted but failed to bridge the gap. They were intrigued. And as it later turned out, Sauermann had encountered a closely related problem in the International Mathematical Olympiad as a high school student. She solved it correctly, and by the time she finished high school, she’d won a gold medal in the prestigious competition four times. (Most likely, it was Chung who placed the problem on that year’s exam, as she was on the committee that wrote the questions, and she frequently took inspiration from Graham’s many puzzles.)

By the end of their three-day visit, Sauermann and Pham had a plan for how to crack the case.

It hinged on a technically demanding method called anti-concentration. Here, an anti-concentration statement asserts that some event has a particularly low chance of happening. But the mechanics of proving these kinds of statements are so intricate that, though Kravitz and others were aware that such an anti-concentration approach might succeed, “we just hadn’t had the guts to actually try it,” he said.

First, though, Sauermann and Pham began the way their predecessors had. They randomly reordered their set of numbers and came up with a procedure to fix any problems—that is, any sequences that add up to zero. Any time they found a zero-sum sequence, they swapped out the last number in the sequence with another one.

This procedure often went without a hitch. But three types of “bad events” would cause it to fail. One: A zero-sum sequence might occur toward the end of the entire arrangement; then there would be no other numbers to swap in. Two: Many zero-sum sequences might appear too close together, making it impossible to fix them all. And three: Fixing one bad sequence might create another zero-sum sequence down the line.

Sauermann and Pham hoped to prove, using anti-concentration, that each of these bad events was sufficiently unlikely. Then there would have to be a way to rearrange the set of numbers to satisfy the conjecture.

To do this, the duo used Fourier analysis—an area of math that lets you rewrite functions as sums of simple waves—to show that in general, when you add up random sets of numbers, no one sum is especially likely to appear. They then used this insight to carefully estimate the probability that each bad event would occur, ultimately showing that the total chance of getting a bad event was less than 100 percent. That was enough to settle the conjecture.

A few months after their stint in Germany, Sauermann and Pham posted their 27-page proof online. They had shown not only that a satisfactory rearrangement was always possible, but that a random ordering could be rearranged to eliminate bad events at least 90% of the time—a massive success rate.

The mathematicians who had previously worked on the problem were surprised to see the remaining case closed so quickly. “Their approach is just completely different,” Müyesser said.

Together, the four papers prove Graham’s conjecture for sets of all sizes. But they all assume that p is very large; though no one has calculated its exact value, think along the lines of 10 raised to the 100th power. To mathematicians, that’s fine—the salient point is that you’re working in the setting of clock arithmetic. But another aspect of the problem technically remains unsolved—you might still try to resolve the conjecture for all p. And if you want to use the result to choreograph a real juggling routine, you’re out of luck: To correspond to such a large p, the routine would have to be much too long.

The proof confirms that even within these strange, limited number settings, “there are some nice structures that always exist,” Alon said. You can always achieve some degree of flexibility, shuffling the numbers in your set around to avoid revisiting the same partial sums.

“To pose a good problem is really an art,” Chung said. “I think Ron would be extremely happy to see the problem solved.”

Director confirms ‘the next step’ with talks over deal for West Ham man eyed

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

West Ham loaned out flop striker Niclas Fullkrug in the summer, and his remarkable form since rejoining Werder Bremen has been well documented. The 33-year-old has 12 goals in 11 games this season, earning a recall to Jurgen Klopp’s Germany squad and prompting swift talk over a permanent WHUFC exit.

Now, Werder Bremen sporting director Clemens Fritz has spoken publicly about the matter, telling Sky Sports Germany (as relayed by the Hamburger Morgenpost) that the plan is to hold formal talks with the Hammers over a permanent move for Fullkrug.

“The next step is to enter into dialogue with West Ham,” Fritz said. “We will, of course, hold talks about this. Ultimately, we also have to see what the financial package looks like.”

West Ham signed Fullkrug for a hefty fee worth up to £27m in 2024. He had been a prolific scorer in Germany, but there were eyebrows raised over the amount paid given that he was aged 31 at the time.

Those concerns were justified, as Fullkrug failed to make a positive impact in English football. Across all competitions, he managed three goals and two assists in 29 games for the Hammers, so his summer exit to Werder Bremen was a much-needed one.

The German has been a prolific scorer in his home country across his career, but few expected him to find the form of his life on the return to Werder Bremen. Fullkrug has 12 goals in 11 games for the club, with Saturday’s 1-0 loss to VfB Stuttgart marking the first Bundesliga game he has failed to score in since August 30th.

His call-up to Klopp’s Germany squad shows just how well he has been doing. Few WHUFC fans would have expected such success given the standard of his performances over two years in East London.

When players out on loan hit impressive form, it is often met with clamour for their return. However, that is highly unlikely to be the case for Fullkrug.

His circumstances are different. Not only did West Ham pay out a big transfer fee and sizeable wages, but he turns 34 later this season, so will likely have eyes on seeing out the twilight years of his career enjoying his football back in his home country.

Fullkrug’s stint at WHUFC is one best forgotten, and neither he nor the club should have a problem with bringing their affiliation to a permanent end. He can focus on maintaining his form and happily seeing out his career in Germany while the Hammers get his wages off the books for good.

Gasperini appreciates his attitude and would like to continue working with him beyond the natural expiration of his contract. Discussions regarding a renewal have therefore begun, with the aim of reaching an agreement to continue working together.

Polar bears are gathering close to this Canadian town—and co-existing with humans

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 8, 2026 10:17 AM EST

Polar bears (Ursus maritimus) are typically solitary creatures. But in a Canadian town on Hudson Bay, the predators gather along the coast every fall to wait for the ice to freeze.

“It’s an incredible phenomenon to experience,” Alysa McCall, director of science at Polar Bears International (PBI), tells Popular Science. “The bears return to this stretch of coast because it’s where the Bay freezes first, thanks to freshwater from the Churchill River plus the Bay’s currents and winds.”

Sea ice platforms help polar bears catch seals, which are their main source of prey. It’s usually harder for them to do so without the ice, as exemplified in a video PBI captured of a bear on the shore unsuccessfully trying to hunt a seal in the water.

This makes polar bears particularly vulnerable to climate change, which is bringing about longer ice-free seasons. In fact, polar bears in the Western Hudson Bay subpopulation today are stuck on land for around one month longer than their grandparents. What’s more, their numbers have approximately halved since 1979 because of sea ice loss.

Nicknamed the Polar Bear Capital of the World, the residents of Churchill, Manitoba have front-row seats to the annual polar bear gathering (you can also watch through their Polar Bear Live Cams). They are thus also exposed to conflict with these very large temporary neighbors, and climate change is extending this potentially dangerous overlap.

“When polar bears are on land, they are generally fasting. They sometimes venture into towns and communities to look for food, and human areas are full of things that attract curious bears – from trash bins to food smells,” McCall explains. “As climate change causes polar bears to spend more time on land, they have more opportunities to encounter people.”

As such, McCall and her colleagues at PBI have developed new tools to facilitate human-polar bear coexistence. One of them—cheekily named Bear-dar—is a medium-range polar bear detection radar currently being used at the Eureka Weather Station in Nunavut, Canada. Now the team is testing Bear-vision, a smart polar bear detection camera, at Churchill’s waste facility. According to McCall, it’s cheaper and simpler to use than Bear-dar, and works like a doorbell camera that warns you if there’s a polar bear hanging out outside.

“The team hopes to make the technology open-source so that Bear-vision will be widely available and accessible. More bear-safe tools mean fewer negative encounters, ultimately keeping people and polar bears separate and safer,” says McCall. “What’s happening along Hudson Bay shows how much communities can do to live harmoniously with nature.”

“I’m very satisfied. Clearly it’s three points and when you win it’s always very nice, but there’s also a way to win and tonight we did it with personality, with quality, playing a great match.”

Fortunately, we’ve had a few minor injuries, involving strong players, but not very long-term ones, nor even a large number of injuries, three or four at most.”

Here Is What an After-Dinner Walk Actually Does to Your Sleep, According to a Board-Certified Doctor

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Here’s a scenario many of us know too well: we eat a plate the size of a hubcap, then lose a fight with the couch by halftime. That slump has a name: postprandial somnolence, or what some people call a “food coma,” and it has a real biological basis.

As Outside previously reported, the smart move after a big dinner is a short, easy walk or “fart walk,” which helps food move through the digestive system—and may also help you get a good night’s rest.

I’m a board-certified internal medicine doctor; here’s how a walk, whether you decide to take a walk on your favorite trail or hit the treadmill, after a good meal may induce sleep.

Growing up, I was told that a big meal makes you sleepy because your stomach steals blood from your brain to digest the food. While digestion does pull extra blood to the gut, the brain guards its own supply closely, so blood flow isn’t the main reason you’re getting tired. Scientists now think a food coma results from several things piling up at once.

In lab studies, people got drowsier in the hours after eating, even when researchers accounted for the time of day and whatever was on the plate. Your internal clock dips in the early afternoon whether you eat or not, reducing performance. Sleep loss at night adds to the drowsiness, and so does a glass or two of wine. In experiments in mice, signals from a full stomach travel up the vagus nerve, a long nerve running from the brain to the gut, and switch on calming brain cells. Whether the same thing happens in people is still unknown.

What we do know is that some of the slump is out of your hands, but not all of it. How much you sleep, how much you drink, and what you do after you finish eating also matter. That’s where a walk can help.

The clearest evidence that walking after eating can be beneficial to digestion is seen in its effect on blood sugar. Getting moving right after a meal blunts the post-meal glucose spike better than sitting or exercising before you eat, and the sooner you start, the bigger the effect. Researchers think working muscles soak up sugar from the blood, and since most people sit after dinner, an evening walk may do the most good.

In healthy adults, eating more sugar and refined carbs has been tied to more nighttime stirring and lighter sleep. Among 18-year-olds, days with bigger glucose swings were followed by slightly shorter sleep. Adults who are overweight or those with obesity at risk for type 2 diabetes showed something similar: bigger daytime glucose swings were followed by earlier waking that night, and higher average glucose was tied to less efficient sleep.

Walking’s effects on sleep are promising but modest. A 2026 meta-analysis found that walking lengthened total sleep and cut time spent awake during the night, though it didn’t help people fall asleep faster, and it’s important to note that most trials tested daytime walking. A prevailing pattern was that sessions of 30 minutes or more showed clearer benefits for how well people felt they slept, so a longer walk might be the move.

A review article from the American Heart Association says that exercise is a zeitgeber, a cue that helps set the body clock. According to the review, physical activity generally improves sleep no matter what time of day you do it, and regular moderate-to-vigorous activity can strengthen the body’s daily rhythms.

The one wrinkle is timing. In a study of nearly 15,000 people wearing activity trackers, light or moderate exercise that wrapped up about two hours before bed wasn’t linked to worse sleep. Hard workouts late in the evening were. The easiest way to build in that buffer is to move dinner up. Eating earlier gives you room for the walk and leaves a longer gap before bed, which a large population study linked to better sleep quality.

Time your walk with sunset, and you may get a bonus: the fading light could help cue your body for sleep. As the sun goes down, the light reaching your eyes gets dimmer and less blue. Light-sensing cells in your eyes report that change to your brain’s master clock, which starts preparing to release melatonin and shift you toward sleep.

An after-dinner walk works best when it’s short, easy, and starts soon after you eat. Here are a few ways to get the most from it:

So the next time a food coma hits, lace up instead of lying down. A short, easy walk blunts the post-meal glucose spike, and as a regular habit, it may set you up for a better night’s rest.

The game's parody brands have taken over Miami.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

With under two weeks to go until the release of Grand Theft Auto VI for PS5 and Xbox, Rockstar Games has been cranking up the marketing for what's already the most-anticipated video game of all time. The publisher has been rolling out prominent billboards, bus wraps and digital placements globally (including a hand-painted GTA VI mural that was controversially "ruined" in Brooklyn).

Most of the advertising blitz uses familiar key art showing the game's two playable protagonists: Lucia Caminos and Jason Duval. But some of the best GTA VI ads don't show the game at all. in Vice City itself (that is, Miami, Florida), Rockstar continues to have fun with its many fictional parody brands, and the ads show just what a huge cultural event GTA has become.

“Rockstar is toning down their humor.” 💀 Meanwhile, GTA 6 ads on real-life public kiosks in Miami: 💀😭 From “Go Topless” to “Suddenly You Are Big Enough,” Rockstar is bringing Vice City’s unhinged humor straight into the real world. The double entendres, fake brands, and savage satire are STILL HERE. 💀🔥 GTA 6 isn’t toning down the humor. Rockstar is putting it on public display. 😂 Via: @GTAVI_Countdown #GTA6 #GTAVI #GrandTheftAutoVI #RockstarGames #ViceCityOctober 26, 2026

To the disappointment of all those real brands that are GTA VI trendjacking, Rockstar doesn't do product placement, and it's also quite selective when it comes to real-world collaborations. Instead, the game will feature hundreds of parody brands.

Some of these have crossed over into real world advertising, with billboards and digital displays cropping up in Miami for the likes of Crowex watches, Beige shapewear and Dix makeup. The game's takeover of the Miami Heat's Kaseya Center has even included outlets for the fictional fastfood chains Burger Shot and Cluckin' Bell.

Many of the ads continue Rockstar's penchant for using innuendo as a sharp social satire on real advertising, cutting through to real messages that brands are often communicating albeit in more euphemistic terms. "Always come early," the watch brand Kronos declares. "Suddenly You Are Big Enough," proclaims jewellery brand Kowalski on an ad showing a diamond-heavy engagement ring.

All the GTA 6 ads I saw on a kiosk in Wynwood (Miami). These shuffled between real ads 😂 https://t.co/l46q91OHRZOctober 19, 2026

It's been estimated that Rockstar's total global promotional costs could reach up to $200 million or even $300 million over the game's full launch cycle. I've seen some people wondering how much adverts for fictional brands like Dix and Juice Loose can contribute to the cause if it's not clear what they're promoting (there's a small logo and QR code in the bottom of some of them).

But this is what makes them so ingenious. They avoid traditional game marketing fatigue by showing the setting rather than the product. GTA is known for its satire of American consumer culture, and fictional companies, ridiculous slogans, and parody advertising are a core part of its identity. Putting these brands out there communicates the game's tone without showing a single mission or gameplay mechanic.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

By blurring the line between marketing and world-building, the ads also create intrigue among newcomers. When people see an advert for a bizarre fictional product with no obvious explanation, it generates curiosity and conversation, which becomes the marketing.

But, ultimately, they also reward existing fans who recognise names like Ammu-Nation, Sprunk, eCola, or other in-universe brands. For long-timer players, these are inside jokes that make them feel like they're being invited back into a world they love.

An advert for a kids' cartoon called Macca & Friends reads "Coming November 19 on Weazel”. The real message is clear to anyone who knows Weazel as the in-universe media company since GTA IV. This kind of advertising doesn't just promote a product; it makes people feel like they're part of the phenomenon. They deepen immersion, keep people talking and cement the game's status as not just the most-anticipated video game of all time but perhaps the most hyped entertainment product in any category.

The Vikings are famous for being warriors, but how frequently did Viking women take part in battles?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Vikings were explorers, farmers, merchants and, most famously, warriors who launched raids across large parts of Europe.

Stereotypes of Vikings usually conjure up images of muscular men. But over the past few decades, researchers have turned their attention to women of the Viking Age (roughly A.D. 793 to 1066) and the roles they held. For instance, there are stories of "shield maidens" who fought alongside men, and some of these texts mention Viking women who fought in battle. The archaeological record also holds evidence, including graves of Viking Age women who were buried with war-grade weapons and shields.

It's still a matter of debate whether women who lived during the Viking Age could be warriors, but this discussion raises another question: It's one thing to have texts describing female fighters and archaeological records of women buried with weapons, but what can the evidence tell us about how frequently Viking women fought in battle?

The answer, experts told Live Science, largely depends on our interpretation of texts, artifacts, and burials of Viking Age women, which often face higher scrutiny than burials of men who were buried with similar (or even fewer) weapons.

The Vikings used a runic system of writing, but they left precious few texts behind. Instead, much of what we know about the Vikings was written about them by people from other cultures or by their Norse descendants.

One of the most evocative texts was written in the 12th century by the Danish historian and theologian Saxo Grammaticus. In "Gesta Danorum" (Latin for "History of the Danes" and translated by Jenny Jochens), he wrote that "there were once women in Denmark who dressed themselves to look like men and spent almost every minute cultivating [combat] skills." As "if they were forgetful of their true selves they put toughness before allure, aimed at conflicts instead of kisses, tasted blood, not lips, sought the clash of arms rather than the arm's embrace, fitted to weapons hands what should have been weaving, desired not the couch but the kill, and those they could have appeased with looks they attacked with lances."

Another text about the Vikings, the medieval Irish "Cogadh Gaedhelre Gallaibh" ("War of the Irish with the Foreigners"), refers to a woman known as the "Red Lady" leading a Viking fleet in a raid on Belfast in the 10th century.

Feed your curiosity with an exclusive mystery every week, solved with science and delivered direct to your inbox before it's seen anywhere else.

There's a lot of uncertainty, said Neil Price, an archaeology professor at Uppsala University in Sweden who has written extensively about the Vikings. The text in the "Cogadh Gaedhel re Gallaibh" that refers to the Red Lady is of debatable accuracy, Price told Live Science in an email.

He suspects that Viking women fought in battle but not frequently. In addition, "female warriors were probably a very small minority," Price said.

A modern-day reenactment depicting Viking warriors.

The account written by Saxo Grammaticus is also contentious. Thomas Kristian Heebøll-Holm, a history professor at the University of Southern Denmark, said Saxo Grammaticus is not a reliable source.

"Saxo Grammaticus does write about female warriors, but only in the parts of his work concerned [with] the legendary past of Denmark," Heebøll-Holm told Live Science in an email. "Furthermore, these writings are not accurate, but moral and fictitious," Heebøll-Holm said, noting that, in some instances, Saxo Grammaticus seemed to be drawing on the stories of the Amazons, a group of legendary female warriors whom the ancient Greeks believed in with stories about them dating back more than 2,500 years.

Archaeological evidence can also be challenging to interpret. Archaeologists know of about 30 Viking Age female burials containing weapons, including three with swords in Scandinavia. Even more Viking Age women's graves have items that could have been used as weapons ‪—‬ including spears, axes and knives ‪—‬ although these tools also could have been used for hunting or tending to farms. Either way, we can't be sure if these women fought in battle.

"The first concern is that the weapons were really used by the deceased and were not merely included as signs of special status or social rank acquired through inheritance," Deborah Shepherd, an independent researcher who holds a doctorate in archaeology and has studied the Vikings extensively, told Live Science in an email. She noted that graves are often not well preserved, making it difficult to gather information.

This 1889 illustration shows a grave at Birka in Sweden. It is now known that the person buried with war-grade weapons and riding gear is a woman.

There were likely "some few female warriors, but they were probably only found in the highest ranking families, and their warrior career was probably short-lived," Shepherd said. "They wouldn't have fought in the great armies of later centuries but possibly did fight in pre-Christian skirmishes." (The Vikings began gradually converting to Christianity between the ninth and 12th centuries.)

There is at least one known case of a Viking woman found with what may be combat-related injuries. A Viking woman found in Solør, Norway, had a head injury consistent with a sword strike. She was 18 or 19 at the time she died, was buried with weapons and lived around 900.

Ole Kastholm, a curator and senior researcher at the Roskilde Museum in Denmark, said there is a lot of uncertainty regarding how frequently Viking Age women fought in battle.

Weapons "do occur as a grave good in a handful of graves with female human remains," Kastholm told Live Science in an email. "Often it is just a single weapon [such] as a spear or [a] battle axe. But we know of at least one example of a female grave with a full set of weapons as well as riding equipment," which is located in Birka, Sweden.

"If this was a man's grave, researchers would have no doubt that it was a warrior grave, and this explanation must necessarily also apply when the interred is a woman," Kastholm said.

That said, there were female warriors in other cultures. "One can also note in passing that female warriors were an almost common feature of central Asian societies for at least a millennium before the Viking Age," Price wrote in his book, "The Sea-King and the Sorceress: Life and Death in the Viking Age" (Basic Books, 2026).

For instance the Scythians are a people who flourished in eastern Europe and Asia and Scythian women have been found buried with horses and weapons. In some cases, the Scythian women suffered combat wounds such as arrowheads and it's been speculated that they may have been an inspiration for stories of the Amazons.

See how much you know about ancient norsemen with our Viking quiz!

Owen Jarus is a regular contributor to Live Science who writes about archaeology and humans' past. He has also written for The Independent (UK), The Canadian Press (CP) and The Associated Press (AP), among others. Owen has a bachelor of arts degree from the University of Toronto and a journalism degree from Ryerson University.

This South Dakota Hunter Borrowed a Bow and Tagged a 197-Class Deer — His First Archery Buck

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 8, 2026 10:00 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

It wasn’t the best conditions for bowhunting in central South Dakota on November 3rd. It was misting and drizzling rain and the wind was blowing between 20 and 30 miles per hour.

But Colby Claussen had seen a huge buck the previous week out of a tree stand and decided to buy a ground blind and place it near the trail where the buck had walked.

“I spotted him a week earlier from my stand at nearly 70 yards walking a trail along a field edge near a standing corn field,” Claussen tells Outdoor Life. “He was working scrapes. I decided to buy an enclosed blind and put it in the woods 30 yards off the trail he walked.”

A week later he was in the blind hunting the buck he’d seen on trail cameras off and on for several years.

“I first saw him several years ago through binoculars while hunting,” Claussen says. “The buck was a good 10 pointer then and about 600 yards away – way too far to shoot.”

After that the 20-year-old hunter got trail camera pictures of the buck and watched him grow over several years.

“This year I saw him from my treestand working scrapes, but he was too far for me to try a shot with the bow I’d borrowed from a friend,” says Claussen, a just-graduated student who farms with his family. The giant buck would be his first ever taken with a bow.

By 1 p.m. on Nov. 3, Claussen was in his ground blind, protected from wind and rain. He spotted a couple small bucks and does that afternoon. Just 10 minutes before shooting light ended that day, he spotted the enormous whitetail he was after.

“He stepped out of the woods to work a scrape at 30 yards, but it was too thick to shoot, so I waited,” Claussen says. “The buck stayed on the trail and kept coming toward me until he got in an opening, broadside at 30 yards.”

Claussen drew his bow and shot an arrow tipped with a G5 Megameat 3-blade expandable broadhead. It hit the buck perfectly behind its shoulders, driving through both lungs, clipping its heart, and exiting the massive deer.

“I knew he was hit well, and he went to the treeline, walked in a small circle, and disappeared into the timber,” says Claussen, who owns Sodak Drone Application, an agricultural business. “I went to where he stood and found my arrow and plenty of blood.”

Claussen waited 30 minutes before trailing the deer, then quickly found the buck just 60 yards from where he’d shot it.

He called family members who soon arrived and helped with field dressing and loading the estimated 300-pound buck into a pickup truck. Then they took it to a meat processor, with the buck’s cape and head delivered to a taxidermist.

Read Next: The Best Hunting Blinds of 2026, Tested and Reviewed

A taxidermist measured Claussen’s buck at 198 1/8 inches, and another skilled deer rack scorer came up with 197 7/8 inches. The non-typical rack has nine points on one side, six on the other, with an inside spread of 21.25 inches. Antler base circumferences are 5 3/8 inches. The buck’s estimated age is 5.5 to 6.5 years.

“A couple years ago I had photos of that buck with half a broken rack from fighting,” Claussen says. “I quit hunting him when he broke his rack. But another hunter that year missed him in rifle season.

“I’m sure glad that hunter missed, and I got another chance to take my buck of a lifetime.”

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

Five examples of Mesoamerican modern architecture

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Mexican architects have been combining principles from ancient architecture with modern techniques. Curator and writer Su Wu picks five examples of this phenomenon from her Mesoamerican Modern book.

Wu’s book, Mesoamerican Modern: Ancient Ideas in Contemporary Mexican Homes, details projects that illustrate this trend, from the 1970s to today, with a focus on residential architecture.

“These homes reflect the complex aesthetic cultures of Mesoamerica,” Wu told Dezeen. She chose the phrase that titles the book over “pre-Columbian” because she wanted to demonstrate that the monumentalism and logic of pre-Columbian Mexican architecture did not end with colonisation.

“I think these homes show instead that rather than being relegated to the past, these Mesoamerican materials, techniques, typologies, and forms are alive in the present and also perhaps inspiration or guides for the future.”

Each shows aspects of monumentalism that recall the stone architecture of the wider Mesoamerican culture, while acknowledging the diversity.

The book tries to depart from projects that just use symbols and focus on the logic of the buildings, noting specific characteristics such as patios as intrinsic the the design.

“For me, the most defining characteristics of Mesoamerican architecture are the ones that reflect underlying principles, rather than the disjunction when symbols become separated from their meaning,” Wu said.

“So throughout the book I wanted to highlight forms that arise from ideas such as the use of light or shadow as a physical object, or how homes are aligned to natural features, or the everyday presence of craft and the handmade, with an expanded sense of beauty as an attenuating consequence.”

Read on for five examples of the Mesoamerican modern style in Mexico with commentary by Wu.

“This 1973 home is one of a few 20th-century homes in Mesoamerican Modern, built by architect Agustín Hernández for his sister Amalia, the founder of Mexico City’s Ballet Folklorico.

“A poet as well as an architect, Hernández is celebrated for injecting ‘Mesoamerican semantics into the brute vocabulary of modernism’, but  wrote most of all about the geometric and cosmic principles at the heart of Mesoamerican architecture, and the ‘mathematical, almost symphonic rhythm’ of Mesoamerican structures and cities.

“His sister Amalia’s home was inspired by the convex forms of seashells as well as by the prominent ocular motifs in Mesoamerican imagery. “

Casa Mobius by Ernesto Gómez Gallardo, 1974

“Built by the architect for his own family in 1974, this house in Mexico City’s San Jeronimo neighborhood is today the studio and home of ceramicist Perla Valtierra.

“Shaped loosely like a mobius strip, with a triangular lattice allowing spaces to fold in upon themselves and a cantilevered living room, the home reconsiders both volumes and typologies, in rooms that are anything but square.

“The house is furnished with an eclectic mix of iconic Japanese, Brasilian and Mexican furniture, including a chair originally designed for the Museo Experimental El Eco in Mexico City by its founder Matthias Goeritz, a close friend and frequent collaborator of Luis Barragan.”

“Architect Teodoro González de León once described the patio as a defining characteristic of a Mesoamerican home, a space that is at once enclosed and exterior, and open to the elements.

“In this house in Estado de Mexico, Mexico City-based architect Fernanda Canales reclaimed land that had been devastated by cattle overgrazing, creating an interlocking series of patios that, in their deliberately undefined sense of function, create more space without having to build more of it.

“Each room in the home, which is approached through a pyramidal foyer with an opening to the sky, looks out onto courtyards or landscape, replete again with plants of the Americas.”

Casa Cometa by Taller Mauricio Rocha + Gabriela Carrillo, 2019

“In this home inspired by the spatial organization of ceremonial Mesoamerican sites, communal areas extend horizontally, while private spaces descend and rise vertically, like a dramatic bedroom tower with a daily view of both the sunrise and the sunset over the Pacific.

“With its overlapping platforms and passageways, the space designed by Gabriela Carrillo and Mauricio Rocha reflects the multiple planes of existence in Mesoamerican cosmology.

“In this house, a walkway weaves under the swimming pool, which itself slips under a long orthogonal platform, cut through with a descending staircase.”

“Casa Monte is a one-bedroom house with no exterior doors. Instead, the house deliberately discourages approach by appearing like a fortress over the tree line, and by its placement on a stepped platform, which creates a sense of separation from the jungle floor below.

“The open space with a pool cutting across the interior suggests the idea that architecture exists foremost as a way to engage with nature.

“Cast by Matos himself in earth-tinted cement on the beach where the house now sits, the home’s hundreds of individual forms were fitted together on-site.”

Feature: Box Art Brawl: The Legend Of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Ultimate Showdown

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Welcome all, to a very special edition of Box Art Brawl!

Looking back at last week, we checked out Revenge of the 'Gator to celebrate its addition to Nintendo Switch Online. Both designs for this one are great in their own way, but it was Japan that took home the trophy with 58% of the vote, leaving North America and Europe to mop up the remaining 42%.

This week, we're doing something special. We've already covered the original Zelda: Ocarina of Time for the N64 along with the 2011 3DS remake. However, with the release of the Switch 2 remake, we thought we'd throw every box art into the ring for an ultimate showdown.

You'll note that we haven't included every Ocarina of Time release here. The bonus disc included with Wind Waker pre-orders for the GameCube also included the logo for Master Quest, so we're not adding it here. Similarly, the Collector's Edition disc for GameCube had no direct reference to Ocarina of Time on its box art, so it too has been excluded.

So with that said, let's get cracking, hm?

Review The Legend Of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time (Switch 2) - A Stunning Remake, But Not Quite Definitive

Round Up The Reviews Are In For The Legend Of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time On Switch 2

Feature Song Of Time - Every Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Release, From N64 To Switch 2

This is the classic box art, right? The one of which most of you will likely be familiar. It's simple yet effective, with the game's iconic logo presented against a plain gold background. Chef's kiss. Did it really need anything else? Probably not, and it served as a wonderful thematic continuation of A Link to the Past.

Europe's design is equally simple, yet equally stunning. This time, we have a jet black background punctuated by a shining gold logo in the centre. In fact, all of the text here is in gold. Again, it's the simplicity that really sells it, and the contrast to the North American version is particularly striking.

Japan's N64 approach is very cool. It utilises the portrait orientation to great effect, showcasing an image of Link riding Epona, while flames surround the outside of the composition. There's a bit more going on here compared to the western designs, but there's still a sense of simplicity with the approach. It's not busy at all, and we love the contrast of the black and white background against the coloured logo.

The North American approach for the 3DS takes the same image of Link and Epona from the original Japanese release. It also leans heavily on that classic gold presentation, resulting in a classy yet eye-catching final image.

This here is the winner of our Ocarina of Time 3D brawl, and it's easy to see why. We can see Link and Epona depicted over on the right, but your eyes are just immediately drawn to the stunning landscape of Hyrule Field. Lon Lon Ranch, Hyrule Castle, and Death Mountain are all visible, and it's just such a stunning interpretation of the game's most iconic environments.

Okay, finally we have the box art for the latest release on Switch 2, and holy moly, it's absolutely gorgeous. Many folks will have picked up the textured foil version, and this just adds a whole new layer to its beauty. Interestingly, this is the only box art to feature Child Link, but it's a nice approach. It really delivers that sense of whimsy and adventure you feel during those opening portions of the game, and it's just such an impactful composition. Lovely stuff. Changing the logo text to gold is a nice touch too.

Sheffield United man answers question over long-term future after West Ham, QPR summer interest

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Sheffield United saw Harrison Burrows endure a tougher 2025/26 season after a fantastic debut campaign in 2024/25. Rival Championship clubs were lurking in the summer in case SUFC opted to cash in on the 24-year-old, with West Ham and QPR credited with interest while Burnley saw a late bid rejected.

However, Burrows has been back in the side and back in form in recent weeks. While speaking to The Star, the former Peterborough United man was asked about his long-term future and whether he sees it with the Blades, to which he responded: “Yeah, 100%. I’m really enjoying my football here. I really enjoy being with the lads, I really enjoy the club and I really enjoy the city. So, yeah, I’m very happy.”

Burrows is currently under contract at Bramall Lane until 2028. Sheffield United are looking to tie down a number of players to new deals, and the left-back is one of them alongside Sydie Peck, Femi Seriki and Michael Cooper.

As touched on before, Burrows endured a fantastic debut year at Sheffield United. After signing from Peterborough United, he stepped up to the Championship brilliantly as he recorded six goals and six assists in 48 games for a Blades side that made the play-off final.

Form proved harder to come by last term, and Sam McCallum emerged as the first choice at left-back before injury hit. He locked down his spot in Chris Wilder’s side early on this season too, performing well while Burrows was sidelined himself.

Harrison Burrows’ Sheffield United stats (all competitions)

However, injury has cast McCallum to the treatment room once again, and Burrows has made a fine return to the team. He has started the last five Championship matches, with only Saturday’s meeting at Charlton Athletic ending in defeat.

Burrows’ form has served as a reminder of what he offers on the left, with his technical ability making him a creative asset for SUFC. There are questions over the defensive side of his game at times, but the quality he offers with the ball can see him play a highly influential role when he’s in good form.

Aged 24, Burrows is already approaching 300 career games. He played 185 times for former club Peterborough United, and Saturday’s start against Charlton Athletic marked his 101st appearance for Sheffield United.

It means he has both an excellent amount of experience and plenty of room to keep developing. Burrows spent his youth in midfield and broke into first-team football in this position too, so he still has progress to make defensively.

With improvements in this area, SUFC will have a well-rounded left-back with strong experience and leadership abilities – as shown by his past captaincy at Peterborough United.

A challenging season and summer interest brought questions over his Bramall Lane future, but Burrows can be a valuable asset in the now and future if a new long-term deal is agreed.

Nintendo's Bill Trinen Explains Why Ocarina Of Time Is The Perfect Place To Start In The Zelda Universe

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

If you're in a position where you've never actually played a Zelda game before, Nintendo's Bill Trinen just wants to let you know Ocarina of Time is the "perfect place" to start.

Although this comment just happens to line up with the arrival of the Switch 2 remake, Nintendo's VP of Player & Product Experience really is convinced this is the best game to begin with, as it's a "foundational story", where the player is taught everything they need to know about the world and characters.

Here's what he had to say at the remake's launch event in San Francisco this week:

Bill Trinen: "This is the perfect place for your first Zelda game, because you are gonna learn everything that there is about the world and the characters that will serve sort of as the foundation for all the others, all the games that came after.

"This game is sort of a really foundational story for the lore of the Legend of Zelda. It really tells the story of Link and his journey becoming a hero. It tells the story of Link and his connection to Zelda and their relationship with Ganondorf. And overall, it communicates kind of what is at the moral core of is all the series, which is the idea that wisdom and courage combined can overcome power."

There will likely be more eyes than ever on the Zelda series in 2027, when the live-action film arrives in cinemas in April. So Ocarina of Time truly is a fantastic entry point into the Zelda universe if you haven't played the games before. The remake on Switch 2 also updates the gameplay and mechanics to offer a more accessible experience.

Review The Legend Of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time (Switch 2) - A Stunning Remake, But Not Quite Definitive

News Nintendo Excited About Broadening Zelda's Audience With Live-Action Movie

"That's one of the most exciting things for us"

2D though I'd probably say the best place to start is the Link's Awakening remake? And IS what Zelda games are supposed to be.
