Bitcoin price prediction — With $80K lost, is more downside ahead for BTC?

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Despite rebounding slightly above the $80,000-level, Bitcoin failed to hold above this level. In fact, it has since fallen to the $75K-level, where it has been consolidating for the last three days.

This relief rally was partly driven by liquidation of sell orders, which in turn produced a short squeeze. According to a CryptoQuant report, more than $290 million in shorts were wiped out, amplifying the uptick.

Furthermore, the stablecoin supply fell by about 5% over the past month, implying part of the capital was being swapped for BTC among other cryptos.

For instance, the Drift Protocol hacker went long after swapping $13 million USD Coin [USDC] on Hyperliquid for Bitcoin. Some large wallets were also accumulating the token even though the apparent demand remained negative, showing the market did not absorb available BTC.

However, Bitcoin did not hold above $80K despite this mild buying activity. This observation could be attributed to a couple of on-chain factors.

The report further observed that spot demand has remained weak, as the monthly average net spot volume delta was at -$28 million and -$9.40 million on Binance and Coinbase, respectively.

In addition, short-term holders have been moving their holdings to exchanges this month. While these transfers of about 32.70K BTC do not prove sales, they do imply looming selling pressure.

Moreover, institutional demand through BTC ETFs fell flat, with $412 million in net outflows recorded. The largest single outflow came from BlackRock’s IBIT, where traders sold $403 million in BTC.

Further analysis of the Hyperliquid exchange revealed that whales have been adding shorts. The net positioning turned bearish after seven months of being bullish.

The last time BTC had such a huge short exposure was in October 2025, when the price crashed to $60K thereafter. These bearish bets have simply added to the pressure.

Finally, the Coinbase Premium Index, which tracks both Coinbase and Binance for the U.S market, has stayed negative.

BTC’s price action at press time revealed a sideways consolidation since the crash to $75,000. The returning mild accumulation could offset bearish positioning and sustain a recovery.

However, there need to be improvements in the apparent demand, ETF inflows, and U.S buying pressure.

Muriuki Lazaro is a on-chain data analyst with a B.Sc. in Data Science. Muriuki specializes in dissecting complex on-chain data into clear and accurate insights for readers in the crypto ecosystem, with a particular focus on Bitcoin.

Digital Foundry Delivers Its Tech Verdict On Stellar Blade's Switch 2 Port

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Stellar Blade also arrived on the Switch 2 this week, and it's been positively received so far. Here on Nintendo Life, we gave it a score of seven out of ten, and although there are some technical drawbacks, Eve's outing stands tall on the hybrid platform.

The team at Digital Foundry has now delivered its tech review of the Unreal Engine 4 title, and while it's no "tour de force", it does translate to a "better than expected Switch 2 port", with what's described as an experience with "gentler compromises" than many more recent titles.

"Technically, it's not a tour de force either, with more conservative visual technology that doesn't leverage current gen hardware like many other high budget titles, but that does translate to a better than expected Switch 2 port, with gentler compromises than many more recent titles. Not everything survives intact, but the game's visual character isn't disrupted by the excision of key lighting systems or anything like that. Instead, players can expect a range of more iterative asset downgrades, some of which only really stand out in head-to-heads."

As for frame generation, on Switch 2, Digital Foundry says it's "not a bad choice considering the CPU and GPU limits of the console", but in the case of Stellar Blade, a "40fps targeting option" may have offered a better experience than the 60fps output with frame gen on. You can also turn frame gen "off" and the game will hover around the 40fps mark.

Overall, though, Stellar Blade is summed up as a "reasonable" and "playable experience" on Nintendo's new system. You can get the full rundown in the video above. There's also a demo on the eShop, and you find out more about how it performs in our review here on Nintendo Life:

Arsenal 1-0 Hull: Not efficient, but enough

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

In yesterday’s preview blog, I spoke about Arsenal’s attacking efficiency, and how that might be key to taking all three points. In yesterday’s game against Hull, Arsenal took 21 shots, 10 of which were on target, and scored one goal from that. Efficient?

Despite generally dominating possession, I thought there was a lack of precision in certain aspects of our first half play. Passes weren’t quite quick enough or accurate enough, but there were moments when we could have found the breakthrough. Gabriel almost got on the end of a Noni Madueke cross which was flicked on towards the back post, Rice had a tame shot from a free kick before a late header which he probably should have done better with.

And then there was Kai Havertz. It’s fair to say Hull didn’t leave a lot of room behind a defensive line that often had six players stretched across it, but late in the half a superb Martin Odegaard pass sent him clean through, one on one with the keeper. I haven’t seen replays but in real time I felt like he should have pulled the trigger – instead he over-complicated it. You have to be prepared to shoot from there, even if it’s on your wrong foot.

Then there was a penalty incident when he complained vociferously, play went on, the ref was then told to go to the screen and we all assumed it would be a penalty. However, the ref said it was a foul but Havertz was offside – and it felt like a massive waste of time. Afterwards, it emerged it should have been a penalty, but apparently the rule is ‘open to interpretation’. So clear as mud then.

In the second half, we seemed to play down our right a lot, with Madueke the outlet most of the time. I thought he did pretty well, much better than Christos Tzolis on the other side, but the breakthrough wouldn’t come. Mikel Merino replaced Rice (who seemed to be on the end of a wild challenge in midfield that the ref just ignored as he tumbled through the air), and Viktor Gyokeres came on for Havertz.

On 71 minutes though, we found the goal. Madueke’s shot was spilled by the keeper, Odegaard was quickest to react to poke the ball home for 1-0. And to be fair, you always felt confident that would be enough. We are the masters of the single goal margin win, and this was another one. Not a day that will live long in the memory or anything, but a 1-0 win that takes us into the Interlull in really good shape, and there’s not much arguing with that.

Or really, much more to say about this game. Mikel Arteta sounded happy enough, saying:

I’m delighted with the win. To go into the international break with a win is one of the best things about being a coach or player, I would say. Regarding the performance, I thought we were the better team, had enough chances to score and make it bigger for ourselves and we weren’t efficient enough to take them.

We then had to rely on generating volume, plenty of persisting until we finally broke them down. Martin then got the goal. Not the most beautiful one, but the most effective one, a simple ‘shoot and then follow the next action’, and that was ultimately enough to win the game.

So Arsenal sit top of the table, with 9 wins from 10 so far this season in the league, and it’s a very impressive run of form. Do I wish we had more attacking potency? Absolutely, and I do worry a bit that it might bite us in the arse somewhere down the line, but for now, we can see that this is a team that just knows how to win, and perhaps that’s the most important thing.

Right, I’m gonna leave it there for this morning. I have to head back to Dublin today. It was great to see lots of you over the weekend, until next time! And we’ll have an Arsecast Extra for you tomorrow too.

Air France A350 Nearly Collides Midair With American A321 After Wrong Turn At LAX

His background spans airport operations, aviation infrastructure, and travel consulting, having worked with global organizations across New Zealand, Europe, and North America. Combining industry expertise with firsthand travel experience across more than 52 countries and five continents, Aaron covers airlines, airports, aviation policy, and the wider travel sector. He is based in Canterbury, New Zealand. His work has been published in Simple Flying and featured by MSN, SAM CHUI Aviation & Travel, EIN News, Pakistan Defence, and Climate Depot.

This article was updated on Monday, November 9, 2026, to include new details on the unauthorized turn and a similar 2025 incident. It was originally published on Sunday, November 8, 2026.

Two aircraft belonging to American Airlines and Air France came within 400 feet (121 meters) of vertical separation as both jets departed from Los Angeles International Airport (LAX). Quick actions by air traffic control and the pilots onboard averted a close call that could have had catastrophic consequences.

The two planes were departing from California's busiest airport in the early hours of Saturday morning. Air France Flight 28 was heading for Papeete (PPT), while American Flight 1660 was flying to Dallas/Fort Worth International Airport (DFW). The two jets — which collectively could have been carrying more than 500 passengers — came dangerously close to each other shortly after takeoff, before controllers intervened to prevent a potential collision.

The Air France Airbus A350-900, operating as AF28, had been cleared to take off from Los Angeles via the SUMMR2 procedure. SUMMR2, according to the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA), is a set route that tells departing aircraft which direction to fly after takeoff. In this case, planes taking off from Runways 24L/R and 25L/R must initially climb straight ahead before following their assigned route.

At the same time, the American jet, destined for Texas, was told by air traffic control to stay at 2,000 feet, to allow the Air France widebody to turn and then head to FL270. However, an unauthorized turn by the Air France aircraft brought the two jets onto conflicting flight paths.

Simple Flying reached out to Air France for comment on the incident; however, an airline spokesperson could not immediately be reached.

According to TurbineTraveler on X, the Air France plane made an unauthorized turn left shortly after takeoff, rather than continuing straight as instructed. This placed the A350 directly in the trajectory of the departing American jet. This led the two planes to come within dangerous proximity of each other, known as a loss of separation event.

The controller quickly spotted the developing conflict and instructed both aircraft to turn away from each other. The controller then questioned the Air France crew about the deviation from its departure route. Both aircraft involved are detailed in the table below, with flight data captured from the Simple Flying Flight Tracker:

Both the American and Air France flights were then able to continue safely to their intended destinations with no reported injuries or harm to anyone on board. A similar incident occurred in Los Angeles in 2025, when an American and ITA Airways jet almost collided midair after a pilot deviation from the busy airport.

Two aircraft lost separation while climbing out of LAX.

So far, reports indicate that neither crew received a Traffic Collision Avoidance System (TCAS) resolution advisory, an automated cockpit warning that instructs pilots to climb or descend to avoid another aircraft. These alerts can prevent midair collisions when aircraft get too close, giving pilots immediate instructions to increase their separation.

Since the incident was first reported, outlets have published different figures for how close AF28 and AA1660 came to each other. According to The Aviation Herald, the A350 began turning left while climbing through 1,800 feet, despite its departure procedure calling for a straight climb to 5,000 feet.

The incident also bears striking similarities to an event involving ITA Airways in October 2025, when a Rome-bound Airbus A330-900 departing Runway 24L turned left toward an American A321 that had taken off from Runway 25R.

As one of the busiest airports in the US and the world, Los Angeles International Airport has carefully controlled departure routes to keep aircraft safely separated. The SUMMR2 procedure directs westbound departures out over the Pacific Ocean, helping keep them clear of other departing and arriving traffic.

SUMMR flights often transition to long-haul or coastal routes, including to destinations in Hawaii, Asia, and the north, like San Francisco. Instead of following this procedure, the Air France pilot made a sharp left turn shortly after departure, which intersected the path of the American jet that had departed from Runway 25R. Fortunately, the departure controller identified the conflict, and their swift actions resulted in the two planes being able to quickly change course and avoid a midair collision.

Mammograms May Offer Early Insight Into Cardiovascular Disease

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Mammograms are widely used to help detect breast cancer, but they may also offer insight into heart health.

The routine X-ray screening may identify breast arterial calcification (BAC), which is the presence of calcium deposits within the walls of the small arteries in the breast.

On a mammogram, BAC is commonly identified as thin, white, parallel lines that give a “tram-track” appearance.

While BAC is harmless to the breasts, it is associated with a significantly higher risk of cardiovascular disease. A 2022 study found women with BAC were 51% more likely to develop heart disease or experience a stroke than those without it.

Laxmi Mehta, MD, a cardiologist at The Ohio State University Wexner Medical Center, said BAC is associated with cardiovascular risk factors, such as diabetes, high blood pressure, and high cholesterol. Additionally, BAC has been connected to:

“The reasons for BAC resulting in higher risks for cardiovascular disease are not definitely determined; however, there are some potential mechanisms, one being that they share similar risk factors that result in vascular injury or metabolic stress that result in vascular calcification and atherosclerosis,” Mehta told Healthline.

Cheng-Han Chen, MD, interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center, added that because BAC and heart disease share many of the same risk factors, “calcium in breast arteries therefore suggests disease in the heart (coronary) and brain arteries as well,” he told Healthline.

While 1 in 39 females in the United States dies from breast cancer each year, cardiovascular disease causes one out of every three deaths, resulting in 1 death per minute.

“Cardiovascular disease is still the most common cause of death in premenopausal women,” Richard Reitherman, MD, PhD, a radiologist and medical director of breast imaging at MemorialCare Breast Center at Orange Coast Medical Center, told Healthline.

“The presentation of cardiovascular disease in premenopausal women is notoriously different than in postmenopausal women, primarily [because] there are no typical symptoms such as chest pain.”

Reitherman said that a prevalent and common health screening procedure such as a mammogram can lead to the early identification of women with silent cardiovascular disease.

“Cardiovascular outcome studies that have included breast arterial calcifications as a risk factor are increasingly reported in the medical literature,” he said.

“The hope is that identifying younger women with silent cardiovascular risks [like BAC along with] appropriate integrated risk identification, analysis, and interventions can significantly reduce mortality in this age group.”

Mehta said there are several reasons BAC has been overlooked as a risk factor for heart disease.

“Mammograms are completed for assessment of breast cancer, and BAC has been historically an ‘incidental’ finding, which is not clinically relevant from an oncologic standpoint,” she said.

Additionally, there is no standardization on quantification of BAC severity, nor is it a required component of mammogram reports, said Mehta.

However, routine reporting of BAC on mammograms is an urgent goal of the American College of Radiology, Society of Breast Imaging, and Medicare, said Reitherman.

In fact, it is gaining traction in the United States. A new Maryland law, passed on October 1, requires mammography centers to notify women when a BAC is detected on their mammogram.

“Automated identification and reporting of these calcifications detected on screening mammograms will become increasingly prevalent in the coming months [and] years,” said Reitherman.

The next step is to identify this group of women to primary care physicians so that appropriate risk evaluation can take place, Reitherman added.

In addition to doctors routinely screening for conditions related to heart disease, such as diabetes, high blood pressure, and high cholesterol, he hopes BAC will also be routinely considered.

“Under current development are protocols to identify patients needing a cardiologist’s training and expertise to appropriately stratify this group of patients,” said Reitherman.

“The common healthcare behavior [of] having regular mammograms not only reduces risk of morbidity and mortality from breast cancer but will hopefully contribute to the reduction of morbidity and mortality from cardiovascular disease in younger women.”

He said this type of integrated healthcare approach demonstrates cross-specialty integration and coordination among women, radiologists, and their healthcare providers to improve women’s lives.

“This is an amazing game-changing opportunity,” Reitherman said.

Mehta said it’s reasonable for women to ask if BAC is present on their mammogram report.

“But if it is not uniformly reported by the radiologist, the individual clinician may not have the answer,” she said.

More research is needed before BAC on mammograms can be used routinely to predict heart disease.

Finding out your BAC could provide additional information to help you and your doctors decide how aggressively to address your cardiovascular risk factors, Chen said.

Reitherman agreed. “The goal of reducing morbidity and mortality from any cause such as cardiovascular disease is based on recognition of risk factors, followed by appropriate lifestyle and pharmacologic interventions,” he said.

Güler scores and assists as Real Madrid win at Mestalla to draw level with Barcelona

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Arda Güler scored once and set up another as José Mourinho's Real Madrid beat Valencia 3-0 to reach 30 points, level with Barcelona, before the international break. Valencia are second from bottom in their last season at Mestalla.

Federico Valverde put Madrid ahead at Mestalla in the 28th minute on Saturday 7 November 2026. Güler doubled the lead eight minutes later. Jude Bellingham reached the byline and pulled the ball back. Güler met it first time and finished with his right foot.

Güler could have had more. Stole Dimitrievski stopped him twice: a strong shot from the edge of the area in the 43rd minute and a one-on-one in the 54th.

Kylian Mbappé’s strike in the 75th minute made it three. That goal stretched his scoring run to seven matches in a row. He has 12 league goals, three behind Barcelona’s Raphinha at the top of the scoring chart.

The visitors had 68% of the ball and completed 568 passes to Valencia’s 225. They took 19 shots, 11 of them on target. Valencia managed four shots and just two on target. Expected goals finished 2.39 to 0.26 in Madrid’s favour.

The hosts’ best moment came around the 20th minute. Aaron Mayol won the ball just outside the area and forced Thibaut Courtois to stretch for a save. Aguirre sent on Luis Rioja for Justin de Haas at half-time, but the pattern did not change.

The game grew bad-tempered. Valencia committed 26 fouls to Madrid’s six, and Aguirre himself was booked for dissent in the 59th minute. In the club’s own view, the punishment for Gayà’s challenge on Mbappé was harsh. Seven yellow cards were shown in all, four of them in a two-minute spell after the red card.

Two wins from 12 league games is all Valencia have to show so far. Only Malaga, on 5 points, are below them. Aguirre has one win from his five league games in charge. The club is already promoting season-ticket prices for the new Nou Mestalla.

The coach is concerned about the home form. Aguirre told EFE he was worried about failing to win any of his three matches at Mestalla. He added that Valencia were short of points with six games left in the first half of the season.

Three goals 🟰 Three points @emirates pic.twitter.com/KepMmEJqB4

— Real Madrid C.F. 🇬🇧🇺🇸 (@realmadriden) November 7, 2026

Madrid’s run has kept them in a three-way race at the top. Barcelona have played 11 games to Madrid’s 12 and sit top on goal difference. Atlético Madrid are third, two points behind with a game in hand.

Valencia return to action first. They face UE Tavernes in the Copa del Rey first round on Tuesday 10 November at 9pm at Camp de Mestalla. The tie was rearranged after the RFEF called off every Cup match on its original date following a fatal rail accident in Toledo. Madrid host Celta Vigo in LaLiga on Sunday 22 November.

Matteo Thun designs Torre Velasca interiors using "building itself as the starting point"

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Photography by Alberto Strada (unless stated otherwise)

Italian architect Matteo Thun looked to the original materials and colours of Milan’s iconic Torre Velasca when creating its new interiors, which feature pink hues inspired by the tower’s dusky facade.

Thun has designed 72 flats in the upper floors of the tower, ranging from suites to duplex penthouses. He approached the interior design of the skyscraper, built in the 1950s by the architecture studio BBPR, with a focus on reinterpretation.

“We allowed the masters and the building itself to speak, imagining a way of living within it that would carry the building a little further into the future, adding a new chapter to its history,” Thun told Dezeen.

“We did not seek contrast: our design was informed directly by the materials and colours of Torre Velasca, and we chose not to intervene where we felt the architecture had already achieved a sense of completeness.”

His studio preserved original details, including doors, window frames, loggias and tiles in the bathrooms and kitchens. These were then juxtaposed with modern furniture and evergreen design classics.

“The interiors bring together new furniture designed by the studio and design references spanning the 1950s to the 1980s, including iconic pieces developed in new colourways in collaboration with historic Milanese companies,” Thun said.

“With a deep appreciation for craftsmanship and a strong attention to detail, these designs share an ability to evolve without losing their identity.”

The colour palette, too, was inspired by the original hues of the Torre Velasca.

In the living rooms, walls are covered in custom-made velvet wall coverings by textile company Dedar in a pale pink colour that resembles the pink plaster on Torre Velasca’s facade.

“The building itself was our starting point,” Thun said.

“The pink plaster, developed and patented specifically for the tower, together with the blue and green floor tiles found both inside and in the loggias, formed the basis of our colour palette,” he continued.

“We developed different gradations of these tones, opting for darker shades of green and blue to define the entrance areas and bedrooms. “

As well as the apartments, Thun also designed the interior of the reception, lobby and the corridors on the ground floor.

And while this was an interior project, Thun says it ended up becoming “a lesson in architecture” for his studio.

“Working with a building as distinctive as Torre Velasca challenged us to think and work first and foremost as architects,” Thun said.

“The challenge was not to reinvent the building, but to find ways to make its original character feel relevant today, while remaining true to BBPR’s vision,” he continued.

“In this sense, the project became a process of discovery, with the building itself serving as our greatest teacher. It was, in many ways, a lesson in architecture for us.”

Thun has previously designed the interior of the boutique hotel The Julius Prague and “hotel-like” rooms for a German hospital.

Photography by Alberto Strada (unless stated otherwise)

Zelda: Ocarina of Time returned this week on the Switch 2 and it looks better than ever. If you are wondering just much of a facelift it's had since the 2011 outing, and the original N64 hit, you might want to check out this side-by-side comparison video.

YouTube channel ElAnalistDeBits has now released a "final graphics comparison" which takes a closer look at the character models, environments, physics, the many locations in the game, and much more.

If you are wondering about the performance, the remake on Switch 2 runs at 60fps. In comparison, the original N64 outing ran at 20fps. And as for the resolution, this new remake ranges from 1080p to 1440p in docked, and 720p to 1080p in handheld (via Digital Foundry).

Tesla Hasn’t Had A Successful New Product Launch Since JB Straubel Stepped Down As CTO

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

As has been widely reported, Tesla is about to end Cybertruck production. The Cybertruck has been a shocking failure. I’m sure there are more detailed analyses out there somewhere, but this short response on X from Ben McCombe that Tina Casey included in her article on the matter sums it up very well:

“Installed tooling to build 125k Cybertrucks a year.

“Made just $100m in gross profits from sales.

That’s pretty wild. A net project loss of around $2 billion. Now, I have to admit that I haven’t fact checked the numbers here, but what we know for certain is that Tesla planned to produce a lot of Cybertrucks per year and ended up producing a small fraction of that target. In fact, the annual sales target for the Cybertruck was higher than 125,000 — it was 250,000, with a goal of getting up to 500,000!

The Cybertruck was hyped up as having more reservations than any vehicle in history and was supposedly going to shatter records and transform the market. I warned people on many occasions that the reservation total was not a real reservation total and was definitely not a reliable indicator of demand because a) a reservation only required a $100 refundable deposit, and b) a ton of people made reservations after Elon Musk said that you could lock in the price of lifetime Full Self Driving when making a reservation (so, you’d only have to pay $6,000 for FSD no matter how much the cost of it went up in the following years). Anyway, the Cybertruck had other issues as well when it finally arrived. It cost about twice as much as it was supposed to cost and it had about half as much range as it was supposed to have. In other words, it was not even close to the vehicle it was supposed to be — its range per dollar cost was about ¼ what it was supposed to be.

The Cybertruck was supposed to be the next big thing after the Tesla Model Y. It was supposed to disrupt the gigantic US pickup truck market. The Model S was a superb and widely acclaimed car, if a bit more expensive than the mass market could afford. The Model S did in fact compete superbly well with the leaders in its segment, the BMW 7 Series and Mercedes S-Class. The Tesla Model X brought a similar offering in a larger package with some fun extra elements (falcon-wing doors, self-presenting doors, a third row). The Tesla Model 3 then completely shook the automotive world as a tremendous market phenomenon and perhaps the biggest product launch in history (in terms of total revenue). The Tesla Model Y was basically the Model 3 in a more popular crossover package and eventually became a much higher selling vehicle. In fact, it became the highest selling vehicle model of any kind in multiple years. Then JB Straubel stepped down as CTO.

Due to their EV passions, JB Straubel and Elon Musk were introduced to each other and had lunch together to talk about electric airplanes (Elon’s dream) and then electric cars (because JB talked more sense into Elon). Then the two of them were introduced to Marc Tarpenning and Martin Eberhard, who had founded Tesla Motors and just barely got started on the idea. After a while, Tarpenning and Eberhard left the company, and Musk and Straubel truly led its development and growth into an automotive superpower (as briefly described above). Since JB Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO of Tesla, however, if one thinks about it, it’s hard to identify any successful product launch from the company or much significant improvement at all.

Straubel left his CTO position in July 2019. The Model Y was unveiled in March 2019 and was about ⅔ the same as the Model 3. It would seem that getting the Model Y designed and prepared for launch was the last big project Straubel worked on. (Note that the Semi was unveiled in November 2017 but was long delayed due to battery supply, according to Elon Musk.) Since the Model Y, what significant product launch have we seen from Tesla? There was the Cybertruck, as we’ve already discussed. That was unveiled in November 2019. When you think about the timing now, did Straubel disagree strongly with Musk on the Cybertruck and decide it was time to move on when Musk pushed forward with his idea for an electric truck? It took another 4+ years for Tesla to start delivering the Cybertruck, in contrast to Model Y deliveries starting in January 2020, less than a year after the model was unveiled. More recently, the Cybercab has been developed, unveiled, and is now in production. A consumer car smaller and cheaper than the Model 3 was never developed and unveiled — just a stripped down, decontented Model 3 — and there basically haven’t been any other product unveilings since Straubel left his role as CTO. Elon Musk has published a couple of somewhat scattered, rambling “master plans,” but we haven’t gotten any significant new products aside from the Cybertruck and the just arriving Cybercab.

Even looking over on the energy storage side of things, the Tesla Megapack was launched in July 2019, the month Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO. Did Straubel just decide he had done enough once he had finished the Megapack launch and gotten the Model Y essentially ready for market? Did he have conflicts with Musk on where to take the company from there?

Yes, there’s been incremental product development and refreshed versions of Tesla cars and energy storage systems. There are still plenty of engineers working on improving things. However, there doesn’t seem to be the same leadership in new product development, and there also isn’t the same practical, moderating element that Straubel seemingly provided to Musk’s more wild, grandiose, sci-fi inspired dreams and ideas.

Perhaps this is all misunderstanding the roles of the two men to some extent. Perhaps things would not be much different if Straubel had stayed in the CTO role and had the same influence he had before leaving. He is still on the board of directors. (What that actually means is unclear, though.) Perhaps Tesla’s success and business would still be primarily based on the Model 3 and Model Y, without new transformative products. Or perhaps we would have had a more normal pickup truck from Tesla, an electric van, better batteries, a better market rollout of the Semi, and other products. Perhaps the Cybertruck, an epic product failure, never would have been shown to the product and hyped up unrealistically with specs and figures disconnected from what was going to be possible. Perhaps we’d have a Model C (affordable, compact Tesla) rather than a Cybercab. Perhaps Tesla would be a very different company.

Who knows? We don’t know the ins and outs of JB Straubel and Elon Musk’s relationship, or how much Straubel actually led Tesla in his CTO role. We don’t know how much Straubel tampered down Musk’s worst instincts or guided product development. We still really don’t even know why Straubel left. According to Tesla, it was so that he could focus more time on Redwood Materials, but why wasn’t that business within the umbrella of Tesla in the first place? Vertical integration has long been a key element of the company. Who knows? There’s a lot we don’t know and never will. However, what we do know is that since JB Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO of Tesla, we haven’t seen a significant, successful new product launch at the company. We’ll have to wait a bit longer to see if the Cybercab is one, and whether that sets a new, positive course for the future of the company or not.

sweeping concrete cutouts shape ZAO’s sculptural art center in rizhao

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

eye-shaped openings carve through the concrete structure to create an open-air cultural space beside a wooded hillside and lake.

ZAO / Zhang Ke Architecture Office has completed the Rizhao Community Art Center in China‘s Shandong province, where a sculptural concrete structure rises from a wooded hillside beside a small lake. The building appears to hover above the landscape, its heavy upper volume supported at three corners while sweeping, eye-shaped openings carve through the level beneath. From a distance, its curved surfaces seem to extend the contours of the surrounding terrain.

Located in Bailuwan, also known as Egret Bay, the project forms part of a larger residential and resort development along China’s Yellow Sea coast. Conceived as a cultural destination for the surrounding community, the center brings exhibition spaces together with areas for public gatherings and performances. Its unusual arrangement places the galleries underground, leaves the central level open to the landscape, and introduces a series of courtyards above.

A network of deep, curving concrete beams supports the upper level, transferring its weight through three structural cores that also accommodate the stairways. This allows the architects to leave much of the ground floor open, with broad openings extending toward the grassy slopes. The concrete ceiling rises and dips overhead, creating an expansive sheltered space that changes in height as visitors move through it.

Footbridges connect this central level with the surrounding roads and pathways, while its northeastern side opens toward the lake. Designed to accommodate outdoor cinema, theater performances, and community events, the space can also be used simply as a place to pass through or gather. With access from several directions, the building becomes part of the landscape’s circulation, offering a public space beneath its substantial concrete roof.

The building’s exterior is formed from cast-in-place concrete, with insulated double-layer walls that retain the grain of their timber formwork. The horizontal impressions lend texture to the large curved surfaces, particularly along the underside of the structure. At the junction between the upper and lower volumes, a narrow opening allows glimpses of the surrounding greenery to enter the otherwise enclosed concrete stairwells.

Moving upstairs, visitors encounter a very different spatial experience. Here, the structural framework defines a sequence of trapezoidal courtyards and smaller community rooms, arranged along winding passages. Inspired by the inward-facing spaces of traditional Chinese gardens, the upper level alternates between enclosed interiors and pockets of open sky. An accessible rooftop terrace eventually opens the building back toward the surrounding forest and lake.

Beneath the grassy hillside, the center’s exhibition spaces are reached by a gently sloping concrete ramp. The gallery level follows the existing topography, with a sculptural ceiling that echoes the changing contours of the ground above. Structural elements divide the interior into interconnected spaces, allowing visitors to move between more enclosed rooms and wider areas for displaying art.

Across these different levels, ZAO uses the same concrete structure to create distinctly different encounters with the landscape. The upper courtyards offer sheltered spaces for reflection, while the central floor remains exposed to the surrounding terrain. Below, the galleries turn visitors’ attention toward art within the hillside itself. Together, these spaces give the community a building that can be explored as readily as the landscape around it.

Broncos and Bo Nix have won 16 straight one-score games

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The Broncos keep finding ways to win games — especially close games.

Denver has won 16 straight games decided by one score or less. Quarterback Bo Nix has been the starter in each of the games.

Via NBC Sports research, the streak ties the 2023-24 Chiefs (with starting quarterback Patrick Mahomes) for the longest streak since 1950.

The Broncos last lost a one-score game in Week 3 of the 2025 season, 23-20 at the Chargers. Since then, the Broncos have won every one-score game in which they have played.

This year, they’re doing it despite being routinely outgained. For the season, they have a yardage differential of minus-658 — third worst in the NFL. And the Broncos have won three games in 2026 despite being outgained by 100 or more yards.

The record for any season is four wins, accomplished by eight different teams. With two more this year, the Broncos will have the record to themselves.

The next opportunity to win a one-score game comes on Sunday, at the Panthers in a rematch of Super Bowl 50.

How to Harness Your Existential Wanderlust

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

“Man* is by Nature a migratory animal,” the elderly Frederick Douglass reflected in an 1887 speech about his global travels. “It does not appear that he was intended to dwell forever in any one locality. He is a born traveler.”

A generation after him, Maya Angelou observed that “you only are free when you realize you belong no place — you belong every place — no place at all.”

Partway in time between Douglass and Angelou, the painter, printmaker, and philosopher Rockwell Kent (June 21, 1882–March 13, 1971) captured this vital interplay between freedom and belonging, between nature and human nature, in the preface to Wilderness (public library) — the exquisite record of the seven months he spent on a remote Alaskan island with his young son in the gloaming hour of the Spanish flu pandemic and the First World War, at the dawn of his artistic life.

We each have a particular style of wanderlust, pulled by particular types of nature that best speak to our own — those places where we most freely lose ourselves and, in consequence, find ourselves. For some, they are the austere open space of blue skies and red canyons. For me, they are the mossy old-growth forests of New Zealand and the American Pacific Northwest. For Kent, it was the severe majesty of the Great North:

It has always been hard for me to understand myself, to know why I work and love and live. Yet it is fortunate that such matters find a way of caring for themselves. I came to Alaska because I love the North. I crave snow-topped mountains, dreary wastes, and the cruel Northern sea with its hard horizons at the edge of the world where infinite space begins. Here skies are clearer and deeper and, for the greater wonders they reveal, a thousand times more eloquent of the eternal mystery than those of softer lands.

In a new preface to his journals, penned in the final year of his life, Kent looks back on the wanderlust of his youth — the roaming restlessness that had shaped his spirit, the spirit from which his art sprang, the art that established him as one of the most celebrated creators of his time — and exhorts the generations of wanderers to come:

Wander where you will over all the world, from every valley seeing forever new hills calling you to climb them, from every mountain top farther peaks enticing you. Always the distant land looks fairest, till you are made at last a restless wanderer never reaching home — never — until you stand one day on the last peak on the border of the interminable sea, stopped by the finality of that.

Contemplating the existential pull beneath it all — the rippling, resonant why of our wanderlust — Kent adds:

We are part and parcel of the big plan of things. We are simply instruments recording in different measure our particular portion of the infinite. And what we absorb of it makes for character, and what we give forth, for [our art].

These are salutary words to read a century later, as our own world is only just shaking off its straitjacket of two-year terror and becoming wanderable again.

Complement with Rebecca Solnit’s wanderlusting history of walking, then revisit Kent’s breathtaking reflections on wilderness, solitude, and creativity.

Two of those hopefuls, Carson Kvapil and Sam Mayer, entered the pits just before Anderson, after a battle for the lead.

What is malloc doing, anyway?

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

malloc has a very nice and simple interface: you ask for a certain amount of memory, and you’re given a void pointer to your block of contiguous bytes that’s been magicked into existence. Once you’re done with it, you call free with the same pointer and the memory disappears into the ether.

But what’s actually happening here? Obviously, as we’re running as a userspace program, malloc must be asking the kernel for the memory through syscalls. However, we may be allocating many small blocks, and syscalls are expensive (575 ns for an empty one, as measured below), so some bookkeeping is done in the background. We’ll demonstrate this on GNU libc (glibc) with the program snippet below, which calls malloc(16) a thousand times.

Using strace, we can see that the whole loop makes only two brk syscalls: the first asks where the program break is (the address where the process’s heap ends), and the second grows the heap by 132 KiB. These are both done in the first malloc call — the other 999 just take from that already allocated memory.

You may see both brk and sbrk mentioned in discussions. brk is a syscall that sets the program break to the address it is provided, and sbrk is a libc convenience wrapper that takes a delta, increasing the program break by that amount.

A running process gets its own address space, split into a number of regions. These include text for executable code, data for initialised global variables, and bss for uninitialised global variables, among others. The following program prints one address from each region of the process.

The addresses rise from the binary’s code up to its stack.

Some of these regions can change as the program runs. For example, malloc(16) grows the heap and moves the break up. Note that in this run, the break and printf in libc are 23.4 TiB apart, about 1,530 times the machine’s 15.6 GiB of RAM. Nearly all of that range is unmapped, so it costs no physical memory.

Because we can move the program break directly, we can write a little allocator of our own. This is called a bump allocator, because each call simply bumps up the amount of memory that we are allowed to use.

This allocator is not very good! It moves the break on every single request, and each move is a syscall. We can time a syscall against malloc to see why malloc requests large blocks from the kernel and cuts them up itself. Each of the following three functions is timed over a million calls.

sbrk(16) costs only 35 ns more than the empty syscall, whereas malloc(16) runs 22.7 times faster than sbrk(16).

The bump allocator also has no way to reuse freed memory. Each request lands straight after the one before, so in the following example, d goes after c even though b has been freed.

The rest of the post improves the allocator one stage at a time.

malloc actually allocates more memory than you request. This is because each allocation, or chunk, has a size header just before the returned pointer; this is how free works without passing an explicit size. Chunk sizes are always multiples of 16, so the low bits of the size are always zero, and glibc keeps three flags in them.

Each chunk is the request plus 8 bytes for the header, rounded up to a multiple of 16, with a minimum of 32. The rounding keeps every pointer that malloc returns 16-byte aligned, which is the alignment x86-64 gives max_align_t, so the memory suits any C type. The 32-byte minimum leaves room for the list pointers that a chunk stores once it is freed.

When we free a chunk, it is put into what’s called a free list to allow reuse. When more data is requested, malloc looks through this free list and splits large chunks to fit small requests, and free merges free neighbours back together. Each chunk in the free list needs two pieces of information: its size (which we already have), and a pointer to the next chunk in the free list. glibc stores the pointer after the size, reusing the memory that the user stored their data in before it was freed!

The toy allocator stores the same two fields in each chunk.

We can improve our my_malloc by rounding the request plus its header up to a multiple of 16 bytes, and taking the first chunk in the free list that is big enough. To keep things simple, the toy’s header is the whole chunk_t, so a chunk keeps room for next even while it is in use. That makes the header 16 bytes where glibc’s is 8, and my_malloc(100) takes a 128-byte chunk where malloc(100) takes 112.

We define take to split that chunk, leaving whatever the request does not need in the free list.

When no free chunk fits, grow_heap moves the program break up in 64 KiB steps using sbrk and puts the new space on the free list as one chunk.

With headers, we can now actually implement a proper my_free function! It steps back from the pointer to the header and hands the chunk c to insert_free. That function walks the list to find the free chunks either side of c by address, prev and next, and links c in between them. Then, if c ends where next starts, or starts where prev ends, the two become one chunk.

A test program allocates a, b and c, frees a and b, allocates d, then frees the rest.

Each line shows the chunk that the call allocated, if any, and then the free list. Every chunk is an [offset, size] pair counted from the start of the heap, and the offset is where its header starts, 16 bytes before the pointer that my_malloc returns.

d gets the chunk at offset 0, split from the 352-byte chunk that a and b merged into. The bump allocator put its fourth request after everything else. Freeing c merges three chunks into one, and freeing d leaves the single 64 KiB chunk that grow_heap added.

my_free reuses memory, but it never returns any to the kernel. Whether a free chunk still costs RAM depends on when the kernel puts physical memory behind an address. Each process sees virtual memory, which the kernel maps onto physical RAM in 4 KiB pages. That separation allows the kernel to hand out address ranges with no physical RAM behind them — a virtual page only needs to be backed by physical memory on its first write.

The following program moves the break up by 1 GiB, then writes one byte to every page and prints a row after each 256 MiB.

Virtual size counts the addresses the process has mapped, and resident set size (RSS) counts the pages that are in RAM. Straight after sbrk, virtual size is already just over 1 GiB, while RSS is 1308 KiB. Each 256 MiB written then costs 65,536 page faults, one per 4 KiB page, increasing the RSS.

Once a chunk’s pages have been written, they stay resident after my_free, until the allocator gives them back to the kernel.

We give memory back to the kernel by lowering the break. We introduce a new function, trim, that lowers the break once the free chunk at the top of the heap reaches 128 KiB, keeping 64 KiB so that the next few requests don’t move the break straight back up.

A test program allocates 1 MiB and then a 16-byte chunk above it, then writes to every page of the 1 MiB block. (Anonymous RSS counts resident memory with no backing file, which keeps libc’s code out of the numbers.)

Freeing the 1 MiB block leaves the break at 1088 KiB and anonymous RSS at 1124 KiB, because the 16-byte chunk above it is still live. Once that chunk goes too, trim lowers the break to 64 KiB, dropping the anonymous RSS to 160 KiB.

Only the top of the heap can be given back. A live chunk holds the break above it, so every free chunk below it stays resident, as the 1 MiB block did here. Over a long run, many allocations and deallocations leave free gaps between live chunks that later requests rarely fill, which is called fragmentation. These gaps stay resident, so in a long-running program, fragmentation can grow RSS until a memory limit kills the process.

The mmap syscall usually maps a file into the address space. With the MAP_ANONYMOUS flag there is no backing file, and the kernel creates a zero-filled, page-aligned mapping anywhere in the address space. Like the heap, its resident pages count towards the anonymous RSS. The munmap syscall returns a block with its own mapping to the kernel as soon as it is freed, whatever lies next to it.

To integrate this into our own allocator, my_malloc chooses between sbrk and mmap by the size of the request. Any request of 128 KiB or more, header included, gets a mapping of its own from map_chunk, rounded up to whole pages; smaller requests take the existing sbrk free list path.

For mmapped chunks, we use one of the three flags in the size header (M) to record that this chunk has its own mapping. my_free checks this bit to know how to free it. It unmaps a mapped chunk with munmap, and puts any other chunk back on the free list, where trim can release it.

Running the previous demo again, we see that freeing the 1 MiB block drops the anonymous RSS while the 16-byte chunk is still live.

The following diagram shows how this might look in the address space.

Why not mmap everything? Some allocators do! Even glibc creates extra heaps, called arenas, with mmap, and spreads threads across them so that they don’t all contend for the lock on the main heap. However, separate mappings have separate regions with no guarantee of contiguity, and chunks can’t be coalesced across these gaps. On the other hand, allocating with sbrk just bumps up the one contiguous region that you already have. A freed chunk that stays on the heap keeps its pages resident, so reusing it causes no page faults. Each new mapping starts with no physical memory behind it, and faults on every page it writes.

We compare the performance of our allocators against glibc’s malloc with a workload that repeatedly allocates and deallocates blocks of varying sizes1. To measure the speed, we find the average time of a step: free a random live block, allocate a replacement of random size, and write to each of its pages. At the end of the run, the live blocks hold 10.3 MiB. The last two columns give the anonymous RSS at that point, and again after every block is freed, which is the memory the allocator keeps.

my-bump-1 never frees or reuses memory, so its RSS is the 1425 MiB that the run allocates in total. Its 1.72 µs per step comes from an sbrk call for every request and page faults on new pages. my-freelist-2 reuses memory, so its RSS of 17.8 MiB is much closer to the 10.3 MiB of live data. However, each step walks the list twice: my_malloc walks it to find a fit, and insert_free walks it to find the chunk’s neighbours. my-trim-3 adds a third walk, because trim looks for the last free chunk on every my_free. my-freelist-2 and my-trim-3 run 65 and 142 times slower than glibc respectively. In return, my-trim-3 keeps only 0.188 MiB once every block is freed. my-mmap-4 calls mmap for large requests, which raises its time per step to 11.8 µs but gives it the lowest RSS with blocks live, at 11.2 MiB.

Why is glibc so fast? The slowdowns in our allocators are due to having to walk a long free list of chunks multiple times. glibc instead keeps free chunks in bins, separate lists by size, so a request can walk a list of appropriately sized chunks directly. It also keeps up to seven freed chunks of each small size in a thread-local cache (tcache) and hands them straight back to the next request of that size.

The tcache also explains the last column, where glibc keeps 19.0 MiB after every block is freed. The heap counts a cached chunk as in use, so one near the top stops glibc from lowering the break. The GLIBC_TUNABLES environment variable can turn the tcache off with GLIBC_TUNABLES=glibc.malloc.tcache_count=0, or fix the mmap threshold at 128 KiB with GLIBC_TUNABLES=glibc.malloc.mmap_threshold=131072.

With the tcache off, freeing everything leaves 0.250 MiB, and each step takes 1.5 times as long as before.

glibc makes 51 mmap calls in the whole run, against the 5040 that my-mmap-4 makes. Freeing a mapped chunk raises glibc’s threshold to that chunk’s size, so later large requests come from the heap. With the threshold fixed at 128 KiB, every large request pays for its syscalls and page faults again, and glibc runs 10.7 times slower.

malloc spends memory to stay away from the kernel, parcelling out large blocks in userspace so that most calls never make a syscall.

The benchmark keeps 4000 blocks live, and each step frees a random block and allocates a replacement of a random size. Of the requests, 90% are 16 to 512 bytes, 9% are up to 16 KiB, and 1% are 128 KiB to 256 KiB. Each new block gets one write per page. An allocator runs 500,000 steps in a fresh process, built at -O2. Run on an Intel Xeon E3-1230 v3. ↩︎

Sony Indefinitely Delays Another PS5 Peripheral Days Before Release

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Sony has indefinitely delayed the PS5’s official Pulse Elevate desktop speakers, which were originally due to deploy days from now, on 12th November.

In a brief update on the PS Blog, the manufacturer said that it had “identified a production issue with the product and have made the decision to delay the launch”.

This delay feels eerily similar to the one that affected its FlexStrike arcade stick earlier in the year, which was originally due out on 6th August but ultimately indefinitely postponed.

At the time, the manufacturer said it had encountered “unexpected production delays”, but that was several months ago now and it hasn’t provided an update since.

Sony’s Edwin Foo said of the Pulse Elevate delay:

“The additional time will allow us to ensure the product will deliver the experience we intended for our players. We apologise for the delay and appreciate your patience and understanding. We look forward to bringing Pulse Elevate to players soon.”

The speakers – which are designed for both desktop and portable use – promise “lifelike, ultra-low latency” audio.

What’s particularly bizarre about this delay is that many outlets and influencers received review units earlier this week.

I think I’m allowed to say Push Square received a pair, and SpawnPoiint posted about them on Thursday:

I wonder what went wrong during production, and whether it’s going to have to scrap its entire inventory and start over from scratch on these.

Democrats plan to push clean energy to lower bills and challenge Trump’s fossil fuel agenda

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Democrats can begin investigations and hold hearings into climate policy rollbacks and elimination of green energy grants by the Republican administration, potentially trying to undo some changes and restore renewable energy incentives.

Some Democrats are promising to push for more clean energy to help tackle rising energy costs when they take control of Congress in January, though President Donald Trump says he will use his veto power aggressively.

With new majorities in the House and Senate after last week’s midterms, Democrats can set an agenda boosting clean energy and addressing climate change. While those are traditionally core priorities of the party, climate was not a central topic in this election cycle.

Democrats can begin investigations and hold hearings into climate policy rollbacks and elimination of green energy grants by the Republican administration, potentially trying to undo some changes and restore renewable energy incentives.

Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse of Rhode Island, the top Democrat on the Senate Committee on Environment and Public Works, said Trump’s clean energy attacks drove up prices for taxpayers. He said Democrats will “work every angle to lower consumers’ energy costs, and we will demand accountability for the massive corruption wrought by Trump’s fossil-fuel donors.”

American Clean Power Association CEO Jason Grumet said everybody who is trying to build clean energy “feels bolstered.”

Enacting major changes would require Republican support because Democrats will lack the numbers to override presidential vetoes. Even then, lower energy bills are not guaranteed because prices also depend on fuel costs, demand and the expense of maintaining the electric grid.

“The wins give Democrats more leverage over spending and more political power to advance a coherent agenda; the question is whether they’ll use that power effectively,” said Lisa Sachs, director of the Columbia Center on Sustainable Investment. “The vote was for affordability and opportunity. Democrats now need to be clear about which policies deliver them.”

Trump said after Tuesday’s election that Democrats will send over “things that are insane” and he will veto them, speaking generally about legislation. But in the comments to Fox News, he predicted that “after a couple of months of vetoes,” he and Democrats will find ways to work together.

The White House said in a statement Wednesday that Trump is “committed to unleashing reliable, affordable, and secure energy sources to lower prices, strengthen our grid, and protect our national security, and is willing to work with anyone to accomplish this.”

Trump promised to lower energy prices. Instead, his war with Iran and other policies increased the price of gasoline, diesel and electricity. At the same time, the administration canceled many clean energy projects and grants and slowed clean energy permitting.

Trump particularly dislikes offshore wind. Developer Orsted said Thursday that Revolution Wind, a large wind farm in federal waters off Rhode Island, has started operating commercially.

Will the president change his tune on green energy? That’s doubtful, said former Sen. Byron Dorgan, D-N.D., co-chair of the government relations practice at ArentFox Schiff.

“He is likely to keep pushing his energy issues, and I think he will be forced to give some room for a new Congress to recreate a different strategy that will add clean energy,” Dorgan said in an email.

Trump promotes expanding production of oil, natural gas and coal, which cause climate change, because he argues that would give the U.S. the lowest-cost electricity. Mike Sommers, CEO of the American Petroleum Institute, the oil and gas industry’s top lobbying group, said that as voters have changed the balance of political power in Washington, “the affordability message is unmistakable.”

Sommers said in a statement there is a real opportunity for bipartisan cooperation to reduce costs and strengthen the economy, starting with an energy permitting reform proposal announced in September. The legislation is aimed at shortening the timeline to get big energy projects started.

Ife Kilimanjaro, executive director of the U.S. Climate Action Network, said Democrats should reject that proposal and use their stronger position to pass legislation that actually builds out much-needed clean energy.

The administration has reshaped the Environmental Protection Agency, reversing pollution limits as it promotes fossil fuels.

Clear Street energy transition analyst Tim Moore said he expects Democrats to investigate the climate and environmental rollbacks, in an effort to block some from taking effect. Democrats could also try amending the Clean Air Act to cut carbon pollution and push back against EPA budget cuts, he added.

Rep. Sean Casten, D-Ill., said he is cautiously optimistic some Republicans will now seek to differentiate themselves from Trump and back pro-environmental policies. But without Republican support, Democrats’ more ambitious bills will largely lay out their agenda for 2028, he added.

Climate is not necessarily at the top of Democrats’ list of priorities. They are targeting healthcare access, trade tariffs and the Iran war.

Rep. Frank Pallone, the New Jersey Democrat who is expected to become chair of the House Energy and Commerce Committee, said the party should focus on healthcare, affordability, consumer and environmental protections, clean energy and regulating Big Tech.

David Victor, a professor of innovation and public policy at the University of California, San Diego, largely expects a stalemate at the federal level on climate and energy policy because of the divided government. He said agreement could come on some bipartisan measures such as a permitting overhaul.

Victor thinks Democratic-led investigations and hearings will center on corruption and other topics that may only touch on energy and climate. Like Casten, Victor said many of the Democratic bills likely will not become law, but will outline what Democrats could accomplish if they win the White House in 2028.

“The Democrats are going to be drafting things that the administration’s not going to like. They don’t have the votes to override a veto, and on top of it, the administration is going to be in turmoil because of its own electoral failures and because of all the investigations,” Victor said. “This seems to me, at the federal level, largely a recipe for kind of intense chaos over the next two years.”

One of the biggest wild cards is the behavior of the administration, which could use executive power to double down on rollbacks of climate and environmental rules, he said.

Democrats scored victories in races for governor in states long controlled by Republicans, including Texas and Georgia.

Leah Stokes, a political scientist at the UC Santa Barbara, said there could be a lot of state action on climate next year because of those wins. That is where climate leadership has traditionally come from, she noted. At the federal level, Stokes is optimistic about extending clean energy tax credits since some Republicans support that.

Jay Inslee, a former Democratic governor and congressman from Washington, also expects growing momentum for clean energy in states that elected Democratic governors, particularly in states where they also won legislative majorities.

“This is a really powerful instrument for the United States to join the rest of the world and get back in a leadership position, even if we still have a climate denier that might exercise a veto on some things that we want to do federally,” Inslee said. “So I hope people will pay attention to the big progress we’re going to make on a state-level basis.“By JENNIFER McDERMOTT Associated Press

Georgia is back at No. 1 in AP Top 25 ahead of Utah and Notre Dame; Ohio State drops to No. 21

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

UGA turned in one of the most dominant performances of the season.

Georgia was voted No. 1 in The Associated Press Top 25 for the second time this season Sunday, unbeaten UCLA matched its highest ranking in 25 years and Ohio State’s free fall continued after a wild weekend that followed the first College Football Playoff rankings of the year.

Miami became the fourth No. 1 team to get knocked out of the top spot, the most in a single season since 2008. Only one team â No. 6 Indiana, which was idle â remained in the same spot it had a week ago in the Top 25.

Georgia turned in one of the most dominant performances of the season in a 42-7 victory at Mississippi on Saturday. The Bulldogs had been No. 1 for one week when they were upset by Auburn on Oct. 17 but never dropped out of the top five, and they were the voters’ choice for No. 1 by a wide margin over Utah and Notre Dame.

Utah moved up one spot to No. 2 after its 28-20 win over rival BYU and the Utes now have their highest-ever AP ranking in the regular season. The Fighting Irish’s 31-24 win over Miami pushed them up one rung to No. 3, while the Hurricanes fell to No. 5 after their first loss.

Georgia earned 53 of the 70 first-place votes from the media voting panel. Utah received 12 first-place votes and Notre Dame got five.

UCLA is 9-0 for the first time since 1998 and moved up to No. 4. It’s the Bruins’ highest ranking since their 2001 team was No. 4 following a 6-0 start. After Miami, Indiana, Alabama, Texas, Tennessee and Texas Tech completed the top 10.

Alabama and Texas both were rewarded for road wins and moved up two spots. The Crimson Tide won at LSU and the Longhorns at Missouri.

Tennessee jumped from No. 16 to No. 9, its highest ranking in two years, after an impressive 34-6 win over a top-10 Kentucky. Texas Tech returned to the top 10 after outscoring West Virginia 53-48.

Ohio State, which was No. 2 two weeks ago, went from No. 11 after its loss at Southern California to No. 21 this week after a 41-28 home loss to Oregon. The Buckeyes hadn’t been ranked so low, excluding the 2020 pandemic season, since 2014.

Kyle Larson wins 2nd consecutive Cup Series title, becomes the 11th NASCAR driver with 3

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Larson was the overwhelming favorite to hoist the Bill France Cup in Homestead long before the green flag dropped.

HOMESTEAD, Fla. (AP) â Kyle Larson is now a three-time NASCAR champion.

Larson clinched his third Cup Series title in six years early in the 400-mile season finale at Homestead-Miami Speedway on Sunday. He became the first back-to-back champion since Jimmie Johnson in 2010; Johnson won five in a row beginning in 2006.

The 34-year-old Larson also became the 11th driver in NASCAR history with three Cup titles, joining a list that includes seven-time champs Richard Petty, Dale Earnhardt and Johnson. Jeff Gordon has four. Lee Petty, David Pearson, Cale Yarborough.

Darrell Waltrip, Tony Stewart and Joey Logano have three.

Larson was the overwhelming favorite to hoist the Bill France Cup in Homestead long before the green flag dropped.

He entered the weekend with a 67-point lead over Christopher Bell and needed only nine points to wrap up the title. He got one point for starting the race and seven more by finishing fourth in the opening stage. But because Bell didn’t win the stage, he had no path to catch Larson the rest of the day.

Larson responded to crew chief Cliff Daniels dropping the news over the radio by saying, “That was anticlimactic.”

Larson put himself in position for his latest title with a historic postseason, dubbed NASCAR’s Chase for the championship. He ended a 51-race winless skid by winning three of the previous nine playoff events and finished in the top 10 in eight of them.

He enjoyed a similar finish while winning it all in 2021, his first season at Hendrick. He won four of the last five races â including the finale at Phoenix Raceway â while still dealing with questions about how his time ended at Chip Ganassi Racing.

His second title came in stunning fashion last year at Phoenix. He took advantage of a late caution â his crew put on two new tires instead of four â and leaped sentimental favorite Denny Hamlin on pit road with two laps to go. Hamlin didn’t have enough time to catch Larson.

Larson’s third title was far less shocking. Securing the title was so much of a slam dunk that Larson was the only Cup driver invited to NASCAR’s championship media day Thursday, leading to a parade event that Larson called awkward.

The biggest question was how Larson and his No. 5 Hendrick Motorsports team would handle the pressure. Larson put that to rest during qualifying Saturday.

“They were debating on if a hot dog is a sandwich and if cereal is a soup,” Larson said. “So I would say they were pretty relaxed. I was surprised by all that.”

A better question: How does Larson compare to the best drivers in motorsports history?

Parnelli Jones, Mario Andretti, A.J. Foyt and Tony Stewart are widely regarded as the best of the best, guys who proved their worth by winning across varying racing series.

Jones won in sprint cars and stock cars around the same time he won the 1963 Indianapolis 500. Andretti won four IndyCar titles, the 1967 Daytona 500 and the 1969 Indianapolis 500. Foyt won four Indy 500s and the 1972 Daytona 500. Stewart won the 1997 IndyCar championship, three Cup Series titles and is currently racing dragsters at the top level, the NHRA.

Larson raced twice at the Indy 500 and has won the Rolex 24 at Daytona, three Chili Bowls and four Knoxville Nationals. And now he’s in elite company with three Cup titles.

“He wins in everything he does, and they’re all completely different cars and tracks,” retired driver and current NBC Sports race analyst Jeff Burton said. “And he’s racing against the best in every discipline.“By MARK LONG AP Sports Writer

Nvidia investigating reports that CEO Jensen Huang was targeted by Chinese intelligence

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The announcement came after Huang acknowledged to the board that he had been intimate with a business executive whom U.S. intelligence determined was gathering information for Beijing.

SAN FRANCISCO (AP) â Nvidia is opening an investigation into reports that CEO Jensen Huang was the “target of a foreign intelligence effort,” the company’s board of directors said Sunday.

The announcement came after Huang acknowledged to the board that he had been intimate with a business executive whom U.S. intelligence determined was gathering information for Beijing, The New York Times reported.

Huang apologized in a message to the company, according to the Times. “I was unfaithful to my wife,” he said. “My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did. I am deeply sorry.”

Nvidia’s board said in a statement it has “no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised,” but that its inquiry would examine all available information. Huang “fully supports the review and has committed to cooperating with it,” the board said.

Huang, 63, will remain CEO during the investigation.

The Times reported last month that U.S. intelligence agencies had discovered that Huang had developed a close relationship with Alice Huang, a Chinese citizen and chip buyer, and determined that she was feeding information directly to Beijing. The two are not related, according to the Times.

Alice Huang was previously a television reporter in China before becoming a top executive at Singapore-based data center company Megaspeed, which has become one of Nvidia’s biggest customers in Southeast Asia. Megaspeed bought more than $4.8 billion worth of Nvidia chips over the past two years, according to the Times.

Google Messages homepage redesign: Pinned chat avatars, bottom search, corner Gemini

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

In addition to the new app icon, Google Messages for Android is testing a big homepage redesign that has three core components.

Update 11/7: Instead of pinned chats just appearing at the top of the list, Google Messages is trying a redesign that significantly elevates them above all conversations.

This redesign takes advantage of the existing background layer that’s currently just home to the logo/wordmark, search, and account menu.

You’ll see large contact photos appear above all chats, which pushes the list of other conversations down the page. Like iMessage on iOS, this layout has the downside of not showing any history or last sent information.

Meanwhile, Google is testing another change where search becomes a centered floating action button (FAB) alongside new chat. When you’re scrolling down the page, you’ll see “Search” in a pill-shaped button, with the other button minimized.

Hate the idea of just the pinned "bobble head" look. I like being able to view message tidbits. Plus, it's directly copying Apple (enough said). I have to use an iPhone at my job. I hate the pinned bubble conversation look and more importantly it's function.

Otherwise, you’ll see a circular magnifying glass icon and “Start chat” in the pill. The animation that accompanies this change looks nice and feels alive, while boosting one-handed usability.

Google has replaced the Gemini FAB with a button in the top-right corner next to your profile image. This matches the design leveraged by Workspace apps on desktop and mobile.

These updates are currently in the beta channel and not yet widely rolled out to all testers. Some users have all aspects of the redesign, but it appears that Google is rolling out the various components in a piecemeal fashion.

Crypto’s Next Two Years Should Be About Survival, Not Competition: Vitalik Buterin

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin has warned crypto executives to switch focus to collective security and survival rather than competition between platforms. This follows multiple hacks that drained billions from the market and crushed traders’ sentiment.

In an interview with MoneyControl, Buterin urged crypto executives to focus on protecting user assets as a collective, as that could shape how regulators approach the sector. The next couple of years are key, as frameworks are still expected in several jurisdictions.

Digital asset firms, particularly centralized exchanges, have focused on gaining a larger share of the market cap, onboarding more users, etc but this buzz around gains leaves room for bad actors. For Buterin, the actions in the next couple of years will determine the long-term roadmap.

Most crypto users agreed with the Ethereum executive because, without asset safety, centralized exchanges will see capital exodus and more backing from traditional finance firms.

Recently, several crypto companies were hacked, including Binance, the largest centralized exchange by trading volume. Bad actors flagged in North Korea drained $6 billion; although large parts were recovered, it still remains the largest crypto hack of all time.

This week, Brazil’s largest exchange, Mercado, also suffered a breach that temporarily suspended trading. In a nutshell, these losses could prompt regulators to tighten requirements, setting the sector back.

Previously, regulators in the US and Europe often cited investor-protection concerns to delay major developments that would open the sector to traditional capital. Notably, spot crypto ETFs are a top example, although things changed after the White House backed the move following President Trump’s election win.

Buterin also added that recent attacks now target user interfaces and centralized components rather than smart contracts. He called for more operations to move on-chain, citing Binance Smart Chain and others.

“One good decision that Binance made is that they also launched Binance Smart Chain. They also benefit from the fact that if you do things with contracts on-chain, you can have very high levels of security. I am expecting we’re going to see more moves in that direction.”

On Ethereum security, Buterin and developers rolled out a roadmap to protect assets ahead of Q-Day. The network is lauded for taking early steps ahead of others as supercomputers get more powerful.

Echoes of Mirror's Edge, Dishonored and a dream set in Italy I once had can be found in Boars in Rome, an expressive parkour platformer set in dystopian Rome

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Years ago, I had this dream where I was parkouring through a greatly sunken version of Venice, no doubt sourced from the many hours I put into Assassin's Creed 2 as a teen who should have been studying for their GCSEs a lot more than they were. It's stuck with me after all this time mostly because of how much I wanted to capture that feeling again somehow, as the real Assassin's Creed didn't quite cut it. And lo and behold, there I was today browsing Bluesky when I came across Boars in Rome, a Mirror's Edge and Counter Strike Surf inspired parkour platformer set in a dystopian version of Rome.

Truthfully, witnessing the clip I've shared below was baffling to me. "This is what I dreamt of!" More or less, anyway, for the setting isn't quite the same; "You have been selected to start a new life off planet. Earth has been abandoned," explains the game's Steam page. "Machines keep working and nature claims what remains. Find out what happened while you were frozen in time by exploring the remnants of Rome where historical monuments mix with futurist megastructures."

Mirrors Edge x Counter Strike Surf where you parkour on anything! Be smooth n stylish in dystopian Rome. Or just jump around and explore, whatever works for you. It's called Boars in Rome! #PitchYaGame[image or embed]

Not all of it matches that dream of mine, some of it leans much more into brutalism than the typical, much older architecture you'd expect of Rome, but it's there nonetheless. In motion, Boars in Rome feels quite oneiric too. There is so much more control over the game's avatar than what my real body is capable of, or wants to do. You can run, jump, slide and wallrun, positioning it quite closely to VHOLUME mechanically. Where it differs is in its additives, movement tech like being able to swing on lampposts or vault over low down obstacles.

VHOLUME is more of a game about providing you with a simple toolkit and leaving you to push it to its maximum. Boars in Roam is a touch more expressive, and I'd dare to even call it an immersive sim. The levels you can romp around are linear, you have an end point to reach, only the journey is up to you. It certainly feels appropriate to put it in the im sim camp given that, at least the game's first level, calls to mind Viktor Antonov's work on Dishonored (and Half-Life 2, too, honestly).

As it currently stands, there's no release date for Boars in Rome, but there is a playtest you can sign up and gain immediate access to if you fancy a go yourself; you can do so on Steam right here, or at least pop it a wishlist.
