Chromagraph Camera is exactly the camera app I’ve been looking for on iPhone

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Last week, movie director Rian Johnson announced a surprise new camera app for iPhone that reimagines what a smartphone photo should look like. Many people would agree that Apple’s iPhone photos are a tad bit overprocessed.

iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone Duo introduced a new film texture option, but in my view, it’s a bit too exaggerated. Chromagraph Camera, on the other hand, feels pretty perfect.

Attempts have been made to improve iPhone photos for years, but they all feel a little bit too heavy, in my view. Most camera apps for iPhone require you to dial settings in. While that can be appropriate for certain settings, I ultimately want my iPhone to just be a point and shoot experience. If I wanted the perfect photo, I’d be using my camera.

That said, iPhone photos just don’t look super excellent out of the box. It’s not a limitation of hardware. If you take a RAW iPhone photo and edit it yourself, you can actually get surprisingly good results. Apple’s camera app just has poor defaults.

When Apple introduced the film grain feature on iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone Duo, I was actually quite interested in it. It was certainly my favorite camera feature of the phone when I briefly tried out the iPhone 18 Pro. For some reason though, the film look just feels a bit exaggerated on iPhone Duo.

Apps like Adobe’s Project Indigo have gotten really close in terms of look, but the fact that there’s so much time spent post-processing means it’s often inconvenient. I’ve also had a few instances of losing photos because the app closed in the background while it was still processing the photo.

User reviews tell a different story. No trial option is a non- starter for me.

When Chromagraph Camera was announced, I was quite interested. I paid the $12.99, and I’ve been happy with the results. I tweeted some of my sample photos when I just got the app, and it’s been well worth it.

$12.99 may be a higher starting price, but if you really want an iPhone camera app that just takes good looking photos without a lot of hassle, it works really well.

Fessés à Guingamp la semaine passée, les Verts ont su rebondir face à la lanterne rouge lavalloise (2-0).

Ian Cathro avait choisi de laisser Sohaib Naïr, en difficulté au Roudourou, sur le banc au profit de Kévin Pedro. Joao Ferreira reprenait le couloir droit. Pour le reste, le coach écossais revenait à son attaque Breum - Boakye - Ballo - Cardona.

Les Verts attaquaient tambour battant. Boakye puis Ferreira testaient le gardien adverse. Malgré la possession du ballon et quelques situations, les occasions franches étaient rares. Quelques minutes plus tard, c'est en transition que les Verts allaient frapper. Le Danois décalait Cardona qui ajustait le gardien lavallois (1-0, 45+1)

Les Verts avaient fait le plus dur au meilleur moment et allaient enfoncer le clou en début de deuxième période. En inversant les rôles : Cardona à la passe en centrant, Breum à la conclusion de la tête seul face au but à 3 mètres (2-0, 50e)

A partir de là, les Verts se contentèrent de gérer. Par deux fois, ils eurent l'occasion de tuer le match mais Davitashvili perdait son duel en frappant à côté du but et El Jamali se trouait de façon incompréhensible dans les 6 mètres alors qu'il n'avait plus qu'à conclure.

Avoid these 5 common open enrollment pitfalls

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Whether you’re among the 53.5% of Americans who get their health insurance through work, the 20.1% enrolled in Medicare, or the 10.5% purchasing ACA coverage, mid-October or early November typically marks the start of the open enrollment period for next year’s insurance coverage.

Life would be easier if your health insurance coverage stayed the same if you liked it or adapted to your life automatically if you didn’t. Unfortunately, we mere mortals have to make decisions about our upcoming healthcare plan each year, in part because employers and other providers make regular changes to their insurance offerings—in addition to any legal changes that might affect federal benefits and ACA coverage.

The good news is that employers, Medicare providers, and the ACA marketplace typically time their open enrollment period to coincide, giving you November to make decisions about next year’s benefits.

But just because you know when to expect open enrollment season—who can miss all the open enrollment carols on the radio?—doesn’t mean you’re prepared for the important decisions you need to make. In fact, there are several common mistakes that could cost you big bucks in 2027 if you don’t know how to avoid them.

Here’s what you need to know about the most common open enrollment pitfalls.

The pitfall: missing a deadline

We all know how fast the fourth quarter tends to go. One minute you’re passing out candy to adorable Spidermen/Gwens and the next you’re eating Christmas cookies while making resolutions for 2027. The open enrollment deadline can get lost in the holiday shuffle.

For reference, these are the important deadlines for enrolling in a health insurance program during open enrollment:

  • ACA Marketplace has two enrollment deadlines:
    • December 15, 2026 for coverage beginning January 1, 2027
    • January 15, 2027 for coverage beginning February 1, 2027
  • Medicare: Enroll by December 7, 2026; coverage changes take effect January 1, 2027
  • Employer-sponsored health insurance coverage: Your deadline will vary depending on your employer. Your HR department will have the necessary information.

There are two possible problems you may face after missing an open enrollment deadline:

  1. You might have to go without insurance. If you miss the ACA open enrollment period, you can’t get insurance through the health insurance marketplace this year—unless you qualify for a special enrollment period. To qualify, you’d need to have gotten married or divorced, had a baby, moved, or lost your previous healthcare coverage.
  2. You might be reenrolled in the same plan. Medicare beneficiaries who miss the open enrollment deadline are typically reenrolled in the same plan if it’s still offered. But it may not be the best option for your needs.

Workers with employer-sponsored insurance plans may still be able to enroll after missing a deadline, depending on how draconian the HR department is—but it’s not a good idea to count on it.

The pitfall: procrastination

As busy as this time of year can be, it’s rare that we miss an open enrollment deadline because it slips our minds. Instead, it’s much more likely that we procrastinate until the last minute. Humans tend to put off decision-making that feels overwhelming, confusing, or deeply boring—which is precisely the kind of choices you need to make during open enrollment.

Luckily, we know how to beat procrastination. If you know you have open enrollment decisions to make but have been putting them off, follow these steps to keep from procrastinating yourself sick:

  1. Make it an appointment on your calendar. Set a specific time to deal with open enrollment paperwork. Schedule it in your calendar the same way you would a work meeting or doctor’s appointment.
  2. Gather necessary information beforehand. After you make your “open enrollment appointment” on your calendar, generate a list of the things you think you’ll need to make decisions. Start gathering this information a little at a time. Some of the data you might need includes the names, birthdates, and Social Security numbers of every member of your household, pay stubs or tax returns to show your household income, how much you paid out of pocket in 2026, and your preferred healthcare providers and medications.
  3. Spend no more than an hour on open enrollment. The reason we avoid tasks like open enrollment is that our brains convince us they’ll take forever. So give yourself permission to work for only 60 minutes. Stop at the end of the hour, even if you’re not completely done. Just schedule the next appointment before you quit for the day.
  4. Don’t let perfectionism thwart your decisions: Part of what makes open enrollment nerve-racking is the fact that it’s impossible to know ahead of time if you’ve made the best decisions for next year’s healthcare needs. Trying to find the perfect plan option that will maximize your healthcare coverage while minimizing your out-of-pocket costs can drive you nuts, since a year of robust health may follow a year of multiple ER visits. Instead of aiming for a perfect plan, base your decision on what’s probable for 2027.

The pitfall: making assumptions

We often try to reduce the complexity of tough decisions by making assumptions. Unfortunately, open enrollment is not a time when assumptions are your friends. It’s very common for healthcare plans to change their coverage and options from one year to the next, even when the plan itself keeps the same name or offered coverage level.

Here are some of the common changes that could catch you flat-footed if you’re not looking for them:

  • A revised formulary: Your plan may no longer cover the medication you’ve been taking for years—or it may start covering a medication that you’ve previously had to pay for out-of-pocket.
  • Increased annual deductible: Next year, you may have to pay more out of pocket before your coverage kicks in.
  • Revised coinsurance amount: Coinsurance is the percentage of the cost of a covered service that you’re responsible for paying, after meeting the annual deductible. Don’t assume that the coinsurance amount will stay the same from year to year.
  • Increased premium amounts for dependents: The monthly premium required to cover your spouse and children can often go up from one year to the next.
  • Increased out-of-pocket maximum: The maximum amount you might pay during 2027 may be higher than the limit set for 2026.

The pitfall: choosing based on price

Expecting a current plan will stay the same is not the only way assumptions could steer you wrong. It’s also easy to make assumptions about how well a plan will fit you based on cost.

For example, rather than compare options one by one, you might decide to enroll in the most expensive plan, assuming the higher cost means it’s better coverage. But the best health insurance plan for your needs depends on your specific situation. Springing for the high-premium/higher-coverage plan may be a waste of money for a marathon runner who sees the doctor only for an annual checkup. (Unless they tear an ACL, of course . . .)

But you might make a similar mistake in the other direction by signing up for the plan with the lowest premiums. It’s easy to assume lower monthly premiums will cost you less overall if you don’t consider the other costs you’ll have to pay. But lower premiums typically means lower coverage levels.

This price catch-22 can contribute to your procrastination, since it may feel like you’re going to overpay no matter what. (As a cynical veteran of open enrollment, I can honestly say that feeling is correct. You are probably going to pay more than you could have if you’d had a crystal ball.)

However, there are some questions you can ask yourself to help reduce the likelihood of making a decision-by-price mistake during open enrollment:

  1. Did my 2026 plan cover my healthcare needs? This gives you a baseline for determining if a new plan will suit your situation.
  2. How much did I spend out of pocket this year? If you spent more than you expected or more than you budgeted for, that can help you identify where you need more robust coverage.
  3. What confused me about my current coverage? Now’s an excellent time to clear up that confusion by contacting your HR representative, the ACA helpline at 1-800-318-2596, or Medicare at 1-800-MEDICARE (1-800-633-4227).

The pitfall: not checking your work

A jaw-dropping 90% of employers have open enrollment errors. These errors may include relatively minor problems like billing mistakes, as well as more major issues like eligible employees not getting enrolled despite opting in—leaving them without coverage.

Before you turn in your enrollment paperwork (or, more likely, hit the SUBMIT button), go back through the entire process to double-check that all the information is correct. Then, when you receive the documents, read through them for accuracy, as well. It’s much easier to fix a clerical error during open enrollment than after the new plan year begins.

Set yourself up for a healthy 2027

Open enrollment may rival filing taxes for the worst annual adulting requirement—but taking some time to make well-thought-out decisions for next year’s health insurance plans will always be something you’re glad you did.

5 tips for getting the most out of AI

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Why do some people seem to be getting a lot out of AI, while others—maybe you—still feel like they’re spinning their wheels trying to squeeze anything remotely useful out of these platforms? The answer is multifaceted, of course.

Some workflows are better suited to the strengths of large language models, while others are more vulnerable to their weaknesses. Some users pay for upgraded plans, which can improve how well the models work.

But a big part of the difference comes down to how you use artificial intelligence. Like any tool, there are ways to make it perform better for you. In other words, the technology is often what you make of it.

If you offload your thinking to AI platforms, you risk dulling your ability to approach problems creatively and becoming dependent on a flawed technological crutch. But if you use AI to challenge yourself, stress-test your work, and outsource routine tasks, you might feel, at least in some sense, like you’ve gotten a significant upgrade.

Consider the calculator: Some people have become so reliant on it that their mental math skills suffer, while others use the technology to tackle more advanced math because they’re not spending their time on simpler problems.

Like all new technologies, what you choose to outsource to AI is up to you. Still, if you do decide to use it, it’s worth considering how to get the most out of the technology—and make sure chatbots serve you, rather than the other way around. Here are a few tips to make these platforms work better for you.

Be smart about your prompts

What you type into your AI actually matters. Take traditional search engines. You may not realize it, but over time, you might have developed an implicit sense of how using certain Boolean operators or more specific proper nouns can yield better, more relevant results to your queries.

The same is true for AI. Being more specific with a chatbot about what information you want can make a big difference, particularly when you’re using the technology as a search tool. It might be easy to slip into thinking that AI should already know what you want, but it doesn’t. Instead, treat a chatbot as an earnest research assistant with the capacity to make a lot of mistakes. There’s an old adage: “The more you know, the more they know.” The same logic applies to AI.

Here’s an example: If I’m shopping for a bookshelf, I no longer sift through countless listings searching for something that feels right. Instead, I take a picture of the spot where I need it to go, give the AI the dimensions (or range of dimensions) I’m looking for, and ask the chatbot to find the best options given those constraints. This saves me a lot of time and allows me to work smarter when using AI for shopping.

Encourage humility

One of the big problems with AI—or any work you don’t do yourself and instead outsource to someone (or something) else—is that it will make mistakes. Fear of an AI oopsie is anxiety-inducing and absolutely real, and one of the best reasons not to completely offload all your tasks to ChatGPT or Claude. Really, don’t do this!

The best approach is to check any work produced by a chatbot yourself. But there are also ways to help these tools work better. If you have the ability to adjust your AI’s settings, tell it to be more humble—and more cautious—when sharing information or making claims. Tell your AI to consider multiple ways of thinking about a question or problem, and to present divergent viewpoints. For kicks, tell it to admit when it’s unsure.

Also, always ask for direct citations and check them off the platform. When you send a prompt to your chatbot, tell it to avoid simply summarizing information and instead to provide direct links to sources so you can verify the claims yourself. Once you do this, you might be shocked by how often AI claims to be consulting a source or generates a fake web page only to apologize when you discover it doesn’t actually exist.

A polite reminder: Just because Claude says it’s “double-checking” something doesn’t mean it actually is. You need to double-check it, too! AI can cite unreliable sources and even hallucinate citations entirely. AI is powerful, but also unpredictable, and should never be treated as an objective source of information. Think of it as a sieve that helps you sift through information and find what you want faster, rather than something you can trust to identify the best results on its own.

Understand memory

Your AI is remembering what you ask it and what you tell it, even if you’re not always aware. This isn’t necessarily unique to AI systems; many tech platforms collect all sorts of data about you and then use that information to tailor content to you (usually, ads). It’s possible you don’t want AI to remember anything about you at all, in which case, check your privacy settings—or perhaps avoid AI entirely.

But if you are comfortable with a chatbot retaining information about you, consider that its memory can actually be a useful tool. For instance, if you’re using AI to draft emails, tell your chatbot to always introduce you in the way you prefer. If you tend to offer to jump on a call or Zoom, tell the AI that you like doing so—and ask it to remember that preference for when you draft emails in the future. If you prefer a more casual tone, tell it that. If you prefer to remain formal, tell it that instead.

Here’s another example: If you’d like your AI to avoid sending you links from Amazon when you’re online shopping—perhaps you don’t have an Amazon account—tell it to remember that preference so you don’t have to keep sifting through those links. Or maybe you’d prefer your AI to prioritize links to The New York Times because you already have a subscription. Tell your AI that, too.

Also, remember that you don’t need to give AI your personal information at all. For kicks, try asking it to erase things from its memory that you don’t want it to retain. There’s absolutely no guarantee that a request alone will erase the information completely, so check your privacy and memory settings, too. But why not give it a stab?

Customize how AI communicates

You spend a lot of time on Google, but you might now be pivoting to AI to surface information instead. One advantage of this is that you can have AI produce results in all sorts of formats and styles, since you dictate the terms in which it expresses itself. For instance, you might prefer to receive information in simple bullet points rather than a long, essay-like string of text. You can share that preference with your chatbot.

Maybe you prefer visuals and graphics? AI is great at whipping those up. For the nerds: Maybe you prefer receiving information in code that you can then copy and paste into a development environment, like Python, so you can run a script to check whatever work the AI did in a more deterministic way.

There are even simpler changes you could make. You might prefer that AI respond in two languages, perhaps because you already know one but are working on developing your skills in the other. Seeing the text side by side as you interact with the platform throughout the day might help boost your fluency.

Another example: I’ve instructed my AI to regularly incorporate more unusual, niche vocabulary. There’s a somewhat well-founded fear that AI is homogenizing our language and making us less creative in how we express ourselves. I told AI to reverse that trend and gave it a list of common AI crutch words to avoid.

Know your way around tokens

AI operates on tokens. You spend input tokens on the information you give a model, including your prompts and relevant context, and output tokens on what it produces in response. You may not always know how many tokens you’ve used, but writing long prompts generally means using more input tokens, while receiving long responses means using more output tokens.

As you employ chatbots and run up against usage limits, consider that you can guide your AI on how much detail to provide for a particular task. Tell it to be brief when you want a short response or just one link, and tell it when you want a really detailed answer.

This simple, ingenious design keeps drones from breaking when they crash

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

At first glance, FlexiQuad looks like a small drone. Just one more buzzing thingy doing its flying thing. Then you see it crashing against a tree. A regular drone would crash and fall to the ground, but this one bounces back from the tree trunk and happily keeps flying along.

The key to this trick is a hollow ring design crafted from flexible materials.

Today, almost every quadcopter uses four stiff arms bolted to a central body that holds its battery, brains, sensors, and sometimes other payloads. ETH Zürich researchers trashed that core and the arms, placing the rotors on a ring, with the batteries and the rest of the electronics spread evenly around the rim. The FlexiQuad’s middle is empty, like a vertically flat donut.

The ring is flexible. That’s its superpower. Hit a wall and it buckles, absorbing the force of the impact and bouncing back. As the structure stabilizes, it keeps itself in the air. 

Developed by engineer Luca Girardi and Stefano Mintchev, a former professor at ETH Zürich’s Environmental Robotics Lab, the drone takes its design inspiration from how the wings of birds and insects bend in some directions but not others. “We were interested in how stiff their wings are and how they bend them—or don’t bend them—in different directions,” Girardi said in a statement. 

Wings yield in some directions but can also hold rigid in others. Engineers call this “anisotropic stiffness,” the resistance of a material or a structure to deformation, which changes depending on the pushing force’s direction. 

Girardi has worked on the idea for about five years. “At first, I was skeptical whether the drone would fly without the central reinforcement,” he said in the statement. “But I really liked the approach. Then we tried it—and it worked.”

“The key was to rethink where the weight sits, as well as where the structure bends,” Girardi tells me. “A heavy central body supported by flexible arms can introduce bending and oscillations.”

Turning the arms of a regular drone from hard to flexible would make the drone difficult to control. Soft arms will bend under the vertical force of the rotors. Even if they were hard enough to allow for takeoff, the wobbling would make it very difficult to control during flight. Once they moved the batteries next to the motors, Girardi explains, the central core of a traditional drone wasn’t needed. “The hollow center follows from that arrangement,” he says.

FlexiQuad’s donut can flex sideways, where the force of a collision will push. At the same time, it stays rigid vertically, which allows the propellers to lift the entire body without any wobbling or sagging.

Bouncing off the walls

The drone’s material flexibility was also key to the design. If it were too soft, the propellers would smack into each other during hard maneuvers as the ring deforms. Too stiff and you’re back to a breakable drone.

The researchers found the best flexibility range overlapped nicely with the stiffness of real wings, which works all the way from tiny insects to big birds. The approach works for drones weighing less than an ounce to up to about 6.6 pounds.

The other part of the design is the distributed mass. Birds and insects spread energy through their bodies instead of a central location. Hanging the weight of the batteries under the rotors gives overall stability to the design, according to the team’s preprint paper on arXiv, which hasn’t been peer reviewed yet. In simulations, an otherwise identical version with one central battery shook 13 times more at the rotors and 8 times more at the electronics during acceleration.

The result of the research is a 14-ounce prototype that is roughly a thousand times bendier than a conventional quadcopter. The drone tops 50 mph and accelerates at more than three times the force of gravity.

“Soft doesn’t automatically mean slow,” Girardi said in his statement. It can also tip into a sharp sideways dodge in the blink of an eye, which makes it as maneuverable as a regular drone. 

According to Girardi and Mintchev’s research, the design is four times more collision resilient than that of a regular drone. When it folds to fit through a gap, the propellers overlap and thrust efficiency drops from 79% to 68%, but it keeps flying.

In tests, FlexiQuad took head-on hits at about 11 mph without sustaining damage. The designers also found another benefit to the flexible body: It can fold itself thin enough to slip through an opening 70% as wide as its normal width. In one test, it went through a 7-inch slot. 

Its squeezing abilities can double as a grip, too. In another test, the drone hugged a soft first-aid pouch, carried it, and set it down. In another, it lifted a payload heavier than itself. 

The drone’s flexibility allows for easy transport: You can open the ring and lay it flat in a medium-size suitcase. Putting it back together is simple thanks to its slide-in batteries and quick-release fasteners.

The FlexiQuad’s inventors say it will be inexpensive to make. The frame is four thin strips of fiberglass laminate (the cheap material circuit boards are made of), for a total raw cost of less than $15, not counting motors, electronics, and batteries. 

Hoops in tight places

There are still (solvable) problems that need fixing. The drone has to learn to perceive its surroundings and navigate on its own. Also, after a hit, the bobbling of its body blurs camera images. The designers say they’ve have already used reinforcement learning to give the FlexiQuad an AI reflex that lets it steady itself after a crash with motion sensors, then resume flying without any pilot intervention. The frame’s bending itself might become a sense for the drone, too, they point out, the way insects feel their wings flex.  

Yet the design is so simple and effective already that ETH has nominated the drone for the university’s own Spark Award for invention of the year. Girardi and Mintchev have also applied for a patent, which envisages the FlexiQuad flying into places where a drone can’t avoid touching things, citing everything from a ship’s cramped ballast tanks to collapsed buildings in search-and-rescue missions and dense forests where scientists take measurements. 

FlexiQuad still has to turn into a commercial reality. Girardi tells me the team is commercializing it through a startup that, rather than selling only FlexiQuad, automates rapid drone design and ties it tightly to suppliers. The prototype sits at technology-readiness level 5 on NASA’s nine-step scale—meaning it’s been validated in realistic conditions—and is working toward level 6. 

The team faces the usual barriers to take it to market, Girardi says, such as how to turn the prototype into a commercial product, scale manufacturing with a positive margin, and ensure customers will buy it at the resulting price. But the market potential is so big, it’s hard not to see the FlexiQuad arriving soon. 

One instant application, which can be put to the test in the war in Ukraine to snatch kamikaze and rogue drones. Girardi tells me that they are about to start a field pilot program using a larger version with a net in its hollow center to catch unauthorized drones in civilian settings, “where you can’t afford kinetic interception—otherwise the debris risks [falling] onto people or sensitive infrastructure.” 

Another application, thanks to its gripping abilities, could be search-and-rescue work, which the team is exploring with firefighters.

And of course, I can see one of these under every Christmas tree. An unbreakable flexible hoop that flies. You know, for kids.

Hershey is making the 2026 holiday season sweeter with a fresh lineup of new and returning treats

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

If you already miss Halloween candy, never fear. The end-of-year holiday season is sure to be sweeter than ever, thanks to Hershey.

Fast Company was privileged to be part of a recent sneak preview of Hershey’s new and returning products for 2026, hosted by the confectionery giant’s “manager of merriment and magic,” Matt Spencer; director of marketing Alyssa Smith-D’Cruz; and associate brand manager Carly Andrews.

Here’s a look at the numbers and some of the new and returning products.

Which season is the most lucrative for Hershey?

While Halloween candy does get the most sales, Hershey’s profits are also boosted by Easter, Valentine’s Day, and the holiday season. Summer months even pitch in, thanks to everyone’s favorite campfire treat—the s’more.

Last year, Hershey grew its holiday share, generating $609 million in revenue. The company expects 2026’s figures to be broadly in line with prior years.

Shares of the Hershey Co. (NYSE: HSY) are down 2.37% year to date as of Friday’s market close, compared with a rise of 7.56% for the broader S&P 500. Hershey says it recognizes the current challenging economic situation that many consumers are facing.

“While consumers are increasingly thoughtful about how they spend, treats remain an accessible way to celebrate seasonal traditions and create moments of joy,” senior marketing director Samantha Siegal told Fast Company. “That’s why we continue to offer choice across pack sizes, formats, and price points.”

What’s returning from Hershey for the 2026 season?

Reese’s lovers rejoice. Both Reese’s Peanut Butter Trees and Mini Trees are back. Peanut butter and milk chocolate are the perfect combination.

Hershey’s Kisses are back with the Snickerdoodle Cookie flavor, Kit Kats are back with Peppermint Stick, and the Standard Milk Chocolate Bar is Grinch-themed. Don’t worry, though—the green guy’s heart is so big these days that he won’t steal your candy.

What’s new at Hershey for the 2026 holiday season?

Speaking of the Grinch, there are a couple of new characters making their debut in 2026. Cindy-Lou Who’s Hershey Kisses taste just like a strawberry sugar cookie. Max, the Grinch’s faithful canine companion, also wanted in on the fun with his new Hershey’s Hug edition.

Meanwhile, another furry friend is getting in on the action. Reese’s Reindeer Mix is made up of milk chocolate-drizzled popcorn, Reese’s baking chips, Reese’s Mini Trees, and Reese’s Pieces.

Also straight from the North Pole comes SkinnyPop Elf Maple Syrup Kettle Corn. Buddy the Elf approves of this salty and sweet treat.

You might not immediately think of these next two offerings as holiday treats, but it’s time to go outside the box.

Jolly Rancher Freeze-Dried Pouches contain cherry and green-apple flavors. Cadbury might seem like an Easter treat, but its Caramello Miniatures are the perfect end-of-year bite-size fun.

These are just some of the wonderful treats coming to a store near you. To Hershey, candy season is all year long. You can check out the full lineup here.

The ‘charismatic’ leader trap: Why organizations shouldn’t depend on one individual, no matter how appealing

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

When we think of a leader, charisma—an individual’s ability to motivate people to follow them, share their vision, and ultimately do what they say—is often at the center of our image. Charismatic, transformational leaders are respected, admired, and even loved. A successful business, however, is about much more than one person, and the danger with an overreliance on an individual is that too much power accumulates at the top of the tree.

No one person has the answer to everything. Without other viewpoints critical thinking diminishes, staff feel powerless to share their views, and danger arises of the culture becoming cultlike rather than collaborative.

Share the load

Collective leadership, where the load is shared and decisions are made through team-based processes, is both an ethical and pragmatic approach. It removes the risk that egos will run unchecked, and supports team motivation and autonomy.

Staff who feel they have a voice and impact on business direction are more invested and creative. This approach also serves to support the leader—as the saying goes, “It’s lonely at the top.”

Good collective leadership looks like this.

  • Sharing decision-making: from fostering a general sense that employees are involved, to creating formal structures for making decisions.
  • Distributing leadership roles: entrusting decision-making to experts, rather than necessarily those in the most senior positions.
  • Building leadership capabilities in others: facilitating opportunities for others to lead and making people feel empowered and safe to do so.
  • Creating the conditions for good thinking: coordinating an environment of collaboration.
  • Rewarding collective action: attributing achievement to everyone who contributed to a positive outcome, not just those in charge.

This is not to suggest that hierarchy is completely removed. Without any top-down structure, informal leaders will emerge, based on their own charisma, performance, and social standing. While responsibility can and should be spread, a good leader must remain accountable.

A step on from collective leadership is regenerative leadership, where responsibility is extended outside of a business toward the ecological and social systems within which all organizations are embedded; i.e., taking steps to improve local environments and the well-being of both staff and the wider community.

For the good of us all, this is the kind of leadership we should aspire to. Like-minded leaders seek each other out, and badges of ethics, such as becoming B Corp certified or employee owned, can help oil the wheels of success, all while creating a better world for us all to live in.

What we’re describing here is a “relational leader,” someone who understands that leadership is not a solo act, but the task of trusting, collaborating, and encouraging human connection.

Make and break sense

The relational leader’s job is not to have all the answers; it is to create the conditions where a team can make meaning together. People are more likely to commit to a new direction when they have participated in making sense of it.

Sense-making is always a social process, built through conversations and relationships. Sense-giving is the practice of helping others understand what is happening, why it matters, and how to move forward. Sense-breaking can be the most challenging, disrupting an existing understanding that is blocking progress. For example, a major pivot in a market where your business has operated for years.

What’s key is that conversations around sense aren’t subjected to gatekeeping, allowing team members to accept change and make faster, better decisions. Waiting for certainty before communicating can leave people to fill in the gap with their own stories, normally with narratives that are worse than reality.

Encourage difference

In business, different viewpoints and life experiences can enhance operations. While it takes significantly more effort, time and intentional leadership to make a diverse team work well, a wider range of skills, knowledge, and opinions will increase collective capability.

In the relational leadership model, leaders do not simply transmit a message downward; they enable dialogue among conflicting perspectives, navigating ambiguity and creating space for different voices to be heard. All of this supports collective sense-making. The emphasis here is not merely participation but preserving difference.

Teams that embrace difference are more likely to innovate and better understand the needs of different clients. What’s crucial is that people feel psychologically safe to share ideas, challenge authority, and admit mistakes.

Hold space for tension

Making space for difference by encouraging multiple voices is one thing; holding space for the tensions that arise when those voices pull in different directions is something quite different. In a traditional leadership model, equilibrium must be restored as quickly as possible. Tensions are treated with defensiveness and tried and tested solutions are favored that do not challenge the status quo—or the leaders’ authority.

Relational leadership draws on the “paradox mindset,” where competing demands sit alongside each and are given space to exist, rather than shutting down differing viewpoints. With this approach, issues can remain open for discussion without blocking progress; parked for review when time and resources allow.

Potentially, a pathway can be found that honors multiple truths, allowing for greater agility with room for a range of perspectives. It is often these moments of constructive friction that create new thinking.

Leadership for the modern world

In today’s world, where complexity and change are not passing conditions but the permanent backdrop of organizational life, leadership is no longer simply about authority or position. Building relationships, giving people space to excel and be themselves, and taking on a range of viewpoints are the keys to lasting success.

AlmaLinux Shows Advantages of ABI Compatibility After RHEL Restricted Source Code Access

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The Fine Print: The following comments are owned by whoever posted them. We are not responsible for them in any way.

I am glad I moved my servers to AlmaLinux. It has been working out really well over the last few years. They were also fast to offer mitigation information and updated packages for each of the high-profile vulnerabilities this year.

If you are worried about what level your processor supports just run this:

$ /lib64/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2 --help | grep -A4 'glibc-hwcaps directories'

Look for the word "supported" on each level. Mine shows this on my large Xeon 6254 (Alma) servers:

x86-64-v4 (supported, searched)     x86-64-v3 (supported, searched)     x86-64-v2 (supported, searched)

At home on my AMD Ryzen 7 3700X desktop (Mint) I get:

x86-64-v4     x86-64-v3 (supported, searched)     x86-64-v2 (supported, searched)

So I have v3 support, but not v4. Hopefully that will be good enough for a long time coming...

We switched our servers and workstations to AlmaLinux after IBM pulled the rug out from under us with 8. I've been very happy with both the product itself and with the people behind it.

RHEL is not open-source anymore? How can that even happen?

It is, but basically only in the barest most minimally technically legal sense.

Only paying customers have access, and of course they can choose who gets to be a paying customer. Once you get access though, it still is open source, you can still publish derivative works and all. This is technically still GPL compliant and has at least held up for three years now.

In real short, the goal was to cut off Oracle, but they went pretty scorched-earth about it.

This was discussed extensively over the last few years.

Disclaimer: lengthy mansplaning post follows for the one or two slashdotters interested but who haven't followed this long-running controversy.

Until a few years ago, RH had a public git repository of their rpm spec files, and probably also their patch sets (can't quite remember). Fairly abruptly they shut down that public repository saying that the repo and folks using it to build RHEL clones didn't provide anything of value to the community (really passive aggressive). This caught a lot of people by surprise and it really was a poke in the eye of the community.

But going back even further, Red Hat's support contracts have always caused heartburn for free software supporters, particularly with regards to the GPL. A RH support contract grants access to SRPM files for the entire distro to their customers, along with of course the actual RPMS. But the wording of the support contract says you cannot use those files on non-supported installations. Meaning they don't want you to pay for one server and then use the downloaded files to update your whole cluster, even though the source code license, particularly in the case of the GPL, explicitly grants such permission. Many free software advocates have complained that although RH abides by the GPL technically, they are using contract law to end run around it.

So far as I know, no actual customers of RH have ever been threatened with contract cancellations over this, and to this day Rocky Linux and Oracle both use these RH-downloaded SRPMs to build their distros. I'm not sure to what extend RH actively pursues the accounts used to download by Rocky and Oracle.

RH's defense is that the upstream CentOS Stream system is public and free to use by the community to build on. This is what Alma Linux does and it works very well while maintaining compatibility with RHEL. The only downside is it's harder convert an existing install between RHEL and AlmaLinux. Back in the old CentOS days it was easy. I'd bring up a server on CentOS, test it in beta for a few months/year, then before bringing it into production I'd finally roll it over into a supported RHEL instance.

I recall an interview with a RH manager on a podcast a couple of years ago when the public RPM spec git repo controversy blew up. He maintained that the RH clones offered nothing of any value to the community. He said that the upstream CentOS Stream system was the public community and that if people want to take from CentOS Stream and create something truly unique and valuable they were free to do so. Alma Linux called his bluff and did just that, and it's worked out great for them. RH is still smarting o

Since 2023 I've seen AlmaLinux release patches, well ahead of RHEL itself, when several news-making exploits reared their ugly heads. They're inarguably offering significant added value to the community.

Yes RH engineers do great work and they deserve to get paid. Of course they also benefiting from the development and testing of the upstream projects, most of whom are doing it for no compensation at all. And RH does support a lot of upstream projects. Meanwhile Alma Linux is not competition for RHEL at all, and they are providing their own value to the community. In fact they contribute back to stream (also with testing) which RH benefits from. I think the Alma Linux/CentOS Stream connection is very h

Amutable Foundations: A Technical Overview

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Posted Nov 7, 2026 9:43 UTC (Sat) by alspnost (guest, #2763) [Link] (2 responses)

Quoting from the article: > Package-based OSes generally provide the user with a large number of regularly updated software packages (many thousands) with complex dependencies, which are composed into a working OS on the final machine it is deployed on. Installations in this style tend to vary greatly from system to system. Even with an identical selection of packages for the target system, the installation/update order, the stateful update logic and the file system data structures generally mean that the bytes that hit the disk on each machine are always different. This is called drift. > Systems put together this way are hence not reproducible, not predictable, and hard to compare and analyze which is essential for tracking down security issues. The second paragraph does not logically follow from the first paragraph. It is just a complete non-sequitur, but the word "hence" suggests otherwise. Systems put together this way can be reproducible, predictable, easy to compare and analyze. Side note: with ASLR, in-memory layout is not predictable, but only very few people complain about that and image-based OS don't make ASLR predictable either. There also is little conceptual difference between a flattened view of layered file system images for an image-based OS or a pre-flattened file system image for a package-based OS, just the time of flattening differs. One could even make a case that creating a flattened view of multiple file system layers at runtime is less reproducible than having a pre-flattened file system which is identical on every boot (at least in the read-only case). OTOH, maybe the Amutable people really have a hard time comparing and analyzing systems with package-based OSes. In that case, maybe their own solution is the only one they can easily work with, and hey... it is valid to create a system they can understand. That's the beauty of FOSS. I just wish they wouldn't bash choices different from their own.

By the time you start an upgrade and by the time it's actually done globally, there very well could have been a package update that came in at the end which the beginning ones did not receive. Many times, it's immaterial, yes -- distributions have done a great job at versioning and keeping non-breaking changes out of versioned releases, but it's a variance nonetheless. Not only packages, but even config changes within a fleets config management system can flow changes into it that the first round didn't, as changes get picked up. Often we don't have the convenience of downtime when managing baremetal systems, which something like Kubernetes abstracted away, but something still has to run baremetal. Amutable's core idea itself is not new, image-based and immutable cores have been around for a long time. What I find most interesting about their approach is the comprehensive approach they are taking with it, from offering a control plane and verification across the entire stack. I've seen these ideas employed in production individually, but am interested to see where they take it when it's coming entirely from the ground up.

I expect they could also tamper the image with which it is reset. At this point, the whole phone is a brick, IMO, and so I bought a new one.

Posted Nov 7, 2026 12:40 UTC (Sat) by NekkoDroid (guest, #176638) [Link] (4 responses)

I am unsure if AOS does it like this, but what Lennart has always been working towards is that the UEFI verifies the kernel (UKI) using the public key, which then verifies the /usr/ partition (which is read-only), which then loads the rest. "the rest" here would be the factory reset part, which is the only really modifiable part (also EFI variables get reset).

So say the nation state enrolled some kinda public key into the mutable part of the system (/var/ or possibly /etc/ but it sounds like it is also read-only) so that the system now also loads some of their system extensions or so, a factory reset would basically wipe those keys (and the extensions). /usr/ can't be enrolled, since that is pinned by the verity root hash and changes in the data would cause errors during reading by the kernel and the UKI is "pinned" by you (or I guess in the case of Amutable them) signing new UKIs. This is assuming you can prevent the nation state from enrolling their keys in your UEFI. But even if they did enroll their own keys in the UEFI the PCR values would be different, resulting in the mutable LUKS partitions not being automatically unlocked, so your mutable data is still inaccessible.

So as long as the cryptography implementations are sound and not bypassed by some 0-days it should be sound for the most part. Unless they are able to get below the bootloader it should be fine (but at that point they don't even need your PIN).

Do tell me if I maybe missed some attack vector which could possibly still exploit something.

Signed bootloaders won't save you if they control that.

So if you want to know whether factory reset is effective against a given threat, consider malicious OS updates, malicious configuration, malicious data exploiting the OS as well as physical modifications. Now if you think a backdoor has been implanted in your device, there are some university research labs focused on finding exactly that sort of thing. (The threat actor in question may be anywhere in a spectrum from stalkers to nation states.) Publishing any found backdoors and zero-days will raise the cost for the attacker and make the world more secure for everyone.

This is why the various "Open" and "Free" movements for hardware and software could be important to you and worth supporting if you care about such things. Without absolute transparency at every level, there are too many places for obscurity, mischief and misdirection to merit trust.

The issue is javascript. Clearly code. (3) says they are not writing that to disk, so it can't be cached much; (4) says they are logging it all (where?); and (6) says they expect it to be signed. It's either lynx forever, or they will have to bend the rules.

It doesn't look like this is aimed at desktop users. I have some k8s nodes that my cloud provider installed Ubuntu on, so I get to enjoy broad compatibility with all the latest CVEs for up to a week. In an ideal world, those nodes don't need a browser, SSH, or a shell at all.

But I'm still very happy on my Debian at home. And actually, most Debian packages turn out to not meaningfully depend on install order, most don't have non-trivial scripts, or only depend on simple triggers. I have a project where I turn Debian packages into erofs layers as a performance hack, and the non-reproducibility of packages is a bit overstated. It's limited to a few things like shared caches updated by triggers which are not frozen by design, but apart from that most of the bits on disk can be made reproducible without having to throw the whole packaging system away, if you so desire.

Asahi Linux 7.3 progress report

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

As such, we made the difficult decision to begin the process of transitioning all of our repositories to the GNU General Public License version 2 or later. Going forward all Asahi Linux code will be licenced under strong copyleft licences only. Those who do not learn from history are doomed to repeat it.

Progress Report: Linux 7.3 - Asahi Linux

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Well here we are again. Linux 7.3 is out and it’s been an eventful few months, so let’s get stuck in to another Asahi Linux progress report!

As always, upstreaming work continues to progress. Aside from the usual patches for low-level SoC blocks, two major drivers made it on to the list this cycle: macaudio and avd. Getting macaudio ready for submission has been a challenge. The audio subsystem on Apple Silicon devices is rather special, and required quite a number of core ALSA changes to properly support it. Apple also uses special variants of speaker and headset amplifiers that were already supported upstream, and these drivers also needed some pretty significant modifications. We have previously discussed a lot of this work at length, and we are finally in a position where we can submit the main driver for review. The driver and Devicetree changes represent a hefty chunk of our total patchset, about 3100 lines.

AVD is a whole different beast. The AVD driver has spent barely 3 months in our downstream tree and is already on the list. This is our shortest turnaround time yet for a major SoC component and bodes well for a transition to upstream-first development some time in the future. This is fantastic news, as the AVD driver and related Devicetree changes are around 13000 lines of code.

Also making an appearance on the list this cycle are initial Devicetrees not only for M4 Pro and Max devices but also M5 thanks to Yureka’s tireless efforts. As usual these initial Devicetrees are barebones and only sufficient to boot the SoC to a TTY, however work is progressing on further enablement. Watch this space!

We have also begun shifting to an upstream-first approach where practical. Changes to the TI USB-PD and SPMI drivers required for new devices, as well as initial support for Thunderbolt, were all developed upstream-first. We are so thrilled to see our focus on upstreaming starting to pay off!

Last time, we announced initial support for M3 series machines via the EXPERT flag on the Asahi installer. We are pleased to announce that we received almost no issues from early adopters, so M3 support is now generally available without the EXPERT flag!

We have also made a number of improvements to M3 support, most notably enabling DCP. On top of performance and battery life improvements, this also allows us to enable sleep on M3 series devices and make the area around the notch useable on the MacBooks! Keep an eye out for m1n1 and kernel updates in the coming days.

Last time, we teased support for virtualising SPTM-enabled versions of macOS in m1n1, which enables reverse engineering on M4 series machines. M5 presents a new challenge, as the versions of macOS which support it all require the use of Exclaves.

Exclaves are small applications running in an isolated environment controlled by the Secure Kernel. On M5 series SoCs and above, macOS uses them for sensitive tasks such as drawing the “camera in use” indicator to the screen. XNU communicates with the Secure Kernel and its Exclaves via SPTM, using yet another IPC protocol called Tightbeam. Much as with SPTM, XNU will panic if it cannot find the running Exclaves. This is all starting to look very similar in concept to Arm’s own TrustZone feature.

While some Exclaves can be bypassed by changing some settings on the command line, we predict that this will not always be the case. As such, Sven started looking at how we could fit all of this in to m1n1. First line of investigation, try putting the Secure Kernel and Exclave userspace in another VM next to SPTM and macOS.

Oh, that worked, and we’re now up to spawning three VMs in EL1 under m1n1! Maybe we can start selling m1n1 to companies fleeing VMWare… With that, we can now virtualise and reverse engineer macOS on both M4 and M5 series machines.

While M3 support is now mostly at feature parity with M1 and M2, the very obvious exception to this is the GPU. Apple’s GPU team seems to follow a tick-tock model, and the M3’s GPU was a very significant departure from the models found on M1 and M2 SoCs. We mentioned quite some time ago that the M3 GPU was being reverse engineered by a new group of contributors, however not much has been said since.

At XDC this year, Alyssa Millburn, Anna Maniscalco and TellowKrinkle gave a talk on their progress reverse engineering the M3 GPU. Work has progressed far enough for m1n1 to successfully initialise the GPU and for the team to draw a triangle! Fans of the “Think Different” sections of these progress reports will find the talk edifying; Apple have once again made a number of… interesting choices which will require significant engineering effort to integrate into upstream Mesa. It is well worth a watch if you’re interested in more detail.

A huge thanks goes out to everyone who has made this work possible. More progress is on the way, so stay tuned.

All the way back in January of 2024, we made the mistake of promising Thunderbolt support within the year. It’s a mistake we won’t quickly repeat. We’ve said almost nothing about Thunderbolt in the two years since, which begs the question: where is it?

Thunderbolt was originally developed by Apple and Intel, with Intel handling the design and fabrication of the hardware required. Since Intel made these controllers available to anyone who wanted them at a (mostly) reasonable price, no one else bothered to design their own. It is only very recently — largely coinciding with the transfer of the standard to the USB-IF — that companies like AMD have decided to roll their own controllers. Apple is of course no exception.

As part of their push for insourcing most silicon IP, Apple made the choice to design their own Thunderbolt controller before most others. Surprising no one, the controller is of course a Cortex-M3 running RTKit coupled with a bespoke Thunderbolt/USB4 router. There is one of these for each Thunderbolt-enabled USB-C port on all Apple Silicon Macs.

Since Intel supplies every other extant Thunderbolt controller on the market, the Linux kernel’s Thunderbolt subsystem is effectively designed around them. It makes a number of assumptions about the hardware topology of the running machine, including hardware capabilities and access patterns for certain registers and regions of shared memory. Apple’s controller breaks a lot of these assumptions. Great.

The Thunderbolt controller is also oddly coupled to PMGR. The reset line for each controller is asserted or deasserted by setting and clearing a set of bits in the middle of PMGR’s MMIO range. No other hardware block works quite like this, and it took some time to realise that these seemingly random writes to PMGR were not just noise.

Luckily for us, Sven was up to the task of dealing with all this nonsense. It just took slightly longer than expected! While the patchset to enable Thunderbolt was sent upstream first (another massive win for us!), we have also pulled it into our downstream kernel and are now shipping it to users!

But there is a catch. Currently, only USB3, XDomain and Thunderbolt Networking are supported. You can plug in USB devices or connect two Thunderbolt computers together and make them ping each other, but that’s about it. PCIe and DisplayPort tunnelling — required for most Thunderbolt docks — are not supported. Yet. Watch this space.

An enormous amount of gratitude is owed to Sven, who has stuck with this thankless chore for nearly 5 years in between all of his other incredible work.

Playing retail PC games on Asahi Linux is a bit of a pain point, to say the least. The software stack required to enable this is incredibly complex. We need to translate x86/amd64 binaries to arm64, and do so fast enough for low-latency gaming. In most cases, we also need to deal with the Windows API. On top of that, almost all games bake in an assumed 4 KiB memory page size to enable certain performance optimisation. Over the past few years, most of these problems have been solved:

When muvm was first developed, it was with the assumption that DRM Native Context would enable near-native GPU performance from inside the VM. Unfortunately, this assumption did not hold. The muvm performance penalty is quite significant for a number of reasons, about 20% depending on the workload. Engineering around the limitations inherent to using a VM also introduces issues; muvm cannot use Wayland, has slow I/O performance, and struggles with certain types of input devices.

This is one area where macOS has us completely licked. While the performance gap between FEX and Rosetta has narrowed, XNU allows userspace processes to map themselves at any page granularity, so all Rosetta needs to do is tell XNU to map 4 KiB pages for its child processes. Linux does not have this flexibility. All pages must be mapped at the same granularity as the kernel. While we did tease Windows games working outside of muvm last year, this relied on ugly hacks inside FEX which have since been removed in the interests of maintainability and translation correctness. Bummer. Even so, compatibility was hit-and-miss, with a lot of (mostly older) software still refusing to work. Double bummer.

Android is currently going through a transition from 4 KiB pages to 16 KiB pages for a variety of reasons. Google Play now requires apps to be built for 16 KiB systems, and any application updates which do not test successfully on such systems will not be approved for publication on the Play Store. What about applications which are no longer updated though? What about sideloaded enterprise slop apps?

Google engineers Kalesh Singh and Frederick Mayle gave a very interesting talk at Linux Plumbers Conference this year about exactly this. Their solution is simple: just fix Linux so that userspace can map 4 KiB pages!

Their addition to the Android kernel is called P3S, or Per Process Page Size. On ARM64 systems, pagetable granularity is controlled by bits set in the Translation Control Registers (TCR). P3S works by tracking the granularity of each process’s pagetables, and writing the appropriate bits to the TCR whenever a process with a different pagetable size is switched to. Because this is a single register write, and because this is a no-op for “native” page size processes, the performance penalty is effectively zero!

While this effort is primarily targeted at Android, the patchset mostly rebases cleanly on top of mainline Linux. What happens if we rebase it on top of our Asahi tree (and fix some bugs)? As it turns out:

Okay okay, Battlefront is a very simple Direct3D 9.0 game from 22 years ago. Not exactly an impressive demo, right? Well what about:

Wolfenstein: The New Order is an extremely well-optimised OpenGL title, and being 64-bit uses WINE’s ARM64EC pathways to massively reduce the amount of work FEX has to do. Again, not exactly an impressive demo if we’re judging based on technical difficulty.

Let’s find something else. Something… worse. Something 32-bit so that FEX and WINE have to work harder. Something that will exercise DXVK too. Something that is so comically buggy and poorly optimised that it doesn’t even work properly on real Windows machines, and relies on community patches and command line switches to start at all on modern computers:

That’s more like it! But we can do even worse. We can even run… Bethesda games!

Performance is of course still patchy. Both Battlefront and Wolfenstein run essentially flawlessly, with the latter hitting 144+ FPS on an M2 Max Mac Studio. GTA IV on the other hand is… not fantastic. Performance is still massively improved over using muvm, and eliminating the use of the VM in general should also eliminate a few classes of bugs. Debugging the GPU driver should also be much easier with this approach.

As always there is a catch, and unfortunately it’s a big one. The current solution is hard-coded for 16 KiB hosts and 4 KiB processes on those hosts. This obviously satisfies Google’s commercial requirements, but adding pagesize-specific kludges to the kernel’s core memory management code is not appropriate upstream. We are unsure if Google intends to generalise this approach and upstream it, or if this is going to stay an Android-only patchset. As such, we do not want to ship this in our downstream tree and have folks become reliant on it when there is this level of uncertainty.

It also requires userspace integration. P3S works by checking the binary metadata for its sections’ page alignments. This works for Google’s use case, as P3S is intended as a fallback mechanism for applications aligned to 4 KiB pages. Modern arm64 binaries are aligned to 64 KiB however, as this guarantees compatibility with systems of all native pagesizes. Thus, a shim is required to force P3S on. The shim triggers P3S, and any child processes will inherit its 4 KiB pagesize. It is not worth spending the effort on implementing this until we have some certainty around what Google intends to do with this patchset.

We have reached out to Google with some questions, and we hope to have more to say soon.

Our custom beamformer for the microphone array found in the MacBooks is written in Rust. Rust’s scientific computing ecosystem is not as mature as C++’s, which is a bit of a problem as beamforming requires realtime FFT and very heavy matrix operations to be done on buffers filled with audio samples. Mature scientific computing libraries have hand-optimised code, often using intrinsics tied to the target ISA. We were not doing this however, and relied on the compiler autovectorising code where it could to achieve our performance targets.

This fell apart when we eliminated the FFT-based analytic transform, replacing it with an implementation based on infinite impulse response (IIR) filters. This should be faster, however the compiler was failing to autovectorise the new implementation. Linkmauve kindly stepped in and added SIMD-optimised code for both arm64 and amd64. This, coupled with other performance optimisations courtesy of Frédéric Bour has resulted in Triforce becoming almost twice as fast, all while using less CPU time!

Fedora Linux 45 was released on 3 November, and we shipped Fedora Asahi Remix on the same day. This is the first time we have been able to get a new version of Fedora Asahi Remix out on the same day as upstream!

We take great pride in our work, and have always done all of our development in the open. We communicate with our users, our followers and our financial sponsors as transparently as is humanly possible. Our finances are in the open, our development branches are in the open, and we report our progress regularly and candidly in the open. Our IRC channels and Matrix rooms are safe spaces, open to anyone with even a passing interest in Asahi Linux or hacking on Apple Silicon.

We do all of this because we firmly believe in Free and Open Source Software, not just as a means to an end but as the end in and of itself. The modern digital world is only possible because of the Free Software movement, and it is more important now than it ever has been that we do all we can to protect the Free Software ecosystem and uphold the ideals upon which it is built.

It may surprise some to learn that WINE was originally made available under the MIT licence. The 90s and 2000s were a very different time in the Free Software ecosystem, and permissively licenced projects were viewed by tech companies as free real estate to pilfer and exploit. WINE was not immune to this, and many monetised proprietary forks of WINE were marketed and sold by various bad actors. The WINE development team grew increasingly concerned that these proprietary forks, while well within their rights to exist, would eventually cause so much fragmentation in the ecosystem that WINE itself would become unviable. In 2002, WINE was relicenced under the LGPL to prevent further proprietary forks from being created, and to encourage direct upstream contribution.

When Asahi Linux was first started, it was with the intent that we develop a Free Software ecosystem for the Apple Silicon platform. To facilitate and encourage the participation of our friends at the BSDs and other Free Software operating systems, we endeavoured to licence as much of our code as possible under the permissive MIT licence.

Unfortunately, this is no longer viable. The malicious practices of the 90s and 2000s have reared their ugly heads once again, this time driven in large part by both the insatiable appetite of frontier AI labs for high-quality training data, and militant animosity toward the Free Software community.

Some time ago, we were made aware of multiple attempts to exploit our permissive licensing to hijack our work, with a direct view to replacing the project and aggressively monetising Linux on Apple Silicon. In some extreme cases, in-development feature code was being lifted straight out of our personal forks and published without attribution or lazily rewritten with LLM assistance to obfuscate its origin. The MIT licence explicitly allows this, and has no carveouts to protect against bad faith actors.

As such, we made the difficult decision to begin the process of transitioning all of our repositories to the GNU General Public License version 2 or later. Going forward all Asahi Linux code will be licenced under strong copyleft licences only. Those who do not learn from history are doomed to repeat it.

As always, we would like to sincerely thank our supporters on OpenCollective and GitHub Sponsors. It is only through your generous backing that we are able to dedicate the time and resources to continuing our work reverse engineering the platform and bringing to it a world-class, polished, and battle-tested Linux desktop experience. You mean the world to us.

Meridian — an experimental web browser built on Servo and GPUI

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Meridian is a web-browser for MacOS built on GPUI and Servo. Meridian is already quite useful to browse websites that are light on javascript and layouting features. The browser is highly configurable: fonts, keybinds, colors, tabs and all.

The project is developed on Tangled, all contributions are welcome, please do browse the list of open issues if you are looking to contribute!

Meridian supports the following features:

Under the hood, Meridian is written in Rust and built on GPUI and Servo. Web pages and browser state is managed by Servo. The browser's chrome is drawn by gpui, the UI framework behind the Zed editor. The glue code to interface servo with gpui lives in separate library, gpui-servo, which exposes Servo as an ordinary gpui component, so anybody can now write a servo-based browser in GPUI!
