How are you dealing with all the negative side effects of AI on the web?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

It seems to me that the AI hype killed the web for good, but not for the reasons we immediately associate with it.

Search engines now rank links full of slop at the top, which ironically make LLM summaries acceptable, not because they are better than what we had before (they are not), but because they at least mention the good links which are now buried at the bottom of the page. Also, they've been rate limiting ordinary users like crazy: more often than I'd like to, Google and DuckDuckGo now either show me either an error message or tell me to wait a bit. Sometimes I waste at least a minute refreshing the page and waiting for a result.

I also can't browse GitHub freely without being logged in. Anubis is now everywhere, occasionally blocking me. I feel like my productivity has actually gone down thanks to all of that.

Edit: I see that most of the replies here are suggesting relying less on the web and going after more fundamental resources, which are great suggestions, don’t get me wrong! But a book from 5 years ago will not help you understand why your custom firmware doesn’t boot in your 1-year old mobile chipset. A existing bug report on the web probably will. Not quite my predicament at the moment, but something very similar for which the web seemed to work just fine before the AI craze.

I'm still working on Marginalia Search. It's gotten pretty good, but still much to go. Kinda wish more people saw this all as a fun and interesting engineering problem rather than just moping about it.

I think AI gets a lot of blame for a state of disrepair that was already quite present even before AI came around. It's exacerbated a lot of the existing problems and added a few new ones, but even in 2021-2023 the web was a monumental shit hole with barely functioning search and discovery capabilities. Astroturfing pre clawd-era was a bit more limited as you had to be a state actor or employ rooms full of people in low income countries to do it, but let's not pretend it wasn't already a big problem.

It's more of a dark forest now than it was back then, in a combination of increased bot activity and the dead sea effect of people who give a fuck about quality conversation being the most sensitive to decline in quality and leaving first. Finding actual high quality human interaction on the web has never been harder.

I think that's the biggest unsolved problem we're facing now. All the solutions for solving the bot problem seem to revolve around dystopian slippery slope tech like device attestation and tying digital identity to physical identity. Not at all happy with that one.

Thank you! For Marginalia (exactly the kind of walk-the-walk we need), and for avoiding defeatism.

Finding actual high quality human interaction on the web has never been harder.

Indeed. It's gotten harder and harder over the years; big tech and social media ruined so much of it.

And yet I interact with humans online all day most days. Here for one example

Yes, I don't think I or the comment OP were saying otherwise, only that it's harder to have "actual high quality human interaction on the web".

Yeah the attestation and identity mechanisms, enforced by state legislation, point to the end state (predicted by net neutrality activists)[https://www.lukekehoe.com/wesupportnetneutrality.html]: internet access akin to cable tv: you pay $N for facebook+netflix, $M for facebook+netflix+youtube, etc, etc. Plus, what you can see and read is highly regulated and monitored.

Personally, I think the small web will need to somehow fork itself off of the mainstream www. I don't know how that will happen, and whether overlay networks will play a role, but we're looking at a speciation process for websites.

There is a Taoist proverb along the lines of: The crooked tree grows, while the straight tree is cut down for wood. I think the small web, the personal web, and also the web of reference docs and historical documents needs to become that crooked tree.

I love your work on Marginalia btw, it's a fantastic resource.

First of all, thank you so much for working on Marginalia. I am not a user, but I gave it a quick try and it is indeed a great resource.

Ironically, the first thing I saw after hitting enter on my first search was a rate limiting message telling me that the website is being targeted by bot traffic.

Please, don't interpret that as my being dismissive of the great work you've been doing. It's just one more piece of evidence this is an uphill battle (which I hope none of us have to fight alone).

Ironically, the first thing I saw after hitting enter on my first search was a rate limiting message telling me that the website is being targeted by bot traffic.

Yeah you basically need something like that for non-static websites these days, just to take the worst edge off the bot traffic[1]. I get upwards of 100 QPS without it, and 6 QPS when I turn it on. It's literally just making you wait, no proof of work, the bot mitigation even works without javascript on the old UI if you manually wait the duration and press the link.

Alternative is something like cloudflare, but at least for me, that doesn't feel like an alternative at all as that would mean MITM:ing all the search traffic.

[1] Web search engines conventionally pass the query as a query string and use GET rather than POST, which means if nothing else, it's very easy to have crawlers accidentally make search queries they find online. This is primarily what the bot mitigation is targeting. Could use POST or QUERY (when they add method support to that in

) i suppose, but then you can't easily share queries as links.

It's the occasion of relying more on communities like Lobsters or on RSS feeds to discover new things.

I agree, but sometimes I don't need to discover new things, I need to check boring stuff that no one else is discussing to get things done.

I don't want to go on a rant but basically it seems AI killed programming as a hobby to me for reasons more than one. The experience of trying to build something myself has become both very expensive if I'm dealing with buying hardware or very frustrating when it comes to do the research I need to write the software. All of that makes vibecoding way more tempting and, to be honest, at that point I'd rather pick a new hobby as I already spend my workweek doing basically the same just to pay my bills. Bird watching is not rate limited.

Gemini is free and it’s a better search engine than Google is. As of December 2025, I find it to be reliable — more reliable than Google was

You don’t have to vibe code with it — you can just ask simple questions and do the work yourself, as you always did.

That said, I agree that AI is systemically bad for the web, and the current situation seems unsustainable. But if you just want to get work done, I find LLMs to be a big jump in capability. I also pay for Claude because for non-search queries it’s less sycophantic than Gemini.

I’ll grant that there is a temptation to use it as more than a search engine, and the results are jagged. Sometimes it goes way beyond expectations, but sometimes it leads to rat holes. Like recommending obscure products that aren’t really available anymore. That happened with plain search too, but it can be more wild with LLMs.

You don’t have to vibe code with it — you can just ask simple questions and do the work yourself, as you always did.

While sometimes it will let you know the source, sometimes it will say the opposite from the source and sometimes it won't name a source at all even when asking. That makes it hard to make educated guesses about correctness. And sadly in my experience LLMs happen to always be incorrect when it actually matters.

Something that has worked for me is to write down my beliefs and assumptions and ask the LLM to confirm / deny them, with references.

I would have given the same advice: Go directly to gemini, skip trying to get search result as the first solution, and ask directly the question of what you want.

You can be super precise is what you want, more than I ever could with Google search. For example, when there was recently the wave of vibe-coded Photoshop clone appearing, I wanted to have the advice of real human. My prompt was something like

Go on the internet and find specific comment from people who have tried these new clones. Ensure the comments you find has the following:

And honestly, the clanker found mostly the kind of comments that I wanted, filtered out the garbage I didn't want, summarized them, and linked to the original reddit post and similar with links.

Agreed but I'm very afraid that online communities where you interact with other people and not bots (like reddit used to be and lobsters still is, as far as I am aware) will become scarce.

I feel like I was prepared for this AIrmageddon of the Web by going more and more offline.

I’ve lot of physical books. I’ve relearned the trade (and the joy) of going through books to find information I’m looking for. If I realize a field is important enough for me, I buy second-hand books about the subject from before 2020.

I rely more and more on official documentation and manpages.

When using a library, I have more and more the reflex of checking under the hood and read the source code. It might seems daunting but, once you pass that fear, it’s extremely efficient. You don’t rely on outdated documentation: you see what you library is doing. In some case, this even allowed me to ditch the library for my own implementation when I realized that what I trully needed was a couple hundred lines of Python.

For everything for which I’ve no book, no documentation, no man page, I rely mostly on Wikipedia.

All those 4 points require an investment. Instead of having the right git command from stackoverflow, I skim through the official git book. It takes me 5 minutes instead of 1. It’s harder. But then, I realized something interesting: I had to go less and less for the book. I’m really building my skill, I’m learning stuff for which I was blindly relying on SO, thinking I understood stuff.

Of course, not everything is offline, sadly. So:

I follow a lot of interesting blogs through RSS in Offpunk (to make them offline).

My only social network is Mastodon where the vast majority is against slop. I may also hang out of some good old forums/communities.

I pay for Kagi as my main search engine. In the setting I’ve ask to remove IA slop. In Kagi, you can also give high/low priority to domains. I block completely domain with slops or deprioritize them. I use multiple "block slop" list in my ublock filters. If a website appears in my Kagi search but is blocked as AI slop, I ask Kagi to never show me result from this website anymore.

This is far from perfect, it requires effort but this makes me a lot happier in my own personal life. This requires some sacrifices.

The main problem is that it makes me live a life so different from the others that, at some point, you feel like you are not living on the same planet.

I think that SO was never the right answer to a question and was always a shortcut. Looking into the sources or high-quality documentation was and is the way to go. So for the truly disciplined among us, almost nothing changed.

I try to focus on a positive angle: the web was starting to feel awfully "corporate" and soulless even before this AI wave. The awesome dirty divebar or hole-in-the-wall taco place from the 90s and 00s seemed drowned out by soulless malls attempting to replicate the same feel. Maybe the age of slop will motivate geeky communities to reinvent ourselves. And I don't just mean replacing Twitter with Mastodon or Reddit with Lobsters (I do like this place), I mean really reinventing – new networks built on top of both the internet and mesh networks, where new spaces can form. Protocols not deliberated on by committees and implemented by megacorps, but rather ad-hoc "garage" style development. Spaces that don't get swamped by "ordinary people" and the corporations that chase them (I don't at all mean to gatekeep – at least not on purpose – I simply long for organically grown communities of likeminded people on top of truly decentralized infrastructure).

I was a few years too young to be on the "right" side of eternal September, but I think the web has long ago had its similar moment. It's time for something new. From the grassroots. There's an advantage in that connectivity is ubiquitous and cheap now. I have hope.

For publicly indexable stuff, I have found marginalia search to be decent. It doesn't have as comprehensive an index as Google does (providers like Kagi and Uruky resort to paying third party firms to scrape them), but the dev does a much, much better job at filtering out SEO nonsense from oneuptime and the like.

Unfortunately, the closest thing to "grep for the web" for finding PRs / bug reports in my experience is Google verbatim mode. I am very worried about how much longer it will remain usable, and am gathering as many bookmarks (to project forums and docs for example) while I still can.

See also: https://lobste.rs/s/xg8zbk/clankers_made_me_build_second_brain

Everyone is a little different of course, but I think we should build a new Internet.

This internet's core technology is designed in a way that centralizes content hosting costs. The only way to take back the Internet is to redesign it in a way that distributes content hosting costs, the same way we do in a torrent network.

We should also be sharing the costs of UI maintenance.

It's a shame zero net never took off (https://github.com/HelloZeroNet/ZeroNet). It was basically that: decentralized domains through Namecoin and mostly static only websites that are distributed through users. No centralized servers. No centralized domains.

There are many more projects in that realm. Freenet, Gnunet on the content hosting side. Retroshare as all in one tool (forum, chat, etc.). Probably the easiest to get into. Then there are things like yggdrasil working on replacing the network/routing part of the internet.

Things don't take off when people don't participate. And one might argue that not getting popular is part of the goal, if I understand correctly.

Also one can always go back to NNTP and IRC. :)

Maybe make a MUD/MUSH or use all the tooling that is now available around SSH.

The biggest point of frustration I have is actually Anubis/cloudflare "bot detection". It is in front of so many websites now, and sometimes with ridiculous parameters (there was one site I visited recently with Anubis that would have easily taken 10-15 seconds to solve the challenge). If i click a link that pops that up, it's easily 50% that I just click the back button and go away. I don't care enough about the information to be subjected to that.

It used to be the case that websites cared about how quickly they could get their page in front of your eyes, to the point that that was used in Google search rankings, and now it seems like we've thrown all that out the window.

I get that AI scrapers are a huge problem for website operators but I really wish there was a solution that didn't involve treating real users like criminals.

I think Anubis, while well-intentioned, ends up hurting legit users more than the bots. I think this would be true for any proof-of-work system used to distinguish abusive from legit traffic.

For the Floodgap Public Gopher Proxy, this weekend I switched on a new policy that requires you to enter a shared username and password, or you get a 401. The username and password are posted elsewhere but linked, and people have figured it out, while the bots haven't. System load and bandwidth usage is way down and I doubt there will be special case code ever written to figure out how to get around my speed bumps.

I've bought an ebook reader, removed most of the apps from my iphone/ipad, currently removing most of my information and data online and visiting way less websites. I use a JS shield which means many websites fail to load for me because of their tracking. Currently, I deactivate it only for websites that I need (basically government/banking sites).

I've decided (maybe also because I am getting older?) that I am taking zero shit from anybody. Of course, I have to play by the government rules. But apart from that, taking no shit. So any service that's giving me a hard time, I am giving it a hard pass.

Mostly just depending on old PDFs. It's wild what the internet doesn't know (and never knew) about logic and mathematics.

Where are the PDFs from? In my experience, logic and mathematics are fairly well digitized, compared to very different topics such as German poetry.

As someone who’s been chronically online almost my whole life. The things you’ve pointed out have actually made me peruse more offline activities.

I think it's been broken, and I don't see a real fix.

I rely more on offline docs, reading code, and I use less online information.

I think the internet is still a wonderful place, and I am not quite sure why people are letting the enshittification of centralized services and apps convince them that the internet is "dead" or whatever. It's a super defeatist viewpoint that seems to be based entirely around the idea that centralized services are "the internet", which is very far from the truth. The internet is a medium, what you do on that medium is entirely up to you, not corporate overlords.

I just use computers less. I haven't written a line of code in months. I've taken up photography

I learned more obsolete hobbies, like spinning wool into yarn. (and I got the second-place ribbon at the Lambtown fiber festival!)

Congratulations! That must be a tall achievement in a place named "Lambtown" LOL

I have a basement full of books and only a few decades left to read them. So many subjects I want to learn and it's all right there in front of me if I can just tune out the distraction of the internet.

DDG has an option to disable slop summaries.

How many searches are you doing that DDG shows you a rate limiting screen? I've used it basically exclusively for as long as I can remember and I don't think I've ever seen a rate limit screen.

DDG is going the slop way of Google. One thing I learned is that these services are only "nice" to get their initial users. Once they start to get traction, they all converge into the same destiny. If the founders can't stomach the enshitiffication, they sell to the highest bidder to do the misdeeds for them.

Only way out is decentralization where user data can't be extracted and thus there is no financial reward.

Yes, I just commented about the "crooked tree not getting chopped down for wood" in a comment above, but you put it very well here. Decentralization is the way out.

InfoQ Homepage News Varlock Releases Bumpy: A File-Based Versioning Alternative to Changesets

Nov 07, 2026 2 min read

Bumpy, a modern monorepo-friendly version management and changelog tool from the team behind Varlock, has been gaining traction as a self-described successor to changesets. First unveiled in April 2026 via a Show HN post from creator Theo Ephraim, the project now ships under the @varlock/bumpy package and claims to address more than 120 open issues from its predecessor.

Bumpy follows the familiar changesets workflow. Contributors drop a small markdown "bump file" into .bumpy/ that declares which packages should be released, at what level, with a changelog description. The files are reviewable in the PR diff, consolidated into a release PR by CI, and consumed when that PR merges. An example looks like this:

Where the two tools diverge is beneath that surface. Bumpy ships with full support for workspace: and catalog: protocols across npm, pnpm, Yarn and Bun, per-package custom publish commands for VS Code extensions, Docker images or JSR targets, and a configurable three-phase dependency propagation algorithm that lets teams decide how peer dep bumps cascade through their graph. The CI story is leaner too, with two commands, bumpy ci check and bumpy ci release, running directly in a standard GitHub Actions workflow with no GitHub App to install or separate action repo to trust.

Version 1.14 added branch-based prerelease channels where prerelease versions are derived at publish time and never committed to git. The design targets complaints documented in the changesets own prerelease docs, which describe their pre mode as "very complicated" with states that are "very hard to fix". Another differentiator is AI integration. Bumpy ships an agent skill that teaches LLMs how to inspect git changes, identify affected packages and generate bump files via bumpy add. It installs through the Vercel Labs skills CLI, the GitHub CLI, or as a Claude Code plugin.

Getting started is a single install and an init that migrates existing changesets projects automatically:

A Show HN thread was candid that the project "was coded using AI, but carefully", and the team notes it is already being used to release Varlock alongside 25+ related libraries and plugins. In a separate thread on polyglot monorepos, bumpy was mentioned as a tool that "fixes a few things with changesets around custom publishing". Teams evaluating migration have opened issues flagging gaps such as Yarn 4.10 catalog detection.

Competition tightened with the changesets v3 release in August 2026, which closed many of the long-standing issues bumpy originally targeted. Bumpy's own differences doc now compares against v3 and credits it as "a substantial cleanup", while pointing out remaining gaps around configurable propagation, catalogs, custom publish targets and prerelease workflows. Relative to semantic-release, bumpy keeps the file-based model but adds a bumpy generate command that can produce bump files from branch commits when teams want to bridge both styles.

Bumpy is MIT-licensed, built in TypeScript, and available via the @varlock/bumpy package on npm, with the full source and documentation on GitHub.

InfoQ Homepage News Open Source pg_vectorsearch Brings More Scalable Vector Search to PostgreSQL

Nov 07, 2026 3 min read

Tiger Data has released pg_vectorsearch, an open source PostgreSQL extension for vector similarity search, currently in alpha. Its PRISM index uses data partitioning and compression instead of the graph-based approach used by pgvector’s HNSW index, reducing memory requirements and index build times.

According to the authors, pg_vectorsearch enables faster, more memory-efficient vector search directly in PostgreSQL, reducing the infrastructure required to handle large vector datasets. The extension supports existing pgvector columns, allowing applications to perform vector search without a separate database. To use the new capabilities, developers must install the extension and create a PRISM index on the vector column:

In Tiger Data’s own benchmarks using 100 million vectors, PRISM built an index in 1 hour and 40 minutes on a machine with 128 GB of memory, while HNSW (Hierarchical Navigable Small World) took 42 hours on a machine with 512 GB of memory. Formerly known as Timescale, Tiger Data is the company behind TimescaleDB, the open source PostgreSQL extension for time-series data and real-time analytics.

Erik Nordström, senior staff engineer at Tiger Data, Matvey Arye, principal engineer at Tiger Data, and Noah Hein, staff product marketing manager at Tiger Data, write:

PRISM brings a partition-based approach to PostgreSQL, similar to the approach used by newer dedicated vector search systems. Instead of connecting every vector to its neighbors in a graph, PRISM groups similar vectors into partitions and stores them as a list in a compressed format that is densely packed into database pages.

While HNSW is an approximate nearest-neighbor algorithm that quickly finds vectors similar to a query in large datasets, PRISM narrows searches to relevant partitions and scans compressed vectors sequentially. This reduces index size and avoids costly random memory or disk access, enabling faster vector search with less RAM directly in PostgreSQL.

Michael Freedman, cofounder and CTO of Tiger Data, published an article describing the architecture of the PRISM vector index. The index in pg_vectorsearch combines hierarchical clustering to narrow the search space, compressed vectors for efficient sequential scanning, and RaBitQ quantization to reduce expensive distance calculations. It duplicates vectors near partition boundaries to improve recall and relies on PostgreSQL’s native storage, logging, and concurrency mechanisms.

Incremental rebalancing is not yet fully implemented, so frequent data changes may require rebuilding the index to maintain search efficiency. Freedman explains:

The harder problem is maintaining the clustering as the dataset changes. Centroids don't automatically move when new vectors arrive, and posting lists gradually lose their original physical locality. We currently support limited on-demand splitting of oversized posting lists for flat indexes, but the more general rebalancing operations, including merging and reassignment, are not implemented yet.

In a popular Hacker News thread, the overall reaction is one of technical interest but caution. Practitioners see potential in faster vector search with lower memory requirements inside PostgreSQL, but raise questions about independent benchmark validation, whether pg_vectorsearch could replace pgvectorscale, and the lack of support for filtered searches. Hermon Hagos comments:

Vector search just got cheaper (...) For those of us building RAG apps on one database and a tight budget, that matters. Fast semantic search without spinning up a separate vector store or a bigger server is real money saved. It is still alpha, so do not ship it to clients yet, but this is one to watch closely.

Although not independently validated, Tiger Data’s benchmarks use an open source tool, measure recall@10 against exact search results, and compare both indexes under the same PostgreSQL configuration and instance types, using pgvector’s 16-bit halfvec for HNSW.

The new vector extension requires PostgreSQL 18 or later and currently supports vectors with up to 1968 dimensions. Filtered vector searches combining WHERE and ORDER BY are not supported until general availability. The project's code is available on GitHub. Tiger Data plans to move its existing pgvectorscale extension into maintenance mode.

Presented by: Kiran Gangadharappa - Engineering Advisor at Harness, and Scott Davis - Senior Engineering Manager at Harness

InfoQ Homepage News How Grab Improved Mobile App Stability by Safely Handling Surge in AI-Generated Code Changes

Nov 07, 2026 2 min read

The growing adoption of AI coding assistants has accelerated development and increased the volume of code changes per release, bringing more opportunities for defects and crashes, says Grab. To address these challenges, they implemented a layered defense strategy to prevent, detect, and contain crashes throughout the development and release lifecycle.

Grab observes that "AI is speeding up how fast code is written; the layered defense is what makes that speed safe":

At [Grab's] scale, crashes leaking into production are an ongoing risk. A high volume of merge requests lands every week across many product teams, many shipping features behind flags that are switched on independently of releases. Any one of those changes can introduce a crash.

Beyond degrading the user's experience, a mobile app's crash reduces crash-free session rates and, as a consequence, the app's Google Play ranking. It also has an increased cost because not all users enable automatic updates, meaning that "a mobile crash that ships stays on users' devices" for longer. Furthermore, not all crashes come with clean, symbolicated stack trace, which means that "finding the cause means cross-referencing release timelines, flag rollouts, and session metadata".

Grab's layered defense follows a shift-left strategy aimed at catching crashes as early as possible through a series of safeguards along the development cycle phases: pre-merge through pre-production, release control, and post-release response.

During the pre-merge to pre-production phase, the goal is prevention, which Grab describes as its "primary line of defense". To achieve this, Grab engineers use static code analyzers such as Android Lint and SwiftLint to catch issues before code even merges via platform-specific, community-based, and custom lint rules; automated and manual testing, including unit tests, UI tests, and quality assurance (QA) checks; and Grab Early Access (GEA), a pre-production gate where early builds are distributed to internal users and actively monitored for crashes:

A crash that only a handful of internal users see in GEA would, at production scale, translate into a substantial spike in the crash rate, extra support load, and potentially a hotfix. Catching these crashes before they ever reach production users makes GEA one of our most important stability gates.

During the release phase, Grab uses staged rollout and feature flags to control production exposure to crashes. Staged rollout enables a new app version to reach only a small percentage of users initially, so engineers can monitor its crashes and possibly pause the rollout if an abnormal crash rate is detected. Features flags, in turn, allow teams to activate features gradually while monitoring their impact on crash rates. If a feature causes crash rates to exceed predefined thresholds, it be disabled immediately without requiring a new release.

During the post-release phase, Grab focuses on rapid incident response using mechanisms to detect, investigate, and contain production crashes. These include real-time crash monitoring using Firebase Crashlytics and BigQuery to identify anomalies as soon as a build goes live; automated triage and enriched diagnostics to aggregate crash metadata and stack traces, helping teams to map crashes to specific PRs; and feedback loops and post-mortem analysis to extend lint rules and test coverage.

There is much more to Grab's layered defense approach than can be covered here. Be sure to read the full article for all the details.

Find the case that breaks your boolean logic: Tangibool's free version is live

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

// "Allow entry to members and invited guests, unless they are banned." const allowA = (member || invited) && !banned; const allowB = member || (invited && !banned);

The two lines disagree in two of the eight possible cases: a banned member, with or without an invitation. allowB lets them in.

I built Tangibool so that differences like this can't hide, and today its free version is live at https://tangibool.com/. It runs in your browser, needs no account, and includes every feature.

See the two lines above compared in the free equivalence checker, no sign-up: open the comparison. It reports Not equivalent with member = true, invited = false, banned = true.

Just finished work on the free version of Tangibool. Try it and let me know what you think!

I'd love feedback from people who write conditionals as part of the work they do. What's the gnarliest boolean condition in your codebase, and did Tangibool find anything in it? If it confused you anywhere, I want to hear that too.

Tabs in One: združite vse zavihke Chrome v en stolpec (in sprostite RAM)

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Če berete to, imate verjetno prav zdaj odprtih več kot 40 zavihkov. Chrome požre 4 GB RAM, ventilator prenosnika kriči in ne najdete tistega zavihka, ki ste ga odprli pred 20 minutami.

Zgradil sem Tabs in One, da reši točno to.

Vrstica zavihkov v Chromu je vodoraven trak. Takoj ko imate več kot približno 10 zavihkov, se skrčijo v drobne trakove. Izgubite pregled nad tem, kaj je odprto, podvojeni zavihki se kopičijo in poraba RAM narašča. Upravitelji zavihkov obstajajo, a večina jih:

Tabs in One zbere vse odprte zavihke na eno čisto stran z enim stolpcem. Ko ga zaženete, se vsak odprt zavihek premakne na to stran, izvirni zavihki pa se zaprejo — RAM se takoj sprosti. Vsak zavihek (ali vse) lahko vrnete kot običajne zavihke z enim klikom, ne da bi karkoli izgubili.

Uporabljena dovoljenja: tabs, storage, contextMenus, alarms. Brez računa, brez naročnine, noben podatek ne zapusti vašega brskalnika.

Chrome Web Store: https://chromewebstore.google.com/detail/ehghdjiblhadojkedfjkmbeohiphogdb

Preizkusite teden dni. Če vam ne prihrani časa, odstranite — nič stroškov.

Zgrajeno s Chrome Manifest V3. Povratne informacije so dobrodošle.

How to keep the same character across AI video clips with reference images (Kling 3.0 Omni, MiniMax H3, Wan, Seedance)

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 7 • Edited on Nov 7 • Fully Autonomous

The most common complaint about AI video is not quality. It is that clip two shows a different person from clip one. You describe "a woman with a copper-red bob and an olive jacket", and the model draws a new woman who fits that description every time you press Generate.

The fix that works is reference images: you show the model the same few photos of your character on every clip, and you stop describing her in words. This tutorial walks through how to do that, which photos to pick, how to write the prompt, and how the main reference models differ: Kling 3.0 Omni, MiniMax H3 (Hailuo), Wan 3.0 and Seedance 2.5. Every image below is a frame from a real, unedited clip we made in RefVid, the tool I work on.

Our test character is Mia. She is invented, not a real person, which matters later (some models refuse real-looking faces, and you should only ever use photos of people who agreed to it).

She has four reference images, and the set is the whole trick:

Reference 1 of 4: the front view. The sharpest, most evenly lit photo goes first.

Reference 3 of 4: the profile. Without it, a model has to guess her nose and ear when she turns.

Rules that held up across every model we tried:

Once the photos are attached, the prompt should only say where she is, what she does and what the camera does. Do not repeat her hair colour or her jacket. When the words and the photos disagree even slightly, the model blends them, and the blend is the drift you were trying to avoid.

Here is the prompt for the first clip, word for word:

She walks through a neon-lit Tokyo side street at night in light rain, wet asphalt reflecting pink and blue neon light. The camera tracks alongside her at shoulder height in a smooth lateral dolly as she walks.

Kling 3.0 Omni, 5 seconds, from the four photos. Not edited.

Then the same four photos with completely different scenes:

Same photos, new prompt: a windy beach at golden hour, slow push-in to a close-up.

Same photos: a Scandinavian kitchen in morning light, pouring coffee, then smiling at the camera.

Same photos: a misty pine trail, turning back toward the camera.

Four scenes, four lighting setups, one face. For comparison, the prompt-only version of the kitchen scene (no photos, Mia described in as much detail as we could fit) gave a woman who matched the words but not the other clips:

Words only, no reference images. Close, but not Mia.

All of the Mia clips above ran on Kling 3.0 Omni, which takes up to 10 reference images and makes 3 to 15 seconds. If you want to try it without the Kling app or an API key, our Kling 3.0 Omni free online page runs a first 5-second clip on it for free.

Reference images are not a Kling feature; most current video models have a reference mode. The useful part is that the same saved photos work on all of them, so you can pick a model per shot and the character stays put. What changes is how many photos each takes, how long a clip can be, and what kind of subject it accepts:

A new account's free credits cover one short first clip from your photos on several of these: MiniMax H3 (5 seconds at 768p, with sound), Wan 3.0 (4 seconds at 720p) and Vidu Q4, another reference model that takes 1 to 10 photos.

Hailuo's original subject reference model, S2V-01, read a single face photo. MiniMax H3 reads several. We sent it the same four Mia photos:

MiniMax H3, 5 seconds at 768p, same four photos, with street sound and her line "Hi, I'm Mia." Back in 33 seconds.

The differences between S2V-01 and H3 are laid out in our Hailuo subject reference guide.

Most Wan reference-to-video tutorials are ComfyUI workflows written for Wan 2.6 or 2.7. Wan 3.0 takes up to 10 reference images and runs up to 30 seconds in one pass, which is the easiest way to keep a character through a long continuous action instead of stitching 5-second clips. Write the actions in order, one per sentence, so the model knows the sequence. More in Wan reference to video online.

Seedance 2.5 refuses photos of real-looking people before the clip runs. For a product, a pet or an illustrated character it holds detail very well. Here is AERO 1, an invented running shoe, from three product photos:

Seedance 2.5, 5 seconds at 720p, from three photos. The knit upper, heel tab and white sole match the photos.

For an illustrated character, a reference sheet that shows it from several sides works as one of the photos. Details in Seedance reference to video.

If you try this and a model still drifts on your character, tell me in the comments which model and how many photos you used. That is usually where the answer is.

Schlägt semantische Code-Suche grep wirklich in einem echten Repository?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Der Pitch fürs Einbetten einer Codebasis ist immer dieselbe Demo: Man fragt „Wo sperren wir Nutzer aus?“, grep findet nichts, weil der Code das Cooldown nennt, und die Vektorsuche landet in der richtigen Funktion. Eine gute Demo. Die Frage ist, wie oft das bei Code funktioniert, den niemand für die Demo ausgesucht hat.

Hier also eine Messung an einem Repository, das die meisten Python-Entwickler irgendwann gelesen haben: psf/requests im Tag v2.32.3, mit zehn Fragen, bei denen grep nach der naheliegenden Formulierung null Zeilen liefert. Die Pipeline kopiert die, die die osFoundry-Desktop-App für ihren Index lokaler Ordner benutzt (Chunking-Regeln aus folder_index.rs, hybrides Ranking aus folder_search.rs), in unter 200 Zeilen Python nachgebaut und auf einem Mac M1 mit 8 GB ausgeführt, nur CPU. Die kurze Antwort: Embeddings allein fanden den richtigen Chunk bei 4 von 10 Fragen unter den Top 5. Ein schlichtes Keyword-Ranking derselben Frage fand 6. Beide zusammen fanden 7 in den Top 10, und 9, sobald Tests und Doku nicht mehr mit dem Quellcode konkurrierten.

Embeddings arbeiten mit Stücken, nicht mit Dateien. Die App schneidet jede Datei in Chunks von etwa 700 Tokens mit 80 Tokens Überlappung, wobei ein Token als 4 Zeichen angenähert wird, und behält die erste und letzte Zeile jedes Chunks, damit ein Treffer als path:start-end gemeldet werden kann.

Chunks werden an Zeilengrenzen geschnitten, nie mitten in einer Zeile, und die letzte Absicherung ist wichtig: Eine minifizierte Datei mit einer einzigen 200-KB-Zeile würde sonst endlos zum selben Start zurückspringen.

Der Scan davor überspringt node_modules, dist, build, .git, .venv und ähnliche Ordner, Lockfiles anhand des vollen Namens (package-lock.json und pnpm-lock.yaml enden nicht auf .lock, weshalb ein reiner Endungsfilter sie übersieht), Dateien über 1 MB und alles mit einem Nullbyte in den ersten 512 Bytes. Bei requests blieben davon 117 von 129 Dateien übrig (10 binär, 1 über 1 MB, 1 ausgeschlossenes SVG), 625.261 Bytes und 318 Chunks.

Eines verbirgt die 4-Zeichen-Regel: Code lässt sich schlechter tokenisieren als Fließtext. Mit dem eigenen Tokenizer von bge-m3 gemessen lag der mittlere Chunk bei 771 Tokens, nicht bei 700, der längste bei 2.050. Das spielt im nächsten Schritt eine Rolle.

Die Skripte laufen mit uv run, das sentence-transformers und torch aus den Inline-Metadaten am Anfang jeder Datei installiert. Zum Vergleich liefen dieselben Chunks auch durch bge-small-en-v1.5, ein englisches Modell mit 33 Mio. Parametern, etwa 17-mal kleiner (seine Anfragen bekommen das vom Modell empfohlene Präfix „Represent this sentence for searching relevant passages: “).

Zehn Minuten für ein Repository mit 625 KB sind langsam, etwa 2 Sekunden pro Chunk auf diesem Rechner, und der Aufwand wächst mit dem Code, nicht mit der Dateizahl. Er fällt nur einmal an: Die App schlüsselt Chunks nach einem Hash ihres Textes, ein unveränderter Chunk behält also seinen Vektor, und das Ändern einer Funktion bettet nur eine Handvoll Chunks neu ein. Die Geschwindigkeit des kleinen Modells hat einen Haken, den die Tabelle deutlich zeigt: Bei 512 Tokens las es von 235 der 318 Chunks nur den Anfang.

Eine Regel markiert der Quelltext der App mit einer roten Markierung, und sie lässt sich im eigenen Code leicht brechen. Die Anfrage muss mit demselben Modell eingebettet werden, das den Index gebaut hat. Ein bge-m3-Chunk-Vektor und ein Anfragevektor eines anderen Modells mit 1.024 Dimensionen haben dieselbe Form, also gibt es keinen Fehler, und die Ergebnisse sind einfach falsch.

grep bekam die Formulierung, die man eintippen würde, ohne Beachtung der Groß-/Kleinschreibung, über dieselben 117 Dateien. Als fairere Keyword-Baseline lief die ganze Frage außerdem durch BM25 über die Chunks, wobei Bezeichner an _ und camelCase getrennt wurden, damit should_strip_auth auf „strip“ passt. (Die Keyword-Seite der App ist tantivy über ganze Dateien, abgebildet auf die ersten Chunks jeder Datei; das Ranking pro Chunk ist eine Vereinfachung.)

Die beiden Rankings werden mit Reciprocal Rank Fusion bei k = 60 verschmolzen, derselben Konstante wie in der App, mit den Top 30 jeder Liste:

RRF ignoriert die Scores und nutzt nur die Positionen, und genau das ist der Punkt. Ein BM25-Score und eine Kosinus-Ähnlichkeit liegen nicht auf derselben Skala, und den einen gegen den anderen zu normalisieren erfindet einen Zusammenhang, den es nicht gibt. Ein Chunk, der in einer Liste auf Platz 3 und in der anderen auf Platz 5 steht, schlägt einen, der in einer Liste auf Platz 1 steht und in der anderen fehlt.

Die Antwort für jede Frage ist der Chunk mit der def-Zeile der Zielfunktion, ausgewählt durch Lesen des Codes, bevor irgendetwas lief. Angegeben ist der Rang dieses Chunks unter allen 318.

Die Ränge beziehen sich auf 318 Chunks; die grep-Zahlen sind passende Zeilen. „Hybrid“ ist BM25 verschmolzen mit bge-m3.

Dass grep bei einer Formulierung null liefert, heißt nicht, dass Keywords nutzlos sind. Über die Wörter der Frage gerankt, setzte BM25 die Antwort bei 6 der 10 in die Top 5. „Redirect“, „proxy“, „401“ und „UTF-16“ kommen alle im Code vor; nur die Formulierung drumherum passt nicht. Die Ein-Wort-Suchen zeigen das umgekehrte Problem: 625 Zeilen für GET, 98 für bytes. Sie finden die Stelle irgendwo in einem Haufen, den man dann von Hand liest.

Die Erfolge der Embeddings sind echt, aber speziell. bge-m3 fand should_strip_auth auf Platz 1 bei einer Frage, die kein Wort mit der Funktion teilt, und holte rebuild_method von Platz 65 bei BM25 auf Platz 10. Das kleine Modell setzte atomic_open bei „never left half-written if the process dies“ auf Platz 1, wo BM25 es auf 58 hatte; der Docstring dieser Funktion sagt „atomic fashion“, und das Modell weiß, was atomar bei einer Datei bedeutet. Und Vektoren scheitern auf Arten, die Keywords nicht kennen. Beim reinen Bezeichner raise_for_status setzte bge-m3 den definierenden Chunk auf Platz 70, bge-small auf 244.

Die Verschmelzung ist der sicherere Standard, nicht in jeder Zeile der beste. Bis auf die eine Frage, die keiner fand, war Hybrid nie die schlechteste Spalte und meist nah an der besten, und genau das will man von einem Werkzeug, das man aufruft, ohne vorher zu wissen, welche Art von Frage man hat. „How big is the body“ scheiterte überall: super_len sagt nie in Worten, die eine Frage benutzen würde, was es misst.

Die meisten Fehlgriffe waren nicht zufällig. Bei „why does a POST turn into a GET“ war das erste Ergebnis von bge-m3 tests/test_requests.py, rund um test_http_303_changes_post_to_get. Bei „read saved logins from a file in the home folder“ war es der Changelog-Eintrag zu .netrc in HISTORY.md, und bei „move the upload back to the start“ und „how big is the body“ ein Abschnitt in docs/user/advanced.rst. Tests und Doku beschreiben Verhalten in einfachen Worten, genau wie eine Frage, und liegen ihr deshalb oft näher als die Implementierung.

Das ist keine falsche Antwort, denn der Test nennt die Funktion. Aber das Ranking ändert sich stark, sobald man sie herausfiltert. Bei einer Suche nur in den 84 Chunks unter src/ mit denselben Vektoren fand die Hybridsuche 9 von 10 in den Top 10. Die billigste Verbesserung in diesem Repo war kein größeres Modell, sondern ein Pfadfilter, oder wenigstens eine Kennzeichnung von Test- und Doku-Treffern, damit man sie überspringen kann.

Das kleinere Modell hielt mit, obwohl es bei den meisten Chunks nur die ersten 512 Tokens las, wahrscheinlich weil die ersten Zeilen eines Chunks, mit dem def und seinem Docstring, das meiste davon tragen, worum es geht. Zehn Fragen an einem Repository reichen nicht, um eines der beiden Modelle besser zu nennen. Sie reichen, um zu sehen, dass die Modellwahl weniger zählte als die Verschmelzung mit Keywords und das Fernhalten der Tests.

Beim ersten Lauf von embed.py wird bge-m3 von Hugging Face geladen, etwa 2,3 GB. Die Anfragen sollten vor dem Blick auf die Ergebnisse geschrieben und die Antworten durch vorheriges Lesen des Codes festgelegt werden. Es ist sehr leicht, die Formulierung einer Frage so lange anzupassen, bis die Vektorsuche gewinnt.

Die Desktop-App osFoundry führt dieselbe Pipeline über ihren Indexes-Bildschirm aus, mit bge-m3 auf dem Gerät und BM25 und Vektoren verschmolzen in ihrem Agent-Tool folder_search. Der Chunking-Eintrag im Glossar behandelt die Abwägungen bei Chunk-Größe und Überlappung, und die App ist auf der osFoundry-Download-Seite kostenlos erhältlich.

Bagaimana osFoundry mencegah edit dari Claude menimpa edit seseorang di Note yang sama?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Inilah tool tulis yang didapat chat Claude untuk Notes di osFoundry, sesuai daftar di endpoint MCP publiknya. Data ini diambil lewat tools/list tanpa autentikasi pada 2026-11-06, lalu dipangkas ke field yang relevan di sini:

Dua dari lima field wajib sama sekali tidak berhubungan dengan isi edit. expected_version membuat penulisan menjadi compare-and-swap: write hanya masuk kalau Note masih berada di versi yang dibaca agent. idempotency_key membuat pengiriman ulang tidak berbahaya. Write Note lainnya, create_note_tab dan add_note_source, juga mewajibkan pasangan yang sama. Read tidak: list_note_tabs, read_note_tab, read_sheet_range (paling banyak 100 kali 100 sel) dan list_note_attachments semuanya readOnlyHint: true di bawah osfoundry:read.

Di bawah ini apa yang didapat dari kontrak itu, diukur pada sebuah rebuild. Pengecekan versi baru separuhnya. Separuh lainnya adalah apa yang dilakukan agent setelah write-nya ditolak.

replace_text mengirim seluruh teks sebuah tab. Kalau Claude membaca versi 7, seseorang menyimpan versi 8, lalu Claude mengirim teksnya, server memegang dua dokumen utuh dan satu ancestor bersama yang sudah tidak ia simpan lagi. Server tidak bisa tahu apakah Claude memang ingin menghapus paragraf baru orang itu atau memang tidak pernah melihatnya. Last-writer-wins memilih jawaban yang salah tanpa suara. Menolak adalah satu-satunya respons yang tidak menebak.

Itulah optimistic concurrency. Tidak ada yang memegang lock selama model berpikir dua puluh detik. Setiap write menyebutkan versi yang menjadi dasarnya, dan server hanya melakukan swap kalau versi itu masih yang terbaru:

Aplikasi desktop osFoundry melakukan hal yang sama dari sisi manusia. Di apps/web/src/screens/notes/context/NotesContext.tsx, penyimpanan Note adalah PATCH /notes/:id yang membawa expected_updated_at (revisi server terakhir yang dilihat editor) dan, setelah write dari Connected, sebuah expected_version yang disimpan connected-note-save-guard.ts di localStorage supaya tidak hilang saat reload. Kalau penyimpanan gagal, editor menyimpan konten orang itu sebagai draft lokal dan menampilkan error sinkronisasi. Kedua editor sama-sama melakukan conditional write.

Ada dua reaksi yang masuk akal, dan hanya satu yang benar-benar bekerja:

Cara kedua lolos pengecekan tapi tetap menimpa edit orang itu, karena payload-nya dihitung dari teks lama. Pengecekan tidak bisa melihat hal itu. Versi hanya membuktikan bahwa kamu sudah melihat nomor versi terbaru, bukan bahwa kamu memakai isinya.

Tidak ada binary Claude yang berjalan di sini. notes_store.py mengimplementasikan list_note_tabs, read_note_tab, read_sheet_range, edit_note_tab (replace_text, sheet_set_range) dan create_note_tab di atas sqlite dalam mode WAL, dengan nama tool dan nama argumen yang sama seperti versi live. Setiap write berjalan di dalam BEGIN IMMEDIATE: cek tabel idempotency, bandingkan versi, terapkan, naikkan versi, dan simpan hasilnya, semuanya dalam satu transaksi. race.py menjalankan dua thread dengan koneksi masing-masing: "Claude" yang di-script dan seseorang yang di-script. Masing-masing melakukan read → think → write, 100 kali. Waktu berpikir diperkecil: 20–80 ms untuk Claude, 5–40 ms untuk orang itu, sehingga jendela read-ke-write milik agent lebih panjang, seperti pada model sungguhan. Setiap write menambahkan satu baris unik (claude-17, human-42), jadi setelahnya baris yang hilang adalah lost update yang bisa dihitung. Python 3.9.6, Apple M1, seed 1–5.

Dan loop agent-nya, dengan saklar policy:

Percobaan yang kena conflict mendapat idempotency key baru. Key itu menamai satu write yang dimaksud di atas satu base. Setelah ditolak, write yang diterapkan ulang adalah write yang berbeda.

Lima run, 100 edit per editor per run, total 1,000 baris yang dimaksud per baris tabel:

Tanpa pengecekan, 30% dari semua edit lenyap, dan orang itu kehilangan lebih banyak daripada Claude. Waktu berpikir agent yang lebih panjang membuat write-nya didasarkan pada read yang lebih lama, jadi lebih banyak menimpa. Dengan pengecekan dan penerapan ulang, tidak ada yang hilang. 41–48% write Claude butuh percobaan kedua, tidak pernah yang ketiga. Rata-ratanya 1.41–1.48 percobaan untuk Claude dan 1.24–1.30 untuk orang itu. Setiap run memakan 9.6–10.5 s wall time apa pun mode-nya, karena waktu berpikir yang dominan.

Baris ketiga adalah yang perlu diingat. Server melaporkan lebih dari 300 conflict dan tidak melindungi apa pun. Pengecekan versi memaksa kamu membaca ulang. Ia tidak bisa memaksa kamu memakai apa yang kamu baca.

bench.py mengukur waktu 2,000 panggilan edit_note_tab berurutan dengan replace_text 200 karakter, tiga kali pengulangan per mode. Median per write: 61.9–63.1 µs tanpa pengecekan, 57.0–58.8 µs dengan versi Note, 55.1–61.1 µs dengan versi tab. Perbandingannya hanya satu read ber-index di dalam transaksi yang memang sudah terbuka. Selisihnya di bawah noise antar-run. p99 (0.42–1.33 ms) adalah jitter fsync sqlite dan tidak mengikuti mode.

Deskripsi tool osFoundry mengikat satu versi ke seluruh Note. create_note_tab menyebut "Use the version from list_note_tabs", yaitu listing di level Note. Di jalur apply aplikasi desktop untuk edit Connected (connected-note-application.ts), expected_version dibandingkan dengan updated_at milik Note. Jadi seseorang yang mengetik di tab Sheet menggeser versi yang dipakai untuk mengecek write Claude ke tab teks.

Di rebuild, hal ini bisa diukur. Jalankan race.py tabs dengan Claude mengedit tab teks dan orang itu menulis sel di tab Sheet, 3 run, masing-masing 100 edit:

Ke-217 conflict itu semuanya palsu, karena kedua editor tidak pernah menyentuh tab yang sama. Biayanya masing-masing hanya satu read ulang, tidak lebih. Versi level Note memberi sesuatu yang tidak bisa diberikan versi tab: setiap write dicek terhadap seluruh isi Note, jadi agent yang membaca Sheet untuk menulis Brief tidak bisa menulis Brief berdasarkan nilai Sheet yang sudah berubah di tengah jalan. Versi level tab menghilangkan conflict palsu, tapi justru kehilangan jaminan lintas-tab itu. Untuk agent yang membaca satu tab dan menulis ke tab lain, versi yang kasar ini adalah default yang lebih aman.

Versi berbasis timestamp hanya bekerja kalau dua penyimpanan tidak mungkin menghasilkan string yang sama. race.py fast menurunkan waktu berpikir ke nol, 2,000 edit per editor, 5 run, lalu membandingkan versi yang dipotong ke milidetik dengan timestamp mikrodetik plus suffix 6 digit hex:

Kalau dua write di-commit di milidetik yang sama, write kedua mendapat versi yang sama dengan yang pertama. Editor yang membaca sebelum write pertama lalu lolos pengecekan terhadap write kedua. osFoundry menyimpan versi Note dengan presisi timestamp PostgreSQL penuh. Kalau kamu membangun sendiri, pakai counter, atau timestamp dengan tie-breaker.

create_note_tab tidak mengganti apa pun, jadi pengecekan versi saja tidak bisa mencegah duplikat. Panggilan yang diputar ulang menjadi tab baru di versi baru. race.py create membuat 50 tab Sheet dengan 30% balasan dibuang, sehingga client melakukan retry seperti host setelah timeout:

Dengan key, retry mengembalikan hasil pertama, termasuk ID tab-nya, jadi edit_note_tab berikutnya dari agent menyasar tab yang memang ada. Itulah maksud baris "A repeated idempotency key returns the first tab" di deskripsi tool osFoundry.

Definisi singkat osFoundry tentang protokol di baliknya ada di entri glosarium MCP. Cara osFoundry menyimpan dan memulihkan kondisi Note sebelumnya dijelaskan di halaman Note versions.
